as a fibre bundle defined as usual by

\displaystyle f^{*}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\coloneqq N\mathbin{{}_{f}\times_{\pi}}\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\left\{(x,g)\in N\times\mathcal{G}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ f(x)=\pi(g)\right\}.

(11)

It is an embedded submanifold of
N\times\mathcal{G}
, and the structural fibre is the same Lie group as for
\mathcal{G}
.
That is, the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are the projections onto the first and second factor, respectively, of
N\times\mathcal{G}
. Actually,
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
carries a natural structure as an LGB.

Corollary 3.1:
Pullbacks of LGBs are LGBs,

[
10
, §2.3, simplified situation of the discussion around Prop. 2.3.1, page 63ff.]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
has a unique (up to isomorphisms) LGB structure such that the projection
\pi_{2}:f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
onto the second factor is an LGB morphism over
f
with
\pi_{2}|_{x}:(f^{*}\mathcal{G})_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}
being a Lie group isomorphism for all
x\in N
.

Remarks 3.2
.

The mentioned reference,
[
10
, §2.3, discussion around Prop. 2.3.1, page 63ff.]
, is rather general, formulated for Lie groupoids. If the reader is only interested into LGBs, then see
e.g.

[
18
, §3, Thm. 3.1]
.

Proof.

By construction, the structural fibre of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
is the same Lie group
G
as for
\mathcal{G}
, and for all
x\in N
we have
\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{x}\cong\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}
, thence, the fibres are Lie groups and the fibrewise group multiplication has the form

\displaystyle(x,g)\cdot(x,q)

\displaystyle=(x,gq)

(12)

for all
x\in N
and
g,q\in\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{x}
.
We are left to show the existence of an LGB atlas. For this fix an LGB atlas
\{(U_{i},\phi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{G}
, where
I
is an (index) set,
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
an open covering of
M
, and
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{G}|_{U_{i}}\to U_{i}\times G
are LGB isomorphisms. Then
f^{-1}(U_{i})
gives rise to an open covering of
N
, and we get

\displaystyle f^{*}\phi_{i}:\left.f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right|_{f^{-1}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\to f^{-1}(U_{i})\times G,

\displaystyle(x,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(x,\phi_{i,f(x)}(g)\right),

where
\phi_{i,f(x)}:\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}\to G
are the Lie group isomorphisms as defined in Def.
2.1
. It is immediate by construction that this gives an LGB atlas.

That this is the unique (up to isomorphisms) LGB structure such that
\pi_{2}:f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is an LGB morphism over
f
inducing a Lie group isomorphism on each fibre simply follows by construction; observe for all
x\in N
that
\pi_{2}|_{x}
is clearly bijective. Furthermore, LGB morphisms need to be homomorphisms, which means here

\displaystyle\pi_{2}\bigl((x,g)\cdot(x,q)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\pi_{2}\bigl((x,g)\bigr)\cdot\pi_{2}\bigl((x,q)\bigr)=gq=\pi_{2}\bigl((x,gq)\bigr)

for all
x\in N
and
g,q\in\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{x}
. By using the bijectivity of
\pi_{2}|_{x}
, the group structure leading to this equation is uniquely the one provided in Eq. (
12
).
Assume we have another underlying LGB atlas in sense of Cor.
3.1
with an LGB chart
\psi_{i}
on
\left.f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right|_{f^{-1}(U_{i})}
(or just w.r.t. a subset of
U_{i}
), then

\displaystyle\phi_{i}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\phi_{i}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\left(f^{*}\phi_{i}\right)^{-1}}_{=(f,\mathds{1}_{G})}\circ f^{*}\phi_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}=(f,\mathds{1}_{G})\circ f^{*}\phi_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1},

(f,\mathds{1}_{G})
is clearly a Lie group isomorphism
f^{-1}(U_{i})\times G\cong U_{i}\times G
of trivial LGBs, and thus the condition about
\pi_{2}|_{x}
being a Lie group isomorphism enforces that we can write

\displaystyle\left(f^{*}\phi_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}\right)(x,g)

\displaystyle=\bigl(x,L_{i}(x,g)\bigr)

for all
(x,g)\in f^{-1}(U)\times G
, where
L_{i}(x,\cdot):G\to G
is a Lie group automorphism. So, in total

\displaystyle\phi_{i}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\bigl(f,L\bigr),

and since
f
and the left hand side are smooth,
L
has to be smooth. We can conclude that
\psi_{i}
gives rise to an LGB atlas compatible with the one defined by
f^{*}\phi_{i}
. This finalizes the proof.
∎

Definition 3.3:
Pullback LGB

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\to M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then we call the LGB structure on
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
as given in Cor.
3.1
the
pullback LGB of
\mathcal{G}
(under
f
)
.

With writing
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
we will often refer to this structure without further mention.

Let us now define
\mathcal{G}
-actions.

Definition 3.4:
Lie group bundle actions,

[
10
, §1.6, special case of Def. 1.6.1, page 34]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then a
right-action of
\mathcal{G}
on
N
is a smooth map

\displaystyle f^{*}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g,

satisfying the following properties:

\displaystyle f(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=\pi(g),

(13)

\displaystyle(p\cdot g)\cdot h

\displaystyle=p\cdot(gh),

(14)

\displaystyle p\cdot e_{f(p)}

\displaystyle=p

(15)

for all
p\in N
and
g,h\in\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}
, where
e
is the neutral section of
\mathcal{G}
.

We analogously define left-actions, and we often write (left or right)
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
. Furthermore, in order to increase readability as long as the dependency on
f
is not important, we introduce the notation

\displaystyle N*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\coloneqq f^{*}\mathcal{G},

(16)

such that the action’s notation has the typical shape
N*\mathcal{G}\to N
; one may also write
N*\mathcal{G}=N\times_{M}\mathcal{G}
. For left actions similarly
\mathcal{G}*N\to N
; even though
\mathcal{G}*N
is the same pullback LGB as for right-actions; we also change the order of notation in this case, that is,
(g,p)\in\mathcal{G}*N
reads
g\in\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}
and
p\in N
.

Remark 3.5:
Relation to the structure of the canonical pullback Lie group bundle over
N

Observe that by the definition of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
we can also write

\displaystyle f(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=f(p),

so, the
\mathcal{G}
-action is defined in such a way that
f
is invariant under it. If
f
is the projection of
N
as a bundle over
M
, then this means that an LGB action is fibre-preserving. Moreover, the fibre-wise group structure on
\mathcal{G}
naturally defines a
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{G}
; in this situation
f
would be
\pi
itself, see also Ex.
3.2
later. Furthermore, having
M=\{*\}
recovers the notion of a Lie group action and condition (
13
) is then trivial.

The other conditions are the typical conditions for actions, especially such that we get a
\mathcal{G}
-action on
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
by

\displaystyle(p,g)\cdot q

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(p\cdot q,q^{-1}g\right)

(17)

for all
p\in N
and
g,q\in\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}
.
4
4

4

In alignment to Def.
3.1
, this action is a map
(f\circ\pi_{1})^{*}\mathcal{G}\to f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, where
\pi_{1}
is the projection onto the first factor in
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
.

As usual, this gives rise to an equivalence relation, whose set of equivalence classes denoted by
f^{*}\mathcal{G}\Big/\mathcal{G}
is isomorphic to
N
(as a set) by
[p,g]\mapsto p\cdot g
, where we denote equivalence classes of
(p,g)\in f^{*}\mathcal{G}
by
[p,g]
. All of this is straight-forward to check. Finally, observe the similarity to associated fibre bundles.

Remark 3.6:
Localizing LGB actions

We can actually localize the LGB action, but in general not with respect to any open neighbourhood of
N
since that is in general not possible in a non-trivial way,
i.e.
the action cannot be brought into the form
(N*\mathcal{G})|_{U}\to U
for arbitrary
U
because
p\cdot g
may for example leave an neighbourhood
U
of
p
. However, with respect to
M
this is possible:

Fix any open neighbourhood
U
of
M
. Then
f^{-1}(U)
is an open neighbourhood of
N
, and we can restrict the action to
f^{-1}(U)
, resulting into a map

\displaystyle\left.(N*\mathcal{G})\right|_{f^{-1}(U)}\to f^{-1}(U),

because of
f(p\cdot g)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Eq.\ \eqref{InvarianceOffUnderGAction}}}}{{=}}\pi(g)=f(p)
, that is, if
(p,g)\in\left.(N*\mathcal{G})\right|_{f^{-1}(U)}
, then
p\in f^{-1}(U)
and so
p\cdot g\in f^{-1}(U)
. In fact, by the definition of
N*\mathcal{G}
as
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, this describes a
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
-action on
f^{-1}(U)

\displaystyle f^{-1}(U)*\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\coloneqq\left(f|_{f^{-1}(U)}\right)^{*}(\mathcal{G}|_{U})=\left.(N*\mathcal{G})\right|_{f^{-1}(U)}

\displaystyle\to f^{-1}(U).

If
x\in M
is a regular value of
f
, then
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold of
N
due to the regular value theorem (for this see
e.g.

[
17
, §A.1, Thm. A.1.32, page 611]
). In that case one could apply the same arguments to restrict the action on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a Lie group one actually gets a typical Lie group action on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
. For more details about this see Ex.
3.2
later.

Remark 3.7:
Left- and right-actions

In the following we usually define everything with respect to right-actions; however, one can of course define the following notions for left actions in a similar manner. If we ever speak of a left action, then we assume precisely this. Some subtle changes like a sign change will be pointed out though.

One can probably see that it is straightforward to extend a lot of the typical notions of Lie group actions to LGB actions; hence, we mainly focus on the definitions and properties which we need in this paper.

Definition 3.8:
Left and right translations,

[
17
, §3.2, notation similar to Def. 3.2.3, page 131]

[
10
, §1.4, special situation of Def. 1.4.1 and its discussion, page 22]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Furthermore assume that we have a right action
N*\mathcal{G}\to N
. We define the
right translation
over
x\in M
with
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
as a map
r_{g}
defined by

\displaystyle f^{-1}(\{x\})

\displaystyle\to f^{-1}(\{x\}),

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g,

and we define the
orbit map
through
p\in f^{-1}(\{x\})
as a map
\Phi_{p}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
we define the
right translation
on
N
as a map
r_{\sigma}
by

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot\sigma_{f(p)}.

If one has a section of
f
,
i.e.
a smooth map
\tau:M\to N
,
x\mapsto\tau_{x}
, with
f\circ\tau=\mathds{1}_{M}
, then we can define the
orbit map
through
\tau
as a map
\Phi_{\tau}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau_{\pi(g)}\cdot g.

Remark 3.9:
Left action and translation

Similarly we define left translations for left actions, which we similarly denote by
l_{g}
and
l_{\sigma}
. By Rem.
3.1
we can define
r_{\sigma}
(and
l_{\sigma}
) also for local sections
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G}|_{U})
by restricting
N
onto
f^{-1}(U)
, where
U
is some open subset of
M
; then
r_{\sigma},l_{\sigma}:f^{-1}(U)\to f^{-1}(U)
. In the same manner one achieves a restriction for
\Phi_{\tau}:\mathcal{G}|_{U}\to f^{-1}(U)
, if
\tau:U\to f^{-1}(U)
.

In case of
N
being
\mathcal{G}
itself we will introduce right (and left) translations later via capital letters.

Remark 3.10:
Group action on sections

Assume that
N
is a fibre bundle over
M
with
f
as its projection. Also observe that
\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
is clearly a group and it may be possible to endow it with an infinite-dimensional Lie group structure (but this is not important here now). In the same fashion as before, we can define a
\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
-action on
\Gamma(N)
given by

\displaystyle\left(\tau\cdot\sigma\right)_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau_{x}\cdot\sigma_{x}

for all
\tau\in\Gamma(N)
,
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, and
x\in M
. It is straightforward to show that this is well-defined. We will make use of this action without further mention.

Remarks 3.11
.

Similar to the arguments in
[
17
, §3.2, discussion after Def. 3.2.3, page 131]
,
\Phi_{p}
is given by the composition of smooth maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to N*\mathcal{G}\to N,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,g)\mapsto p\cdot g.

The second arrow/map is smooth due to the fact that we have a smooth action; the first one is smooth because
N*\mathcal{G}
is the pullback LGB
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
and the first arrow is precisely the embedding of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
into
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
as a fibre over
p
; recall
e.g.
Cor.
3.1
.

For the right-translation
r_{\sigma}
we proceed in a similar fashion, namely,
r_{\sigma}
is a composition of smooth maps

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\to N*\mathcal{G}\to N,

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(p,\sigma_{f(p)}\right)\mapsto p\cdot\sigma_{f(p)}.

The first map describes now a section of
N*\mathcal{G}=f^{*}\mathcal{G}
as LGB over
N
, and thence an embedding. Thus, smoothness follows again as previously. Similarly,
\Phi_{\tau}
is the composition of maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to N*\mathcal{G}\to N,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\tau_{\pi(g)},g\right)\mapsto\tau_{\pi(g)}\cdot g,

and the first arrow is clearly a smooth map
\mathcal{G}\to N\times\mathcal{G}
with values in
f^{*}\mathcal{G}=N*\mathcal{G}
which is an embedded submanifold of
N\times\mathcal{G}
. Thence, smoothness follows as usual. In fact,
\Phi_{\tau}|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}=\Phi_{\tau_{x}}
.

However, for
r_{g}
smoothness can only be discussed if
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is a smooth manifold. That is for example the case if
x
is a regular value of
f
; recall the regular value theorem as cited in
[
17
, §A.1, Thm. A.1.32, page 611]
. This would be the case if
e.g.

f
is a submersion. If
x
is a regular value, then
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold of
N
, and
r_{g}
is a similar composition of smooth maps as for
r_{\sigma}
but restricted to
f^{-1}(\{x\})

\displaystyle f^{-1}(\{x\})

\displaystyle\to\left.\left(N*\mathcal{G}\right)\right|_{f^{-1}(\{x\})}\to f^{-1}(\{x\}),

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,g)\mapsto p\cdot g.

Since
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold,
\left.\left(N*\mathcal{G}\right)\right|_{f^{-1}(\{x\})}
is also a fibre bundle, see for example
[
17
, §4.1, Lemma 4.1.16, page 204]
, and trivially an embedded submanifold of
N*\mathcal{G}
. Altogether, the same arguments as for
r_{\sigma}
apply.

Last but not least,
r_{\sigma}
is clearly a diffeomorphism with inverse
r_{\sigma^{-1}}
, where
\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)_{x}\coloneqq\sigma_{x}^{-1}\coloneqq(\sigma_{x})^{-1}
. Similarly for
r_{g}
, if
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold (otherwise
r_{g}
is just a bijection). Analogously for
l_{\sigma}
and
l_{g}
in case of a left action.

Motivated by the previous remark, it might be useful to require that
f
is a submersion, or that
N
is actually some bundle over
M
and
f
its projection. In fact, this will be the case later.

3.2
Examples of LGB actions

If
M
is a point or
f
a constant map, then we recover the typical notion of a Lie group action acting on
N
.
Additionally, we have the following examples. The notation will be as in Def.
3.1
.

Example 3.12:
LGB acting on itself

Each LGB
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
acts on itself from the left and right, having
N\coloneqq\mathcal{G}
and
f\coloneqq\pi
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle(g,h)

\displaystyle\mapsto gh.

That this satisfies all properties for an LGB action is clear by definition of LGBs; however, let us give a note about the smoothness of this action. Recall that an LGB is locally isomorphic to a trivial LGB
U\times G
(
U
an open subset of
M
) with its canonical group multiplication,
(x,g)\cdot(x,q)=(x,gq)
. Hence, using that the multiplication of
G
is smooth and using local LGB trivializations of
\mathcal{G}
and
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
(recall Cor.
3.1
and its proof to show that
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
is locally diffeomorphic to the product manifold
U\times G\times G
), we achieve smoothness of the
\mathcal{G}
-action on itself because it is locally of the form

\displaystyle U\times G\times G

\displaystyle\to U\times G,

\displaystyle(x,g,q)

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,gq).

We will also call the
\mathcal{G}
-action on itself the
multiplication in
\mathcal{G}
.

Remarks 3.13
.

Similarly one can argue that the
inverse map

\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
,
g\mapsto g^{-1}
, is smooth, too.

Example 3.14:
Trivial action,
[
10
, §1.6, special situation of Ex. 1.6.3, page 35]

The projection
\pi_{1}
onto the first factor of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\to}}N
satisfies the properties of a right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
, that is, the action is given by

\displaystyle N*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g\coloneqq p.

That this action satisfies the properties of an action for all
f
is trivial, hence we call it the
trivial action
.

Example 3.15:
Actions of trivial LGBs

Assume that
\mathcal{G}
is trivial, that is
\mathcal{G}\cong M\times G
as LGBs, where
G
is the structural Lie group of
\mathcal{G}
. In that case, for
p\in N
, the product
p\cdot q
is only defined if
q\in\mathcal{G}
is of the form
(f(p),g)
, where
g\in G
; for this recall that
(p,q)
needs to be an element of
N*\mathcal{G}=f^{*}\mathcal{G}
in order to define
(p,q)\mapsto p\cdot q
. We also have

\displaystyle f^{*}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong N\times G,

the trivial
G
-LGB over N. Hence, let us define a map by

\displaystyle N\times G

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g\coloneqq p\cdot(f(p),g),

which is clearly a smooth map since it is a composition of the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
and

\displaystyle N\times G

\displaystyle\to f^{*}\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,f(p),g).

The latter is smooth because
(p,g)\mapsto(p,f(p),g)
is a smooth map
N\times G\to N\times M\times G
and
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
is an embedded submanifold of
N\times M\times G
. Using Def.
3.1
, it is trivial to see that
p\cdot e=p
, and

\displaystyle p\cdot(gh)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\underbrace{(f(p),gh)}_{\mathclap{=(f(p),g)\cdot(f(p),h)}}=\bigl(p\cdot(f(p),g)\bigr)\cdot(f(p),h)=(p\cdot g)\cdot h

for all
g,h\in G
. Hence, we have a
G
-action on
N
, and by construction it is equivalent to the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
. Due to the discussion in Rem.
3.1
we can therefore conclude that every
\mathcal{G}
-action is locally a typical Lie group action. If
f
is a submersion, then the action is also a
G
-action on each fibre of
f
.

Observe, that one can therefore recover the notion of Lie group actions not only via
M=\{*\}
, the point manifold, (or equivalently via constant maps
f
) but also via trivial LGBs. Translations with constant sections of
M\times G
w.r.t. the action
N*\mathcal{G}\to N
are trivially to be seen as translations with an element of
G
w.r.t. the action
N\times G\to N
.

Hence, if one wants something ”truly” new regarding LGB actions, then one has to look at global structures of non-trivial LGBs and their actions. In fact, the following type of example can provide such a new structure, which we will understand later once we have introduced the physical theory.

Example 3.16:
Inner group bundle acting on associated fibre bundles,

[
10
, §1.6, simplified version of Ex. 1.6.4, page 35]

Let
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{P}}}{{\to}}M
be a principal
G
-bundle with structural Lie group
G
over a smooth manifold
M
, and recall Ex.
2.2
. Furthermore, let
F
be another smooth manifold, equipped with a smooth left
G
-action
\Psi:G\times F\to F
. In total we have two associated bundles over
M
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{G}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\coloneqq P\times_{\Psi}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{c_{G}(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{F}}}

the inner group bundle of
P
and an associated
F
-bundle, respectively.

Then we have a left
c_{G}(P)
-action on
\mathcal{F}
given by

\displaystyle c_{G}(P)*\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}c_{G}(P)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\bigl(\left[p,g\right],\left[p,v\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[p,\Psi\left(g,v\right)\right]=\left[p\cdot g,v\right]

for all
p\in P_{x}
(
x\in M
),
g\in G
and
v\in F
.

Proof.

\bullet
We first check again that the action is well-defined, that is, we are going to prove that the action is independent of the choice of fixed point in
P_{x}
.
Thence, let
x\in M
,
p\in P_{x}
and
p^{\prime}\coloneqq p\cdot g^{\prime}
be another element of
P_{x}
, where
g^{\prime}\in G
. Also let
[p_{1},v]\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
and
[p_{2},g]\in c_{G}(P)_{x}
; then we have unique elements
q_{i},q_{i}^{\prime}
of
G
such that (
i\in\{1,2\}
)

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=p\cdot q_{i},

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=p^{\prime}\cdot q_{i}^{\prime},

especially, it follows
q_{i}=g^{\prime}q_{i}^{\prime}
.

On one hand, if we use
p
as fixed element of
P_{x}
to calculate the multiplication, we get

\displaystyle[p_{2},g]\cdot[p_{1},v]

\displaystyle=[p,c_{q_{2}}(g)]\cdot[p,\Psi(q_{1},v)]=\left[p\cdot c_{q_{2}}(g),\Psi(q_{1},v)\right]=\left[p\cdot c_{q_{2}}\left(g\right)\penalty\ q_{1},v\right].

On the other hand, using
p^{\prime}
as a fixed element, we derive, using
q_{i}^{\prime}=\left(g^{\prime}\right)^{-1}q_{i}
,

\displaystyle[p_{2},g]\cdot[p_{1},v]

\displaystyle=\left[p^{\prime}\cdot c_{q_{2}^{\prime}}\left(g\right)\penalty\ q_{1}^{\prime},v\right]=\left[p\cdot g^{\prime}q_{2}^{\prime}g\left(q_{2}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}q_{1}^{\prime},v\right]=\left[p\cdot q_{2}gq_{2}^{-1}q_{1},v\right]=\left[p\cdot c_{q_{2}}\left(g\right)\penalty\ q_{1},v\right],

which finalizes the argument needed to show that the action is well-defined.

\bullet
To show the smoothness of the proposed action we simply use the atlas constructed in the proof for Thm.
2.2
; the idea of the atlas there does not just work for
c_{G}(P)
but it also serves for constructing a fibre bundle atlas for
\mathcal{F}
following the same relevant parts of the cited proof. That is, we will use a principal bundle atlas for
P
, hence for some
U\subset M
open and a local trivialization
\varphi_{U}:P_{U}\to U\times G
of
P
we write

\displaystyle\varphi_{U}(p)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\pi_{P}(p),\beta_{U}(p)\bigr)

for all
p\in P
, where
\beta_{U}:P_{U}\to G
is an equivariant map. Then we have maps
\phi_{c_{G}(P)}:\left.c_{G}(P)\right|_{U}\to U\times G
and
\phi_{\mathcal{F}}:\left.\mathcal{F}\right|_{U}\to U\times F
given by

\displaystyle\phi_{c_{G}(P)}\bigl([p,g]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{P}(p),c_{\beta_{U}(p)}(g)\right),

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{F}}\bigl([p,v]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{P}(p),\Psi_{\beta_{U}(p)}(v)\right),

with inverses

\displaystyle\phi_{c_{G}(P)}^{-1}(x,g)

\displaystyle=\left[\varphi_{U}^{-1}\left(x,e\right),g\right],

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(x,v)

\displaystyle=\left[\varphi_{U}^{-1}\left(x,e\right),v\right].

Following the related calculations in the proof for Thm.
2.2
one shows that the proposed action looks locally like (w.r.t. these maps)

\displaystyle\bigl((x,g),(x,v)\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(x,\Psi_{\beta_{U}\left(\varphi_{U}^{-1}\left(x,e\right)\cdot g\right)}(v)\right)=\bigl(x,\Psi_{g}(v)\bigr),

which is clearly a smooth map.

\bullet
Let us now quickly check that the conditions in Def.
3.1
are satisfied. We have

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{F}}\bigl([p,g]\cdot[p,v]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\left[p,\Psi\left(g,v\right)\right]\right)=\pi_{P}(p)=\pi_{c_{G}(P)}\bigl([p,g]\bigr)

for all
p\in P_{x}
(
x\in M
),
v\in F
and
g\in G
; similarly, having additionally
h\in G
,

\displaystyle[p,h]\cdot\bigl([p,g]\cdot[p,v]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left[p,\Psi_{h}\left(\Psi_{g}(v)\right)\right]=\left[p,\Psi_{hg}(v)\right]=[p,hg]\cdot[p,v]=\bigl([p,h]\penalty\ [p,g]\bigr)\cdot[p,v],

and

\displaystyle[p,e]\cdot[p,v]

\displaystyle=[p\cdot e,v]=[p,v].

Therefore this describes an action.
∎

Remark 3.17:
Relation to automorphisms of principal bundles and gauge transformations

Recall that gauge transformations are related to principal bundle automorphisms
f
of the principal bundle
P
; see
e.g.

[
17
, §5.3, Def. 5.3.1, page 256f.]
and
[
17
, §5.4, Thm. 5.4.4, page 273]
. That is,
f
is a diffeomorphism
P\to P
with

\displaystyle\pi_{P}\circ f

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{M},

\displaystyle f(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=f(p)\cdot g

for all
p\in P
and
g\in G
. The group of such maps will be denoted by
\sAut(P)
. One can identify such automorphisms with certain
G
-valued maps on
P
, following
[
17
, §5.3, Def. 5.3.2 & Prop. 5.3.3, page 266f.]
: We define the following set of smooth maps
P\to G
by

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\sigma:P\to G\text{ smooth}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \sigma(p\cdot g)=c_{g^{-1}}\bigl(\sigma(p)\bigr)\text{ for all }p\in P,g\in G\right\}.

It is straightforward to check that this is a group w.r.t. pointwise multiplication. Furthermore, there is a group isomorphism

\displaystyle\sAut(P)

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma_{f},

where
\sigma_{f}
is defined by

\displaystyle f(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sigma_{f}(p)

for all
p\in P
; one can prove that this is well-defined.

As argued in
[
17
, §5.3, Thm. 5.3.8, page 269]
,
\sAut(P)
acts (on the left) on associated fibre bundles
\mathcal{F}=P\times_{\Psi}F
by

\displaystyle f\cdot[p,v]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[f(p),v\right]=\left[p\cdot\sigma_{f}(p),v\right]

for all
[p,v]\in\mathcal{F}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and
f\in\sAut(P)
. Therefore one could investigate whether there is also an action just with an element
g
of
G
, basically the restriction of
\sigma_{f}
onto the fibre
P_{x}
. However, the action given by
g\cdot[p,v]=\left[p\cdot g,v\right]
for
g\in G
is in general clearly only well-defined w.r.t. a change of the representative of
[p,v]=\left[p\cdot q,\Psi_{q^{-1}}(v)\right]
(
q\in G
), if
G
is abelian. But one can resolve this by looking at it carefully: The rough idea is that
g
basically comes from
\sigma_{f}(p)
in this context, but

\displaystyle\sigma_{f}(p\cdot q)

\displaystyle=c_{q^{-1}}\bigl(\sigma_{f}(p)\bigr).

Roughly, while
p
is multiplied with
g
,
p\cdot q
has to be multiplied with
q^{-1}gq
. It is easy to check that this resolves that issue, and the result is precisely the action described in Ex.
3.2
. In fact, we have the following proposition:

For the following proposition observe that the (local) sections of an LGB have a group structure given by pointwise multiplication.

Proposition 3.18:
Gauge transformations as sections of the inner LGB,

[
10
, §1.4, (the last sentence of) Ex. 1.4.7, page 25]

Let
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{P}}}{{\to}}M
be a principal bundle with structural Lie group
G
over a smooth manifold
M
. Then there is a group isomorphism

\displaystyle\sAut(P)

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{f}

where
q_{f}\in\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr)
is defined by

\displaystyle\left.q_{f}\right|_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl[p,\sigma_{f}(p)\bigr]

for all
x\in M
, where
p
is any element of
P
such that
\pi_{P}(p)=x
, and
\sigma_{f}
is the element of
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
corresponding to
f
as introduced in Rem.
3.2
.

Remarks 3.19
.

As one may guess,
\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr)
is the analogue of
C^{\infty}(M;G)^{G}
such that one could ask for a more direct analogue to
\sAut(P)
. Indeed, as argued in
[
10
, §1.3, Prop. 1.3.9, page 20]
,
c_{G}(P)
is isomorphic to
\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G
, where
P\times_{M}P\coloneqq\pi_{P}^{*}P
, and the
G
-action is the diagonal action on
P\times P
. One can prove that an isomorphism is given by

\displaystyle c_{G}(P)

\displaystyle\to\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G,

\displaystyle[p,g]

\displaystyle\mapsto[p,p\cdot g].

It is also argued in
[
10
, §1.4, Ex. 1.4.7, page 25]
that
\sAut(P)
is isomorphic to
\Gamma\left(\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G\right)
by

\displaystyle\sAut(P)

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\left(\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G\right),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto L_{f},

where
L_{f}\in\Gamma\left(\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G\right)
is given by

\displaystyle\left.L_{f}\right|_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq[p,f(p)]=\left[p,p\cdot\sigma_{f}(p)\right]

for all
x\in M
, where
p
is any element of
P
such that
\pi_{P}(p)=x
. This is clearly well-defined, and, so, while
c_{G}(P)
is the bundle-analogue of
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
one can think of
\left(P\times_{M}P\right)\Big/G
as the bundle-analogue of
\sAut(P)
.

However, this description often arises if one wants to use the formalism of groupoids and algebroids, here especially using the
gauge groupoid
and
Atiyah algebroid
induced by
P
. These would allow an even more elegant version of the gauge transformations, however, we intend to write this paper in such a way that there is no need that the reader has knowledge about those bundle structures. See the cited references for more details in that regard.

Proof of Prop.
3.2
.

\bullet
Let us first quickly check whether
q_{f}\in\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr)
is well-defined for all
f\in\sAut(P)
. For
p\in P_{x}
(
x\in M
) we have

\displaystyle\left.q_{f}\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\left[p,\sigma_{f}(p)\right],

If
p^{\prime}=p\cdot g
(
g\in G
) is another element of
P_{x}
, then, using
p^{\prime}
to define
\left.q_{f}\right|_{x}
,

\displaystyle\left.q_{f}\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\left[p\cdot g,\sigma_{f}(p\cdot g)\right]=\left[p\cdot g,c_{g^{-1}}\bigl(\sigma_{f}(p)\bigr)\right]=\left[p,\sigma_{f}(p)\right],

also using the definition of
c_{G}(P)
, recall Ex.
2.2
. It follows that
q_{f}
is well-defined, and it is clear that
q_{f}
is smooth.

\bullet
We want to show that
f\mapsto q_{f}
is a group isomorphism by using that it is a composition of the group isomorphisms
\sAut(P)\to C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
as in Rem.
3.2
and

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr),

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{\sigma},

(18)

where
q_{\sigma}
is effectively the same definition as
q_{f}
, that is
q_{\sigma}|_{x}=[p,\sigma(p)]
which is well-defined by the very same reasons as before. It is only left to show that
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}\to\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr)
is a group isomorphism. For injectivity let
\sigma^{\prime}
be another element of
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
and assume
[p,\sigma(p)]=\left[p,\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right]
. Then

\displaystyle e_{x}

\displaystyle=[p,e]=[p,\sigma(p)]\cdot\underbrace{\left(\left[p,\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right]\right)^{-1}}_{=\left[p,\left(\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right)^{-1}\right]}=\left[p,\sigma(p)\left(\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right)^{-1}\right],

such that

\displaystyle\sigma(p)\left(\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=e,

so
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and hence injectivity follows. For surjectivity observe that for a section
q\in\Gamma\bigl(c_{G}(P)\bigr)
we can define a map
\sigma:P\to G
uniquely (unique due to the simply transitive action on
P
) by

\displaystyle q_{x}

\displaystyle=[p,\sigma(p)].

This map satisfies

\displaystyle[p,\sigma(p)]

\displaystyle=\left[p\cdot g,c_{g^{-1}}\bigl(\sigma(p)\bigr)\right]=[p\cdot g,\sigma(p\cdot g)]

for all
g\in G
; the last equality implies
\sigma(p\cdot g)=c_{g^{-1}}\bigl(\sigma(p)\bigr)
, which is precisely what we need for
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
. It is only left to show smoothness of
\sigma
. For an open neighbourhood
U\subset M
of
x
fix a trivialization
\varphi_{U}:P|_{U}\to U\times G
, and we denote

\displaystyle\varphi_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{P}\left(p^{\prime}\right),\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\right)

for all
p^{\prime}\in P
, where
\beta_{U}:P|_{U}\to G
is an equivariant map,
i.e.

\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\cdot g\right)=\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)\penalty\ g
for all
g\in G
. As shown in the proof of Thm.
2.2
, we have a trivialization of
c_{G}(P)
given by

\displaystyle c_{G}(P)|_{U}

\displaystyle\to U\times G,

\displaystyle\left[p^{\prime},g\right]

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\pi_{P}\left(p^{\prime}\right),c_{\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)}(g)\right).

Applying that trivialization to
q
we derive that

\displaystyle\left[p^{\prime}\mapsto c_{\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)}\left(\sigma\left(p^{\prime}\right)\right)\right]

is smooth, because
q
is smooth. Since
c_{\beta_{U}\left(p^{\prime}\right)}
is an element of
\mathrm{Aut}(G)
, we conclude that
\sigma
is smooth. Hence,
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
, so, Def. (
18
) is also surjective and thence bijective.

Finally let us show that Def. (
18
) is a group isomorphism. Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be elements of
C^{\infty}(P;G)^{G}
, then Def. (
18
) reads

\displaystyle\sigma\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}

with

\displaystyle\left.q_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\left[p,\sigma(p)\penalty\ \sigma^{\prime}(p)\right]=[p,\sigma(p)]\cdot\left[p,\sigma^{\prime}(p)\right]=\left.q_{\sigma}\right|_{x}\cdot\left.q_{\sigma^{\prime}}\right|_{x},

such that Def. (
18
) satisfies

\displaystyle\sigma\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{\sigma}\cdot q_{\sigma^{\prime}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4
Lie algebra bundles (LABs)

4.1
Definition

Lie algebras are the infinitesimal version of Lie groups, hence, we expect something similar for LGBs, the Lie algebra bundles:

Definition 4.1:
Lie algebra bundle (LAB),
[
10
, §3.3, Definition 3.3.8, page 104]

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra, and
\mathcal{g},M
be smooth manifolds. A vector bundle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is called a
Lie algebra bundle (LAB)
if:

1.

\mathfrak{g}
and each fibre
\mathcal{g}_{x}
,
x\in M
, are Lie algebras;

2.

there exists a bundle atlas
\left\{\left(U_{i},\phi_{i}\right)\right\}_{i\in I}
such that the induced maps

\displaystyle\phi_{ix}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left.\phi_{i}\right|_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}:\mathcal{g}_{x}\to\mathfrak{g}

are Lie algebra isomorphisms, where
I
is an (index) set,
U_{i}
are open sets covering
M
,
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{g}|_{U}\to U\times\mathfrak{g}
subordinate trivializations, and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
the projection onto the second factor. This atlas will be called
Lie algebra bundle atlas
or
LAB atlas
.

We often say that
\mathcal{g}
is an LAB (over
M
)
, whose structural Lie algebra is either clear by context or not explicitly needed; and we may also denote LABs by
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
.

Of course, we have the typical trivial examples:

Example 4.2:
Trivial examples

We recover the notion of a Lie algebra, if
M
consist of just one point. Moreover, the
trivial LAB
is given as the product manifold
\mathcal{g}\coloneqq M\times\mathfrak{g}\to M
. We have obviously a canonical smooth field of Lie brackets on this bundle
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}:\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\times\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
,
i.e.

\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{g}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{g}\right)
which restricts to the Lie algebra bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathfrak{g}}
of
\mathfrak{g}
on each fibre. The bracket is given by

\displaystyle\left[(x,X),(x,Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(x,\left[X,Y\right]_{\mathfrak{g}}\right)

for all
(x,X),(x,Y)\in M\times\mathfrak{g}
. Smoothness is an immediate consequence.

The definition of LAB morphisms is straight-forward:

Definition 4.3:
LAB morphism,

[
10
, §. 4.3, simplified version of Def. 4.3.1, page 158]

Let
\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{g}}}}{{\to}}M
and
\mathcal{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{h}}}}{{\to}}N
be two LGBs over two smooth manifolds
M
and
N
. An
LAB morphism
is a pair of smooth maps
F:\mathcal{h}\to\mathcal{g}
and
f:N\to M
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{g}}\circ F

\displaystyle=f\circ\pi_{\mathcal{h}},

(19)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\text{ linear},

(20)

\displaystyle F\left(\left[g,q\right]_{\mathcal{h}_{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[F(g),F(q)\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{f(p)}}

(21)

for all
g,q\in\mathcal{h}_{p}
(
p\in N
), where
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{h}_{p}}
and
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{f(p)}}
are Lie brackets of
\mathcal{h}_{p}
and
g_{f(p)}
, respectively. We also say that
F
is an LAB morphism over
f
. If
N=M
and
f=\mathrm{id}_{M}
, then we often omit mentioning
f
explicitly and just write that
F
is a (base-preserving) LAB morphism
.

We speak of an
LAB isomorphism (over
f
)
if
F
is a diffeomorphism.

Remark 4.4:
Smooth field of Lie brackets

We have similar remarks as in Rem.
2.5
. Additionally, we have locally a canonical smooth field of Lie brackets which restricts to a Lie bracket on each fibre because every LAB is locally isomorphic to a trivial LAB as in Ex.
4.1
. Define a field of Lie brackets
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}:\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\times\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
,
i.e.

\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{g}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{g}\right)
, by

\displaystyle\left[X,Y\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[X,Y\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}

(22)

for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
(
x\in M
). Using a local trivialization, this bracket is locally of the form as in Rem.
2.5
such that smoothness follows.

In fact, as also argued in
[
2
, §16.2, Example 2, page 114; but speaking in the context of Lie algebroids there, a generalization of LABs]
, every vector bundle equipped with a smooth field of Lie brackets is an LAB.

Endomorphisms of a vector bundle are of course another important example of LABs.

Example 4.5:
Endomorphisms of a vector bundle,
[
10
, §3.3, part of Ex. 3.3.4]

Let
V\to M
be a vector bundle, and denote with
\mathrm{End}(V)\to M
its bundle of fibre-wise endomorphisms (its sections are the base-preserving bundle endomorphisms of
V
). This is clearly an LAB whose field of Lie brackets is given by the commutator.

As we have seen for LGBs, the pullback of LABs is again an LAB.

Corollary 4.6:
Pullbacks of LABs are LABs,
[
18
, §3, Thm. 3.2]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LAB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then the pullback vector bundle
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
has a unique (up to isomorphisms) LAB structure such that the projection
\pi_{2}:f^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
onto the second factor is an LAB morphism over
f
with
\pi_{2}|_{x}:(f^{*}\mathcal{g})_{x}\to\mathcal{g}_{f(x)}
being a Lie algebra isomorphism for all
x\in N
.

Proof.

Either prove this similarly as Cor.
3.1
(by also using a similar statement already known for the pullbacks of vector bundles), or observe that the pullback
f^{*}\left(\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)
of the field of Lie brackets
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
on
\mathcal{g}
as a section is clearly also a smooth field of Lie brackets on
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
with same structural Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
∎

Definition 4.7:
Pullback LAB

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{g}\to M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then we call the LAB structure on
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
as given in Cor.
4.1
the
pullback LAB of
\mathcal{g}
(under
f
)
.

By writing
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
we will often refer to this structure without further mention.

4.2
From LGBs to LABs

Let us now quickly discuss how LGBs and LABs are related; it is very similar to the relation of Lie groups and algebras, now somewhat fibre-wise. We will follow the style of
[
17
, §1.5.2, page 40ff.]
and
[
10
, §3.5, page 119ff.]
; our approach will be using left-invariant vector fields but the mentioned latter reference actually uses right-invariant vector fields.

Let us start with introducing the basic notations.

Definition 4.8:
Left and right translation and conjugation,

[
17
, §1.5, similar notation to Def. 1.5.3, page 40]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. For
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) we define the following maps:

•

Left translation
given by

\displaystyle L_{g}:\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G}_{x},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto gh.

•

Right translation
given by

\displaystyle R_{g}:\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G}_{x},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto hg.

•

Conjugation
given by

\displaystyle c_{g}:\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G}_{x},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto ghg^{-1}.

Remarks 4.9
.

By definition of
\mathcal{G}
, all these maps are smooth. Furthermore, they clearly satisfy the typical properties as known for these maps since
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a Lie group for all
x\in M
; for reference about their basic properties see for example
[
17
, §1.5, Lemma 1.5.5, page 40f.]
.

The left and right translations of Def.
4.2
and
3.1
align, and thus the smoothness concerns as mentioned in the last part of Rem.
3.11
for right translations
r_{g}=R_{g}
(
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
x\in M
) do not arise. Moreover, while the conjugation
c_{g}
is a Lie group automorphism of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, it describes an LGB automorphism of
\mathcal{G}
if extended to sections; following
[
10
, §1.4, Def. 1.4.6 and its discussion afterwards, page 24f.]
. That is for
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
we define the conjugation
c_{\sigma}
as a smooth map by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\mapsto c_{\sigma}(q)\coloneqq\left(L_{\sigma}\circ R_{\sigma^{-1}}\right)(q)=\left(R_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ L_{\sigma}\right)(q)=\sigma_{\pi(q)}\cdot q\cdot\sigma_{\pi(q)}^{-1}.

It is clear that
c_{\sigma}(gq)=c_{\sigma}(g)\cdot c_{\sigma}(q)
for all
g,q\in\mathcal{G}
with
\pi(g)=\pi(q)
, and that a smooth inverse is given by
c_{\sigma^{-1}}
; thence,
c_{\sigma}
is an LGB isomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
on itself, an automorphism, in sense of Def.
2.1
. It is also trivial to check that we have
c_{\sigma\cdot\tau}=c_{\sigma}\circ c_{\tau}
, where
\tau
is another section of
\mathcal{G}
.

Analogously we defined
R_{\sigma}
as
r_{\sigma}
of Def.
3.1
; with the capital letter we put an emphasis on that the
\mathcal{G}
-action acts on
\mathcal{G}
itself. Similarly for left translations.

Since these are diffeomorphism of the fibres, it makes sense to say that a left-invariant vector field of
\mathcal{G}
has to be a vertical vector field, that is, it is in the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\pi
, the total differential/tangent map of the projection of
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
. For this recall that there is the notion of a
vertical bundle
for fibre bundles
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varpi}}{{\to}}M
(as
e.g.
introduced in
[
17
, §5.1.1, for principal bundles, but it is straightforward to extend the definitions; page 258ff.]
), which is defined as a subbundle
\mathrm{VF}\to F
of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}F\to F
given as the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\varpi:\mathrm{T}F\to\mathrm{T}M
. The fibres
\mathrm{V}_{v}F
of
\mathrm{V}F
at
v\in F
are then given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}_{v}F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}_{v}F_{x},

where
x\coloneqq\varpi(v)\in M
and
F_{x}
is the fibre of
F
at
x
.
F_{x}
is an embedded submanifold of
F
, thence, by definition a section
X\in\Gamma(\mathrm{V}F)
restricts to a vector field on the fibres, that is,

\displaystyle X|_{F_{x}}\in\mathfrak{X}(F_{x}).

In our case
F=\mathcal{G}
, hence
F_{x}=\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a Lie group, so, the vertical bundle just consists of the tangent bundles of Lie groups of all fibres. All of these are generated by their Lie algebra at
e_{x}
, the identity element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Hence, it is natural to guess that the LAB for
\mathcal{G}
will be
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{e_{M}}
, where
e_{M}
is the image of
M
under the identity/neutral section of
\mathcal{G}
, thus, an embedding of
M
into
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{e_{M}}
is a fibre bundle by
[
17
, §4.1, Lemma 4.1.16, page 204]
, and clearly a vector bundle. Equivalently, since the identity section
e
is an embedding, we think of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{e_{M}}
as the pullback vector bundle
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
, which is conveniently a vector bundle over
M
.

Hence, let us now show that
\mathcal{G}
will be related to
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
similar to how a Lie group will be related to its Lie algebra.

Definition 4.10:
Left-invariant vector fields on LGBs,

[
10
, §3.5, special situation of Def. 3.5.2, page 120]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
is a
left-invariant vector field
if

1.

X
is vertical, that is,

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\in\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}),

2.

X
is invariant under the left-multiplication on each fibre,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{q}(X_{g})

\displaystyle=X_{qg}

for all
q,g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, where
x\coloneqq\pi(g)=\pi(q)
.

The set of all left-invariant vector fields on
\mathcal{G}
will be denoted by
L(\mathcal{G})
.

Remarks 4.11
.

Observe that the second point in the definition is well-defined because
X
is a vertical vector field; that is, recall that
L_{q}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
such that
\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{q}:\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathrm{T}_{qg}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, hence,
\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{q}:\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}_{qg}\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 4.12:
Abstract notation 1

Since
X
is vertical, recall that we can view the restriction of
X
onto a fibre as a vector field on that fibre,
i.e.

\displaystyle X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{X}\left(\mathcal{G}_{x}\right).

\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a Lie group and left translations are diffeomorphisms on it, hence, the left-invariance can also be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}L_{q}\left(X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{q}^{*}\left(X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\right).

(23)

For this recall that
\mathrm{D}L_{q}\in\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{G}_{x};L_{q}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}_{x}\right)
for the left hand side, and that
L_{q}^{*}
is the pullback of sections on the right hand side, that is,
L_{q}^{*}\left(X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\right)\in\Gamma\left(L_{q}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}_{x}\right)
.
Furthermore,
X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is therefore a left-invariant vector field on
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as Lie group. Which is why one may also define the left-invariance of
X
as a vector field on
\mathcal{G}
by saying that it has to restrict to a left-invariant vector field on each fibre in the usual sense of Lie groups.

One quickly shows that this is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
.

Lemma 4.13:
Closure of Lie bracket for left-invariant vector fields,

[
10
, §3.5, special situation of Lemma 3.5.5, page 122]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. Then
L(\mathcal{G})
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in L(\mathcal{G})
, then we need to show

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi\bigl(\left[X,Y\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

and if this holds, then we also need to derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{q}\left(\left.\left[X,Y\right]\right|_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\left[X,Y\right]\right|_{qg}.

One can either immediately show these directly by using statements like
[
17
, Proposition A.1.49; page 615]
, which essentially describes how the Lie bracket of vector fields react under push-forwards. Or use the knowledge about Lie groups, recall Rem.
4.2
: Each fibre
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is an embedded submanifold of
\mathcal{G}
and both,
X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
and
Y|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
, are vector fields of this submanifold. Thus,
\left.\bigl[X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}},Y|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\bigr]\right|_{p}
has values in
\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{G}_{x}
for all
p\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Especially,

\displaystyle\left.\bigl[X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}},Y|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\bigr]\right|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G}_{x}).

Because of this and since
X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
and
Y|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
are left-invariant vector fields of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
(a Lie group), left-invariance of
\left.\bigl[X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}},Y|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\bigr]\right|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
follows, and thus the statement.
∎

Of course, elements of
L(\mathcal{G})
are determined by their values at
e_{M}
, as already suggested previously. Let us show this now; starting with a small auxiliary result.

Corollary 4.14:
L(\mathcal{G})
a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module,

[
10
, §3.5, comment before Lemma 3.5.5, page 122]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. Then
L(\mathcal{G})
is a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module under the multiplication

\displaystyle fX

\displaystyle\coloneqq\pi^{*}f\penalty\ X

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
X\in L(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

Obviously,
fX\in\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})
since

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi(fX)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}\pi(\pi^{*}f\penalty\ X)=\pi^{*}f\penalty\ \mathrm{D}\pi(X)=0.

Furthermore,
fX|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
(
x\in M
) is left-invariant over
\mathcal{G}_{x}
since
X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is left-invariant and
f|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\equiv f(x)\in\mathbb{R}
. Thence,
fX\in L(\mathcal{G})
.
∎

Corollary 4.15:
L(\mathcal{G})
as sections of
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
,

[
10
, §3.5, comment before Lemma 3.5.5, page 122; parts of Cor. 3.5.4, page 121]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and denote with
e
the identity section of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have an isomorphism of
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules

\displaystyle L(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\left(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\right),

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{*}X.

The inverse of this map is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\right)

\displaystyle\to L(\mathcal{G}),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{\nu},

where
X_{\nu}
is given by

\displaystyle\left.X_{\nu}\right|_{g}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}\bigl(\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\nu_{x})\bigr)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
x\coloneqq\pi(g)
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
is the projection onto the second component in
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 4.16:
Abstract notation 2

Since
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\cong\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{e_{M}}
is a very natural isomorphism, we will often just write

\displaystyle\left.X_{\nu}\right|_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}(\nu_{x}),

omitting
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
and using that natural isomorphism without further mention.

Also recall that we can actually define a left translations by (local) sections of
\mathcal{G}
,
i.e.
for
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
we define the left translation
L_{\sigma}
as a map by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma_{\pi(q)}\cdot q.

This map is a diffeomorphism, and restricts to the fibres
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as embedded submanifolds to the map
L_{\sigma_{x}}
; we discussed this in more generality in Rem.
3.11
. Observe that for vertical vector fields
Y\in\Gamma(\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{q}L_{\sigma}(Y_{q})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(L_{\sigma}\circ\gamma)\equiv\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(L_{\sigma_{\pi(q)}}\circ\gamma\right)=\mathrm{D}_{q}L_{g}(Y_{q})

where
g\coloneqq\sigma_{\pi(q)}
and
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{G}_{\pi(q)}
(
I
an open interval containing 0) is a curve with
\gamma(0)=q
and
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t|_{t=0}\gamma=Y_{q}
. Therefore
L_{\sigma}
restricts onto vertical vector fields and is then just the left translation via an element in the fibre over a fixed base point. In total one can then introduce the brief notation

\displaystyle X_{\nu}\circ\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}L_{\sigma}(\nu)=\mathrm{D}L_{\sigma}|_{e_{M}}(\nu)=\mathrm{D}L_{\sigma}\circ\nu.

However, be careful, in general one cannot simply replace
L_{g}
with
L_{\sigma}
, even if
\sigma_{\pi(g)}=g
. This only works with respect to vertical tangent vectors; once horizontal parts play a role things change,
L_{g}
is a priori not even defined then. Once we turn to the definition of horizontal distributions we will come back to this.

Proof of Cor.
4.2
.

This map is clearly
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear, especially due to

\displaystyle e^{*}(fX)

\displaystyle=e^{*}(\pi^{*}f\penalty\ X)=(f\circ\underbrace{\pi\circ e}_{=\mathds{1}_{M}})\penalty\ e^{*}X=f\penalty\ e^{*}X

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
X\in L(\mathcal{G})
; for this recall Cor.
4.2
.

We essentially only need to show that the suggested inverse
\nu\mapsto X_{\nu}
is well-defined. First of all, that
X_{\nu}
is vertical and left-invariant is clear by construction;
\nu_{x}
(
x\in M
) is an element of the Lie algebra of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and thus
X_{\nu}|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is a left-invariant vector field on
\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
X
is therefore an element of
L(\mathcal{G})
once we know that
X
is smooth. We show smoothness similar as in
[
17
, §1.5, proof of Lemma 1.5.13, page 42]
: Denote the multiplication in
\mathcal{G}
by
\mu:\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
. Then observe that
(0_{g},\nu_{x})
(
0_{g}\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
,
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, the zero vector field
0
on
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
) is an element of

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\left(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{(Y,Z)\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ Y\in\mathrm{T}_{q}\mathcal{G},Z\in\mathrm{T}_{h}\mathcal{G}\text{ with }\mathrm{D}_{q}\pi(Y)=\mathrm{D}_{h}\pi(Z),\text{ where }q,h\in\mathcal{G}\text{ with }\pi(q)=\pi(h)\right\}

because
\nu_{x}\in\mathrm{V}_{e_{x}}\mathcal{G}
.
5
5

5

If it is not clear how to derive the tangent bundle of
\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
, then see later when we will discuss it in a more general manner. However, essentially recall that
\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
.
Therefore we can calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(g,e_{x})}\mu(0_{g},\nu_{x})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(g\cdot\gamma)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(L_{g}\circ\gamma)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}(\nu_{x})=X_{\nu}|_{g},

where
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
I
an open interval containing 0) is a curve with
\gamma(0)=e_{x}
and
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t|_{t=0}\gamma=\nu_{x}
. Since
\mu
is smooth,
\mathrm{D}\mu
is smooth, and thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}),

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\left.\bigl(\mathrm{D}\mu\circ(0,\nu_{\pi})\bigr)\right|_{g}=\mathrm{D}_{(g,e_{x})}\mu\left(0_{g},\nu_{\pi(g)}\right)=X_{\nu}|_{g}

is smooth, also using the smoothness of
\nu
,
\pi
and
g\mapsto 0_{g}
.

Finally, that
\phi:L(\mathcal{G})\to\Gamma\left(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\right)
,
X\mapsto e^{*}X
, is bijective is also clear, similar to typical gauge theory; we know that
X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is a left-invariant vector field on
\mathcal{G}_{x}
by Rem.
4.2
. Hence, for
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
,

\displaystyle\left.\left(X|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\right)\right|_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}(X_{e_{x}})=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}\left(e^{*}X|_{x}\right).

This is precisely the structure of the suggested inverse, that is,

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=X_{e^{*}X}=(\psi\circ\phi)(X),

where
\psi:\Gamma(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})\to L(\mathcal{G}),\nu\mapsto X_{\nu}
. Hence, injectivity follows; surjectivity simply follows similarly by

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\psi)(\nu)|_{x}

\displaystyle=\left.e^{*}X_{\nu}\right|_{x}=\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{e_{x}}}_{=\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{V}_{e_{x}}\mathcal{G}}}(\nu_{x})=\nu_{x}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})
and
x\in M
. This finishes the proof.
∎

This result shows the typical statement about that elements of
L(\mathcal{G})
are uniquely determined by their values at
e_{M}
. It immediately follows, too, that:

Corollary 4.17:
LGBs induce an LAB structure,

[
10
, §3.5, simplified version of the discussion after Cor. 3.5.4, page 121ff.]

Let
G\to\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and denote with
e
the identity section of
\mathcal{G}
. Then
\mathcal{g}\coloneqq e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\to M
admits the structure as an LAB with structural Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the Lie algebra of
G
, and the fibres
\mathcal{g}_{x}
(
x\in N
) are the Lie algebras of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. The field of Lie algebra brackets
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
is given by

\displaystyle\left[\nu,\mu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq e^{*}\bigl([X_{\nu},X_{\mu}]\bigr)

for all
\nu,\mu\in\Gamma(e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})
, where
X_{\nu},X_{\mu}
are elements of
L(\mathcal{G})
as given in Cor.
4.2
. Point-wise

\displaystyle\left[\nu_{x},\mu_{x}\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=\bigl.[X_{\nu},X_{\mu}]\bigr|_{e_{x}}

for all
x\in M
.

Proof.

As already discussed
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
is a vector bundle. The fibres are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{g}_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}_{e_{x}}\mathcal{G}_{x}\cong\mathrm{T}_{e_{G}}G=\mathfrak{g}

for all
x\in M
, where we used that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is isomorphic to
G
as a Lie group whose neutral element we denoted formally by
e_{G}
. All fibres are Lie algebras of the fibre Lie group, isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}
. By construction, the Lie bracket is precisely the Lie bracket isomorphic to the one of
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
is smooth. Therefore we conclude that
\mathcal{g}
is an LAB with structural Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Alternatively see
[
10
, §3.5, Ex. 3.5.12, page 126]
for an explicit construction of an LAB atlas.
∎

Definition 4.18:
The LAB of an LGB,

[
10
, §3.5, special situation of Def. 3.5.1, page 120]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and denote with
e
the identity section of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we define the
LAB
\mathcal{g}
of
\mathcal{G}
as the vector bundle
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.

In the following
\mathcal{g}
will usually denote the LAB of
\mathcal{G}
.

Example 4.19:
Endomorphisms of a vector bundle as LAB of fibre-wise automorphisms

Recall Ex.
2.1
and
4.1
. For a vector bundle
V\to M
one can show that the LAB of
\mathrm{Aut}(V)
is given by
\mathrm{End}(V)
; the proof is precisely as for the automorphisms and endomorphisms of a vector space as in
[
17
, §1.5.4, page 45ff.]
, just canonically extended to a bundle language.

Example 4.20:
Associated LAB

We discussed associated LGBs in Subsection
2.2
. So, let
G,H
be Lie groups,
P\to M
a principal
G
-bundle over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\psi
a
G
-representation on
H
. Then we have the associated LGB
\mathcal{H}\coloneqq P\times_{\psi}H
.

Also recall Remark
2.2
,
\psi_{*}\coloneqq\left[g\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{e_{G}}\psi_{g}\right]
is a
G
-representation on
\mathfrak{h}
, the Lie algebra of
H
, where
e_{G}
is the neutral element of
G
. Hence, we also have the associated bundle
\mathcal{h}\coloneqq P\times_{\psi_{*}}\mathfrak{h}
.

It is now natural to guess that
\mathcal{h}
is the LAB of
\mathcal{H}
, and this is indeed the case, we will give a sketch; the proof’s construction is similar to
[
10
, §3.1, Prop. 3.1.4, page 90]
; also recall the short introduction of associated bundles at the beginning of Subsection
2.2
. For
x\in M
we have the fibre
\mathcal{H}_{x}=P_{x}\times_{\psi}H
which is isomorphic to
H
as a Lie group by
H\ni h\mapsto[p,h]
for a fixed
p\in P_{x}
. Thus the complete flow of an LAB element of
\mathcal{H}
(in the fibre over
x
) is equivalent to
[p,\gamma]
where
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to H
,
t\mapsto\mathrm{e\;\!}^{tX}
, for an
X\in\mathfrak{h}
. Thus, we can already conclude that the fibre of the LAB of
\mathcal{H}
over
x
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{h}=\mathcal{h}_{x}
as Lie algebra. By the definition of
\mathcal{H}
we also have

\displaystyle[p,\gamma]

\displaystyle=\left[p\cdot g,\psi_{g}\circ\gamma\right]

for all
g\in G
. Denoting
[\cdot,\cdot]\eqqcolon\pi
we get infinitesimally

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\left(p,e_{H}\right)}\pi\left(p,X\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\left(p\cdot g,e_{H}\right)}\pi\bigl(p\cdot g,\mathrm{D}_{e}\psi_{g}(X)\bigr),

where
e_{H}
is the neutral element of
H
. One can trivially check that this gives an equivalence relation on
P_{x}\times\mathfrak{h}
, precisely the one we need for
\mathcal{h}
. It finally follows that the LAB of
\mathcal{H}
is given by
\mathcal{h}
.

As a special example, the LAB of the inner group bundle
c_{G}(P)
, Ex.
2.2
, is the
adjoint bundle
\mathrm{Ad}(P)
of
P
,
\mathrm{Ad}(P)\coloneqq P\times_{\mathrm{Ad}}\mathfrak{g}
, where
\mathrm{Ad}
is the adjoint representation of
G
. This is also a special example of the generalized Atiyah sequence (of both, a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids and algebroids) in
[
10
, §3.5, Def. 3.5.19, page 130]
.

4.3
Vertical Maurer-Cartan form of LGBs

As one may expect, the last result gives hints about the vertical bundle structure of
\mathcal{G}
; this can be shown with the Maurer-Cartan form on LGBs, which we will call vertical Maurer-Cartan form. It will be clear later why we choose to add this adjective; however, as a first argument recall Rem.
4.2
, especially the last paragraph.

Corollary 4.21:
Well-definedness of the vertical Maurer-Cartan form

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. Define the following map

\displaystyle(\mu_{\mathcal{G}})_{g}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}\right)(v)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
v\in V_{g}\mathcal{G}
. Then this map is an element of
\Gamma(\mathrm{V}^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
, where
\mathrm{V}^{*}\mathcal{G}
is the dual bundle of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}:\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{V}_{e_{x}}\mathcal{G}\cong\left(\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)_{g}

where
x\coloneqq\pi(g)
and
e_{x}
is the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Smoothness follows similarly to the smoothness of left-invariant vector fields, that is, denote with
\Phi
the multiplication
\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
and recall the arguments and the notation in the proof of Cor.
4.2
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\left(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{(Y,Z)\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ Y\in\mathrm{T}_{q}\mathcal{G},Z\in\mathrm{T}_{h}\mathcal{G}\text{ with }\mathrm{D}_{q}\pi(Y)=\mathrm{D}_{h}\pi(Z),\text{ where }q,h\in\mathcal{G}\text{ with }\pi(q)=\pi(h)\right\}

and thus

\displaystyle\left(0_{g^{-1}},v\right)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G})

where
0
is the zero vector field,
v\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Therefore we can calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\left(g^{-1},g\right)}\Phi\left(0_{g^{-1}},v\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(g^{-1}\cdot\gamma\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(L_{g^{-1}}\circ\gamma\right)=\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}(v)=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}(v),

where
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
I
an open interval containing 0, and
x\coloneqq\pi(g)
) is a curve with
\gamma(0)=g
and
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t|_{t=0}\gamma=v
. Denote with
0^{-1}
the vector field on
\mathcal{G}
given by
g\mapsto 0_{g^{-1}}
and with
\iota_{0^{-1}}
the contraction with
0^{-1}
, that is,

\displaystyle\left.\left(\iota_{0^{-1}}\mathrm{D}\Phi\right)\right|_{g}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\left(g^{-1},g\right)}\Phi\left(0_{g^{-1}},\cdot\right)=\Bigl[\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}\ni v\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{\left(g^{-1},g\right)}\Phi\left(0_{g^{-1}},v\right)\Bigr]=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, using the structure of
\mathrm{T}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G})
. Thus, we get in total that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{0^{-1}}\mathrm{D}\Phi\in\Gamma(V^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}),

using the smoothness of all involved parts, especially that
\Phi
is smooth, hence also
\mathrm{D}\Phi
is smooth as an element of
\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G};\Phi^{*}\mathcal{G})
.
∎

Definition 4.22:
Vertical Maurer-Cartan form of LGBs,

[
17
, generalization of Def. 3.5.2, page 148]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. The map defined in Cor.
4.3
is the
vertical Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
,
i.e.
defined to be an element of
\Gamma\left(\mathrm{V}^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)
given by

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}\right)(v)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
v\in V_{g}\mathcal{G}
,
where
\mathrm{V}^{*}\mathcal{G}
is the dual bundle of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 4.23:
Recovering of the classical definition

Observe that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
(
x\in M
) is the typical Maurer-Cartan form of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, hence,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
restricts to the Maurer-Cartan form of Lie groups on each fibre.

Also recall Subsection
1.3
, we have a 1:1 correspondence of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
to the following commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

which is the same diagram as in
[
10
, §3.5, special situation of Prop. 3.5.3, page 121]
.

We can finally finish the discussion about the vertical bundle of an LGB.

Corollary 4.24:
Vertical tangent space of
\mathcal{G}
,

[
10
, §3.5, a reformulation of Prop. 3.5.3, page 121]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. Then we have an isomorphism of vector bundles

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}.

Remarks 4.25
.

Observe that by Cor.
4.1
we then know that
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
admits a unique LAB structure such that
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
is an isomorphism of LABs. This statement is also not in contradiction with
\mathcal{g}=e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
(
e
the identity section of
\mathcal{G}
), because

\displaystyle e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong e^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong(\pi\circ e)^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong\mathcal{g}.

By this we also know that
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
is trivial if and only if
\mathcal{g}
is trivial; as also argued in
[
10
, §3.5, discussion after Cor. 3.5.4, page 121]
. Compare this result with
\mathrm{T}G\cong G\times\mathfrak{g}
, where
G
is a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We recover this result by restricting to the Lie group fibres
\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
), that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}_{x}\cong\mathcal{G}_{x}\times\mathcal{g}_{x}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}.

Last but not least, sections of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
are therefore generated by sections of
\mathcal{g}
, the left-invariant vector fields.

Proof of Cor.
4.3
.

This can be quickly shown by recalling Rem.
4.3
, that is, we have the following commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
is defined as in Def.
4.3
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
restricts to the Maurer-Cartan form of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) on each fibre of
\mathcal{G}
; especially,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{g}
is a fibre-wise isomorphism (since
\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}
is an isomorphism). Hence, as described in Subsection
1.3
,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}
as an element of
\Gamma(\mathrm{V}^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{G})
,
i.e.
a vector bundle morphism
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\to\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
(linearity of
\mu_{G}
is clear), is a vector bundle isomorphism. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.4
Exponential map of LGBs

By Remark
4.2
it is clear that we have a natural exponential map, just given by the fibre-wise exponential. If one is interested into a more general exponential map, then see
[
10
, §3.6, page 132ff.]
. However, since our situation is much simpler, we quickly finish this discussion just making use of the already existing exponential map in each fibre; a straightforward generalization on results as provided in
[
17
, §1.7, page 55ff.]
.

Definition 4.26:
Exponential map,

[
10
, §3.6, second part of Ex. 3.6.2, page 133f.]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{g}\to M
its LAB. Then we define the
exponential map
\exp:\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{G}
by

\displaystyle\exp(X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e\;\!}^{X}\coloneqq\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}(X)

for all
x\in M
and
X\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
, where
\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is the exponential map of the Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as
e.g.
provided in
[
17
, §1.7, Def. 1.7.6, page 57]
. Its extension to sections, also denoted by
\exp:\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, is canonically given by

\displaystyle\left.\exp(\nu)\right|_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\exp(\nu_{x})

for all
x\in M
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
.

Usually, the LGB is given by context, otherwise we will denote the exponential map by
\exp_{\mathcal{G}}
instead.

The exponential map is well-defined, especially it is smooth because it describes the flow of left-invariant vector fields as it also happens for Lie groups; see for example
[
17
, Prop. 1.7.12, page 58]
for the Lie group statement. Also recall Cor.
4.2
, we denote left-invariant vector fields by
X_{\nu}
where
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
due to the 1:1 correspondence of
L(\mathcal{G})
and
\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
.

Corollary 4.27:
The exponential map as flow of
L(\mathcal{G})
,

[
10
, discussion at the beginning of §3.6, Prop. 3.6.1 and its discussion afterwards; page 132f.]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{g}\to M
its LAB. Then the flow of a left-invariant vector field
X_{\nu}\in L(\mathcal{G})
(
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
) is a complete flow
\phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
given by

\displaystyle\phi(t,g)

\displaystyle=g\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{\pi(g)}}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Especially, the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times M

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle(t,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}

is smooth.

Proof.

As mentioned in Remark
4.2
,
X_{\nu}
is a vertical vector field so that
\left.X_{\nu}\right|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is a left-invariant vector field of the Lie group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
for all
x\in M
.
\left.X_{\nu}\right|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is the left-invariant vector field in
\mathcal{G}_{x}
related to
\nu_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
by Cor.
4.2
, and the flow
\phi_{x}
of
\left.X_{\nu}\right|_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is well-known, as
e.g.
in
[
17
, §1.7, Prop. 1.7.12, page 58]
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G}_{x},

\displaystyle(t,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto\phi_{x}(t,g)=g\cdot\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\left(t\nu_{x}\right),

where
\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is the exponential map of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Since this works for all fibres
\mathcal{G}_{x}
we get by Def.
4.4
that the flow
\phi
of
X_{\nu}
is complete and given by

\displaystyle\phi(t,g)

\displaystyle=g\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{\pi(g)}}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
. We also have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}

\displaystyle=e_{x}\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{\pi\left(e_{x}\right)}}=\phi\left(t,e_{x}\right)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in M
, such that smoothness of
(t,x)\mapsto\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}
follows as composition of smooth maps.
∎

Remark 4.28:
Simplifying notation related to the exponential map

Due to Def.
4.4
we recover a lot of the typical properties of the exponential map as in
[
17
, §1.7, Prop. 1.7.9, page 57]
, if we understand these properties point-wise. Recall Remark
3.1
, then for example

\displaystyle\mathrm{e\;\!}^{(t+s)\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{s\nu}

for all
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. As in
[
10
, §3.6, discussion after Prop. 3.6.1, page 133]
, we say that
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}
is smooth in the sense of the second statement of Cor.
4.4
, and by construction we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left[t\mapsto\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}\right]

for all
x\in M
, so that we also write

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}.

Since all of this is rather natural, we will make use of that without further mention.

This discussion also highlights that one could understand the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra
\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
as the Lie algebra of the infinite-dimensional Lie group
\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
; thus, one could construct the LABs of LGBs by starting in that fashion, and then
\mathcal{g}
is constructed by making use of the 1:1 correspondence of vector bundles and locally free sheaf of modules of constant rank.

4.5
LABs of pullback LGBs

We are going to define LGB representations and corresponding LAB representations. Since group representation are a special form of actions, we will have something similar in the case of LGB representations. Since actions are defined as maps on a pullback of an LGB
\mathcal{G}
, which is also an LGB by Cor.
3.1
, we expect that the corresponding LAB representation is related to the LAB of the pullback of
\mathcal{G}
. It is natural to think of this LAB as the pullback of
\mathcal{g}
, which is also an LAB by Cor.
4.1
:

Corollary 4.29:
LAB of pullback LGB is pullback LAB,

[
18
, §3, Thm. 3.5, page 21]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and let
f:N\to M
be a smooth map defined on another smooth manifold
N
. Then the LAB of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
is isomorphic to the pullback LAB
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
.

Proof.

By Cor.
3.1
we know that
\pi_{2}:f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
, the projection onto the second factor, is an LGB morphism over
f
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and it is fibre-wise a Lie group isomorphism such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\pi_{2}:\mathcal{h}_{p}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{g}_{x}

is a Lie algebra isomorphism for all
p\in N
, where
e_{x}
is the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
for
x\coloneqq f(p)
, and
\mathcal{h}
and
\mathcal{g}
are the LABs of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}
, respectively; for all of that recall that
\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}
and
\mathcal{G}_{x}
are Lie groups. Hence, we have a vector bundle morphism over
f
given by the following commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{\left(\mathds{1}_{N},e_{f}\right)}\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which describes fibre-wise a Lie algebra isomorphism, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\left(\mathds{1}_{N},e_{f}\right)}\pi_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}\pi_{2}\circ\left(\mathds{1}_{N},e_{f}\right)=\left[N\ni p\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{\left(p,e_{f(p)}\right)}\pi_{2}\right].

By our notes in Subsection
1.3
, we therefore achieve an LAB isomorphism
\mathcal{h}\to f^{*}\mathcal{g}
.
∎

Remark 4.30:
LAB of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}

Since this isomorphism is very natural, we always use that identification and will refer to
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
as
the
LAB of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
.

With this we can quickly show the following familiar result.

Corollary 4.31:
Differentials of LGB morphisms are LAB morphisms,

[
10
, §3.5, section about morphisms, page 124f.]

Let
\mathcal{H}\to N
and
\mathcal{G}\to M
be two LGBs over two smooth manifolds
N
and
M
, and we denote with
\mathcal{h}
and
\mathcal{g}
the LABs of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
, respectively. Furthermore, assume that we have an LGB morphism
F:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
over a smooth map
f:N\to M
. Then

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}F\right|_{\mathcal{h}}:\mathcal{h}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{g}

is an LAB morphism over
f
.

Proof.

Again by our notes in Subsection
1.3
, we can view
F
as a base-preserving LGB morphism
F:\mathcal{H}\to f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, since
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
is an LGB whose structure is naturally inherited by
\mathcal{G}
as given in Cor.
3.1
; similarly for its LAB by Cor.
4.1
. Thus, it is fibre-wise a Lie group morphism, and its tangent map restricted to
\mathcal{h}=e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{H}
(
e
the identity section of
\mathcal{H}
) gives therefore fibre-wise a Lie algebra morphism. Thus,
\left.\mathrm{D}F\right|_{\mathcal{h}}:\mathcal{h}\to f^{*}\mathcal{g}
is an LAB morphism (using Cor.
4.5
), and can be seen as an LAB morphism
\mathcal{h}\to\mathcal{g}
over
f
. Alternatively, it is straightforward and trivial to show it directly.
∎

5
LGB actions, part II

Finally, we come to the last part of the
basics
for LGBs and their notions needed.

5.1
LGB and LAB representations

As usual, representations are a special type of group action, with an infinitesimal analogue.

Definition 5.1:
LGB representations,

[
10
, §1.7, special situation of the remark before Def. 1.7.1, page 43]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
,
V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}M
be a vector bundle, and assume that we have a left
\mathcal{G}
-action on
V
,
\Psi:\mathcal{G}*V\coloneqq p^{*}\mathcal{G}\to V
. Then we say that
\Psi
is a
\mathcal{G}
-representation on
V
if it is linear, that is,

\displaystyle\Psi(g,\alpha v)

\displaystyle=\alpha\Psi(g,v),

\displaystyle\Psi(g,v+w)

\displaystyle=\Psi(g,v)+\Psi(g,w)

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and
(g,v),(g,w)\in\mathcal{G}*V
. In alignment with previous notations we may also write
\Psi(g,v)=\Psi_{g}(v)
, or
\Psi(g,v)=g\cdot v
.

Remarks 5.2
.

Observe that
(g,v)\in\mathcal{G}*V
means that
p(v)=\pi(g)
, same for
(g,w)
. Hence, given a base point in
x\in M
, the pairs
(g,v)
in
\mathcal{G}*V|_{p^{-1}(\{x\})}
are given by elements
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
and
v\in V_{x}
. By Def.
3.1
we also have

\displaystyle p\bigl(\Psi(g,v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi(g)=p(v)=x.

Thence, linearity of
\Psi
is well-defined. In fact, observe that
\mathcal{G}*V=p^{*}\mathcal{G}\cong\pi^{*}V
as fibre bundle, therefore
\mathcal{G}*V
carries not only the structure of an LGB but also of a vector bundle. That is, we have the following commuting diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}*V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

the horizontal arrows describe the vector bundle structure (viewing
\mathcal{G}*V
as the vector bundle
\pi^{*}V
), and the vertical ones the LGB structure (viewing
\mathcal{G}*V
as the LGB
p^{*}\mathcal{G}
), where
\mathrm{pr}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) are the projections onto the
i
-th component.

By fixing a base point
x\in M
we clearly have the typical notion of a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-representation on
V_{x}
