. Equivalently, we could therefore obviously define LGB representations as a base-preserving LGB morphism
\Psi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(V)
as also in
[
10
, §1.7, Def. 1.7.1, page 43]
.

Corollary 5.3:
LGB representations as LGB morphisms,

[
10
, §1.7, Prop. 1.7.2, page 43]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
,
V\to M
be a vector bundle. Then every
\mathcal{G}
-representation
\Psi:\mathcal{G}*V\to V
is equivalent to a base-preserving LGB morphism
\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(V)
, related by

\displaystyle\Psi(g,v)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Psi}(g)(v)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and
v\in V_{x}
.

Remarks 5.4
.

We will usually denote both interpretations with the same notation.

Proof of Cor.
5.1
.

This is an immediate consequence of Def.
5.1
and
3.1
.
\Psi
is smooth if and only if
\widetilde{\Psi}
is smooth; this is due to the fact that the LGB atlas of
\mathrm{Aut}(V)
is inherited by an atlas of
V
as constructed in Ex.
2.1
, and due to that
\mathcal{G}*V
is an embedded submanifold of
\mathcal{G}\times V
.
∎

Example 5.5:
Recovering Lie group representations on vector spaces

Similarly as to Ex.
3.2
, if
\mathcal{G}=M\times G
is a trivial LGB over
M
,
G
its structural Lie group, and
V=M\times W
a trivial vector bundle with structural vector space
W
, then
\Psi
is equivalent to a
G
-representation on
W
. Backwards, every Lie group representation gives a representation of trivial LGBs on trivial vector bundles.

Also recall Ex.
2.1
: We can similarly construct another LGB needed for the adjoint representation.

Example 5.6:
Another LGB example: Automorphisms of LABs,

[
10
, §1.7, special situation of Ex. 1.7.12, page 46]

Let
\mathcal{g}
be an LAB over the smooth manifold
M
. We denote with
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})\to M
the bundle of fibre-wise Lie algebra automorphisms of
\mathcal{g}
, where the sections of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})
are the base-preserving LAB automorphisms of
\mathcal{g}
. As in Ex.
2.1
one can show
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})
is an LGB by using LAB trivializations of
\mathcal{g}
instead of vector bundle trivializations.

The notation of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})
may be confusing with the notation as for vector bundles. We usually refer to this LAB automorphism when speaking of LABs and state it explicitly if we just mean the vector bundle version.

For the next major example of an LGB representation recall Def.
4.2
.

Example 5.7:
Adjoint LGB representation,

[
10
, §3.5, special situation of Prop. 3.5.20, page 131]

For
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) we have the conjugation
c_{g}
which is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-automorphism. In the usual way we define the
adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}
as a base-preserving LGB morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{g}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}c_{g}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
).

That this is a
\mathcal{G}
-representation on
\mathcal{g}
follows quickly: By construction
\mathrm{Ad}_{g}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g}_{x})=\left.\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})\right|_{x}
so that the adjoint representation is clearly well-defined. As in the Lie group case, due to the fact that
c_{gq}=c_{g}\circ c_{q}
for all
g,q\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
and
c_{g}(e_{x})=e_{x}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{gq}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\left(c_{g}\circ c_{q}\right)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}c_{g}\circ\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}c_{q}=\mathrm{Ad}_{g}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{q}.

Smoothness could either be shown in a bit more generalized way than the proof in
[
17
, §2.1, Thm. 2.1.45, page 101f.]
, but that would be a bit tedious; instead let us use the already-known smoothness of the adjoint representation in each fibre. Fix an open subset
U
of
M
such that
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{g}
are trivial, that is,
\mathcal{G}|_{U}\cong U\times G
and
\mathcal{g}|_{U}\cong U\times\mathfrak{g}
as LGB and LAB, respectively, where
G
is the structural Lie group with its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Then also clearly
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})|_{U}\cong U\times\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
as LGBs. By construction we then have w.r.t. these trivializations

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{(x,g)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(x,\mathrm{D}_{e_{G}}c^{G}_{g}\Bigr)=\Bigl(x,\mathrm{Ad}^{G}_{g}\Bigr)

for all
(x,g)\in U\times G
, where
\mathrm{Ad}^{G}_{g}:G\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
is the adjoint representation of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}
. Smoothness now follows trivially by the canonical manifold structure of product manifolds and the smoothness of
\mathrm{Ad}^{G}
.

Infinitesimally, we have LAB actions and representations; recall Cor.
4.1
.

Definition 5.8:
LAB actions,

[
10
, §4.1, reformulated version for LABs of Def. 4.1.1, page 149]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LAB, and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then a
\mathcal{g}
-action on
N
is a base-preserving vector bundle morphism

\displaystyle f^{*}\mathcal{g}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}N,

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\rho(\nu),

satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}f\circ\rho

\displaystyle=0

(24)

and such that the induced map

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{g})

\displaystyle\to\Gamma(f^{*}\mathcal{g})\to\mathfrak{X}(N),

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mapsto f^{*}\mu\mapsto\rho\left(f^{*}\mu\right)

is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, that is,

\displaystyle\rho\biggl(f^{*}\left(\left[\mu,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\bigl[\rho\left(f^{*}\mu\right),\rho\left(f^{*}\nu\right)\bigr]

(25)

for all
\mu,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
.

Remarks 5.9
.

For the readers familiar with Lie algebroids, especially action Lie algebroid structures on trivial LABs, Eq. (
25
) should look familiar; in fact, as shown in
[
10
, §4.1, Prop. 4.1.2, page 149f.]
, given an LAB action one can construct a Lie algebroid structure on
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
. This leads to an action Lie algebroid structure on a possibly non-trivial bundle.

As for Lie group and algebra actions, an LGB action induces an LAB action.

Lemma 5.10:
LGB actions induce LAB actions,

[
10
, §4.1, special situation of Thm. 4.1.6, page 152]

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LGB over
M
and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then any right
\mathcal{G}
-action
\Phi:N*\mathcal{G}=f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to N
on
N
induces a
\mathcal{g}
-action
\rho
on
N
by

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\mathrm{D}\Phi\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}},

i.e.

\displaystyle\rho(\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\left(p,e_{f(p)}\right)}\Phi(\eta)

for all
\eta\in(f^{*}\mathcal{g})_{p}
(
p\in N
).

Remarks 5.11
.

Similarly to the discussion as in
[
17
, §3.4, page 141ff.]
, one can show the same for left actions, but one has to use the multiplication with the inverse, that is,

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\mathrm{D}\Phi^{\prime}\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}},

where

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{G}*N

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle(g,p)

\displaystyle\mapsto g^{-1}\cdot p.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

We generalize the proof as provided in
[
17
, §3.4, proof of Prop. 3.4.4, page 144f.]
. Observe that we have

\displaystyle\rho(p,\nu)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\left(p,e_{f(p)}\right)}\Phi(p,\nu)\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N

for all
(p,\nu)\in f^{*}\mathcal{g}
, and thus describes a base-preserving vector bundle morphism
f^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{T}N
. We can rewrite Eq. (
13
) to

\displaystyle f\circ\Phi

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}
is the projection onto the second factor in
f^{*}\mathcal{G}\subset N\times\mathcal{G}
. Thence, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}f\circ\rho

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}f\circ\left.\mathrm{D}\Phi\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\left.\mathrm{D}(f\circ\Phi)\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\left.\left(\mathrm{D}\pi\circ\mathrm{Dpr}_{2}^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}\right)\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\left.\mathrm{D}\pi\right|_{\mathcal{g}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}=0,

making use of that
\mathcal{g}
consists of vertical vectors, and where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}
is the projection onto the second factor in
f^{*}\mathcal{g}\subset N\times\mathcal{g}
.

The proof of Eq. (
25
) is as straightforward as usual, as a quick argument for the experienced reader observe that Eq. (
15
) and (
14
) imply that
\Phi
induces a Lie group homomorphism
\Gamma(\mathcal{G})\to\mathrm{Diff}(M)
(
\mathrm{Diff}
the group of diffeomorphisms) so that we have the desired Lie algebra homomorphism on an infinitesimal scale.
6
6

6

We use the notion of sections to avoid the possible lack of smooth structure on the preimages of
f
.
Let
\mu,\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
, then we know that

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\mu),\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\nu)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}\Phi\left(\left[f^{*}\mu,f^{*}\nu\right]_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right),

if we can show that
\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\mu)
and
\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\nu)
are
\Phi
-related to
f^{*}\mu
and
f^{*}\nu
, respectively; this is a common procedure, see for example
[
17
, §A.1, Prop. A.1.49, page 615]
. That is, we need to show now that

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\mu)\right|_{\Phi(p,g)=p\cdot g}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\left(\left.\left(f^{*}\mu\right)\right|_{(p,g)}\right)

(26)

for all
(p,g)\in N*\mathcal{G}
, similarly for
\nu
; here we understand the LABs of LGBs as left-invariant vector fields as in Cor.
4.2
. Observe that we have in general for
\eta\in\Gamma(f^{*}\mathcal{g})\cong L(f^{*}\mathcal{G})
, using the definition of
\rho
,

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}\phi(\eta)\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p\cdot g,e_{x})}\Phi\left(\eta_{(p\cdot g,e_{x})}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Phi\left(p\cdot g,\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\xi_{p\cdot g}}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(p\cdot g\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\xi_{p\cdot g}}\right)

for all
(p,g)\in N*\mathcal{G}
,
where
t\in\mathbb{R}
and we write in general
\eta_{(p,e_{x})}=\left(p,\xi_{p}\right)
with
\xi_{p}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
(
x\coloneqq f(p)
), and the exponential
\mathrm{e\;\!}
is the one of
\mathcal{G}
. But we also have similarly

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\bigl(\underbrace{\eta_{(p,g)}}_{\mathclap{=\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}L_{(p,g)}\left(\eta_{(p,e_{x})}\right)}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\left(\Phi\circ L_{(p,g)}\right)\left(p,\xi_{p}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Phi\left(p,g\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\xi_{p}}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(p\cdot g\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\xi_{p}}\right),

where
L
is the left-multiplication in
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
. So, in order to achieve Eq. (
26
) we now set
\eta=f^{*}\mu
. That is,

\eta_{(p,e_{x})}=\left(p,\mu_{f(p)}\right),

therefore
\xi_{p}=\mu_{f(p)}
,
and due to Eq. (
13
) and Rem.
3.1
we derive
\xi_{p}=\xi_{p\cdot g}
. In total, we see that Eq. (
26
) is satisfied.

Finally, as argued earlier, we can prove

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\mu),\mathrm{D}\Phi(f^{*}\nu)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}\Phi\left(\left[f^{*}\mu,f^{*}\nu\right]_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)=\mathrm{D}\Phi\left(f^{*}\left(\left[\mu,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)\right),

making use of that the field of Lie brackets of
f^{*}\mathcal{g}
is the
f
-pullback of
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
as a section. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.12:
Variants of the LAB action as Lie algebra homomorphism

It is clear that one has a local version of Eq. (
25
). In fact, as one sees in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, the argument for Eq. (
25
) works pointwise in
M
, so, one may also think of a homomorphism of Lie algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{g}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{X}\left(f^{-1}(\{x\})\right),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[f^{-1}(\{x\})\ni p\mapsto\rho(p,\nu)\right]

for all
x\in M
, where we also used Eq. (
24
); that is, we expect that an LAB action is fibre-wise a Lie algebra action. Due to a possible lack of manifold structure on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
we did not restrict to
x\in M
to avoid technical difficulties. However,
f
will be the projection of a bundle later, and in that case the fibre
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold of
N
. As argued in Remark
3.1
, the
\mathcal{G}
-action is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-action restricted on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
. By what we know about Lie group actions we immediately know that we have the aforementioned Lie algebra homomorphism, and so also Eq. (
25
), in total a vector bundle morphism
f^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{T}N
which gives fibre-wise over
x
rise to a Lie algebra action.

An important example will be fundamental vector fields which we will introduce later. Another example are LAB representations induced by LGB representations; hence let us introduce LAB representations. We will introduce them in a reverted order than the LGB representations, that is, first defining them as certain LAB morphisms, and then trivially concluding a relation to LAB actions; for the following recall Ex.
4.1
.

Definition 5.13:
LAB representations,
[
10
, §3.3, Def. 3.3.13, page 107]

Let
\mathcal{g}\to M
be an LAB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
V\to M
be a vector bundle. A
\mathcal{g}
-representation on
V
is an LAB morphism
\psi:\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{End}(V)
.

Corollary 5.14:
LAB representations are specific LAB actions,

[
10
, §4.1, special consequence of Prop. 4.1.7 but we do not assume integrability of the LAB, page 153]

Let
\mathcal{g}\to M
be an LAB over a smooth manifold
M
,
V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}M
be a vector bundle, and
\psi:\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{End}(V)
a
\mathcal{g}
-representation on
V
. Then
\psi
defines an LAB action
\widetilde{\psi}:p^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{T}V
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}(v,\nu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\psi(\nu)(v)

for all
(v,\nu)\in p^{*}\mathcal{g}
, where one makes use of the identification
\mathrm{T}_{v}V_{x}=\mathrm{V}_{v}V\cong V_{x}
(
x\coloneqq p(v)
), so that
V_{x}\subset\mathrm{T}_{v}V
.

Remarks 5.15
.

In fact, similar to LGBs we could have defined LAB representations as a certain type of LAB action with values in certain
linear vector fields
. However, it would exceed this work to introduce these vector fields; start with
[
10
, §3.4, page 110]
for more elaborated details on how to do this.

As for LGB representations and linear actions, we denote both, LAB representation and its associated sense of action, in the same fashion.

This is a known relationship in the case of Lie algebra representations on vector spaces and their associated actions; usually this is proven by assuming a Lie group representation, however, one can show it without assuming integrability of the Lie algebra action. See for example
[
6
, §2.1, proof of Prop. 2.1.16, page 22]
. Hence, we will just show that the proof of Cor.
5.1
breaks down to proving it for Lie algebras acting on vector spaces and refer to this reference for the remaining part of the proof.

Proof of Cor.
5.1
.

By construction we have that
\widetilde{\psi}
has values in the vertical bundle
\mathrm{V}V
of
V
, hence Eq. (
24
) follows.
Making use of
\mathrm{V}_{v}V\cong V_{x}
for all
v\in V_{x}
(
x\in M
), it is clear that
p^{*}V\cong\mathrm{V}V
as vector bundles, and so it is trivial to see that
\widetilde{\psi}
as a map
p^{*}\mathcal{g}\to p^{*}V
is a smooth and base-preserving vector bundle morphism due to that
\psi
is a smooth map with values in
\mathrm{End}(V)
(making again use of that
p^{*}\mathcal{g}
is an embedded submanifold of
V\times\mathcal{g}
, similarly for
p^{*}V
as embedded submanifold of
V\times V
).

As argued in Rem.
5.1
, since
\mathcal{g}
acts via a representation on a bundle
V
,
\widetilde{\psi}:p^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{V}V
will be an LAB action, if it induces a Lie algebra action over each base point
x\in M
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{g}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{X}(V_{x}),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[V_{x}\ni v\mapsto\widetilde{\psi}\left(v,\nu\right)\right].

Since
\psi
is just a
\mathcal{g}_{x}
-representation on
V_{x}
over
x
we know by
[
6
, §2.1, proof of Prop. 2.1.16, page 22]
that
\psi
indeed gives rise to a
\mathcal{g}_{x}
-action on
V_{x}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}\left(v,\nu\right)

\displaystyle=-\psi(\nu)(v)

for all
v\in V_{x}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
, which is precisely the form of
\widetilde{\psi}
. Thus,
\widetilde{\psi}
is an LAB action.
∎

Usually statements like Cor.
5.1
are proven by assuming integrability: LGB representations as linear actions
V*\mathcal{G}\to V
and as LGB morphisms
\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(V)
are the same by Cor.
5.1
; the former induces LAB actions
p^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{T}V
by Lemma
5.1
, and in the same manner the latter implies LAB morphisms
\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{End}(V)
by Cor.
4.5
. Either way, LAB representations can be viewed as certain LAB actions.

The adjoint representation is an important example inherited by an LGB representation; this example will conclude this subsection.

Example 5.16:
Adjoint LAB representations,

[
10
, §3.3, special situation of Ex. 3.3.15, page 108]

Let us assume the same situation as in Ex.
5.1
, especially we have the adjoint representation of the LGB
\mathcal{G}\to M
,
\mathrm{Ad}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{g})
. As discussed earlier, its infinitesimal version is a
\mathcal{g}
-representation on itself, the
adjoint representation
\mathrm{ad}
of
\mathcal{g}
. By construction,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\nu}(\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{ad}(\nu)(\mu)=\left[\nu,\mu\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}

for all
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
(
x\in M
).

5.2
Fundamental vector fields

The LAB actions inherited by LGB actions are also called fundamental vector fields; we are following
[
17
, §3.4, generalization of Def. 3.4.1, page 143]
for the labelling:

Definition 5.17:
Fundamental vector fields

Let
M
and
N
be two smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\to M
an LGB over
M
,
f:N\to M
a smooth map, and assume we have a right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
, denoted as
\Phi:N*\mathcal{G}\to N
. For
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
we define its induced
fundamental vector field
\widetilde{\nu}
as an element of
\mathfrak{X}(N)
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{e_{f(p)}}\Phi_{p}\left(\nu_{f(p)}\right)

for all
p\in N
, where
\Phi_{p}
is the orbit map defined in Def.
3.1
and
e_{f(p)}
the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}
.

For left actions we define fundamental vector fields similarly by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{e_{f(p)}}\Phi^{\prime}_{p}\left(\nu_{f(p)}\right)

for all
p\in N
,
where
\Phi^{\prime}_{p}
is a slightly adjusted orbit map given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}

\displaystyle\to N,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto g^{-1}\cdot p.

Remark 5.18:
Notation

Again, point-wise over
x\coloneqq f(p)
this is just the typical definition of a fundamental vector field with respect to
\nu_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
(except that
f^{-1}(\{x\})
may not be a manifold). Hence, one has also a point-wise definition which we will also denote similarly by
\widetilde{\nu_{x}}
. If
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is not a manifold, then
\left.\widetilde{\nu_{x}}\right|_{p}
is just a formal notation, and it only defines an element of
\mathrm{T}_{p}N
which may not be related to a vector field on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
, not even to a vector field on
N
if
\nu_{x}
does not formally come from a fixed section of
\mathcal{g}
.

However, if
f
is
e.g.
the projection of a bundle, then we have a
\mathcal{G}
-action on the fibre
f^{-1}(\{x\})
as manifold, and therefore
\widetilde{\nu}|_{f^{-1}(\{x\})}
and
\widetilde{\nu_{x}}
give rise to a vector field on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
. In other words, fundamental vector fields are vertical vector fields as expected and are fibre-wise fundamental vector fields coming from a Lie group action.

For long expressions we use the following different font

\displaystyle\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\nu}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits

instead of
\widetilde{\nu}
.

Remark 5.19:
Map to fundamental vector fields an LAB action

As anticipated, this is related to the LAB action coming from the right
\mathcal{G}
-action
\Phi:N*\mathcal{G}\to N
. The following can also be shown for left actions in a similar manner by recalling Rem.
5.11
.

By Lemma
5.1
we have an LAB action
\rho:f^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathrm{T}N
given by
\left.\rho\coloneqq\mathrm{D}\Phi\right|_{f^{*}\mathcal{g}}
. We have

\displaystyle\rho(p,\nu)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Phi\left(p,\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{f(p)}}\Phi_{p}(\nu)=\widetilde{\nu}_{p}

for all
(p,\nu)\in f^{*}\mathcal{g}
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathrm{e\;\!}
is the exponential map of
\mathcal{G}
, and
e_{f(p)}
the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{f(p)}
. Thus, the map to fundamental vector fields

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{g})

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathfrak{X}(N),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\nu},

is equivalent to the map

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathcal{g})

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{X}(N),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\rho\left(f^{*}\nu\right)

induced by
\rho
as in Def.
5.1
, which also implies that the map to the fundamental vector fields is a homomorphism of Lie algebras. Last but not least, by Def.
5.1
it follows that fundamental vector fields are in the kernel of
\mathrm{D}f
.

5.3
Differential of smooth LGB actions

Lemma 5.20:
Tangent bundle of pullback fibre bundles

Let
M
and
N
be two smooth manifolds,
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a fibre bundle over
M
, and
f:N\to M
a smooth map. Then we have for its tangent spaces

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{(p,v)}\left(f^{*}F\right)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(X,Y)\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{v}F\text{ with }\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)=\mathrm{D}_{v}\pi(Y)\bigr\}=\mathrm{T}N\mathbin{{}_{\mathrm{D}f}\times_{\mathrm{D}\pi}}\mathrm{T}F

for all
(p,v)\in f^{*}F
.

Proof.

Recall that
(p,v)\in f^{*}F
implies that

\displaystyle f(p)

\displaystyle=\pi(v)

such that we can immediately derive its infinitesimal version as

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)=\mathrm{D}_{v}\pi(Y)

for all
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{v}F
. Hence, we have derived that
\mathrm{T}_{(p,v)}\left(f^{*}F\right)
is a subset of the set of such pairs
(X,Y)
. That this is an equivalent description quickly follows by the fact that
f
and
\pi
are transversal to each other (trivially, because
\pi
is a surjective submersion). This means, the following linear map

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{p}N\times\mathrm{T}_{v}F

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}_{f(p)}M,

\displaystyle(X,Y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)-\mathrm{D}_{v}\pi(Y)

is surjective because
\pi
is a submersion; it is also well-defined because of
f(p)=\pi(v)
. Hence, the dimension of the kernel of this map has the dimension

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(N)+\mathrm{dim}(F)-\mathrm{dim}(M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}(N)+\mathrm{rk}(F)=\mathrm{dim}(f^{*}N),

where
\mathrm{dim}
denotes the dimension as a manifold and
\mathrm{rk}
the rank of a bundle (the dimension of its structural fibre). Its dimension is precisely the dimension of
f^{*}F
, and since it is about finite dimensions we can therefore identify
\mathrm{T}_{(p,v)}\left(f^{*}F\right)
with this kernel. This concludes the proof due to the fact that the kernel consists of
(X,Y)
with
\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)=\mathrm{D}_{v}\pi(Y)
.
∎

With this we can finally show the following theorem; also recall the notations introduced in Def.
3.1
,
4.2
,
4.3
and
5.2
.

Theorem 5.21:
Differential of smooth LGB actions

Let
M
and
N
be two smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
an LGB over
M
,
f:N\to M
a smooth map, and assume we have a right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
N
,
\Phi:N*\mathcal{G}\to N
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\left(Y-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

for all
(p,g)\in N*\mathcal{G}
and
(X,Y)\in\mathrm{T}_{(p,g)}(N*\mathcal{G})
, where
x\coloneqq f(p)=\pi(g)
,
\sigma
is any (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
with
\sigma_{x}=g
,
e
is the identity section of
\mathcal{G}
, and
\omega
is an element of
\mathrm{T}_{x}M
given by

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y).

We can also write instead

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}.

If
f
is a surjective submersion, then we can also write

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}(Y)-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma})\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)

(31)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\Bigl(X-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{\tau}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)+\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}\Bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr),

(32)

where
\tau
is any (local) section
7
7

7

That is,
f\circ\tau=\mathds{1}_{M}
(locally).
of
f
with
\tau_{x}=p
, and
\Phi_{\tau}
is the orbit map through
\tau
.

Remarks 5.22
.

The assumption about
f
being a surjective submersion is being stated in order to assure the existence of
\tau
and the manifold structure on
f^{-1}(\{x\})
as an embedded submanifold of
N
; see the proof for more details. If the existence of
\tau
and the embedded submanifold structure is known otherwise, then those equations can still be derived. Following the proof, one may also just need the structure of an immersed submanifold.

Proof of Thm.
5.3
.

We want to calculate the derivative of
\Phi
, and due to
N*\mathcal{G}=f^{*}\mathcal{G}
we are going to use Lemma
5.3
. That is, fix
(p,g)\in N*\mathcal{G}
and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N
,
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y)\eqqcolon\omega\in\mathrm{T}_{f(p)}M.

Recall that we can localize LGB actions in sense of Rem.
3.1
; so, let
x\coloneqq f(p)=\pi(g)
, and fix a trivialization of
\mathcal{G}
around
x
. Then it is clear that there is a (local)
8
8

8

For simplicity of notation we omit the notation of restricting on some open subset of
M
.
section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
with
\sigma_{x}=g
. Observe that we can write

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)+\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega).

The two first summands result into a vertical tangent vector due to

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y)-\mathrm{D}_{x}\underbrace{(\pi\circ\sigma)}_{=\mathds{1}_{M}}(\omega)=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y)-\omega=0.

For the following recall the notations introduced in Def.
3.1
and
4.2
; we can derive that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{D}_{x}e

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{x}\underbrace{\left(R_{\sigma}\circ e\right)}_{\mathclap{x\mapsto e_{x}\sigma_{x}=\sigma_{x}}}=\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma.

So, we can also write

\displaystyle Y^{v}

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)=Y-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr).

We have proven that
Y^{v}\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
, and thus
(0_{p},Y^{v})\in\mathrm{T}_{(p,g)}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})
by Lemma
5.3
, where
0_{p}
is the zero tangent vector of
\mathrm{T}_{p}N
. In the same fashion we also have

\displaystyle\left(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{T}_{(p,g)}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})

because of

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y-Y^{v})=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi(Y)=\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X),

thence we can write in
\mathrm{T}_{(p,g)}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)+Y^{v}\right)=\left(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\right)+(0_{p},Y^{v}).

Hence also

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\left(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\right)+\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(0_{p},Y^{v})

(33)

The second summand is quickly calculated as

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(0_{p},Y^{v})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Phi(p,\gamma)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\underbrace{(p\cdot\gamma)}_{\Phi_{p}(\gamma)}=\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})

where
\gamma:I\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
I
open interval of
\mathbb{R}
containing 0) is a curve with
\gamma(0)=g
and
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t|_{t=0}\gamma=Y^{v}
; this is due to the verticality of
Y^{v}
, and so
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})
is also well-defined since
\Phi_{p}
is a map
\mathcal{G}_{x}\to N
. Now recall Def.
4.3
and
5.2
, and then observe that we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}\circ\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}L_{g}\circ\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}\right)(Y^{v})=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\underbrace{\left(\Phi_{p}\circ L_{g}\right)}_{\mathclap{\mathcal{G}\ni q\mapsto p\cdot gq=\Phi_{p\cdot g}(q)}}\left(\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}(Y^{v})\right)=\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}(Y^{v})}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

making use of the verticality of
Y^{v}
such that operators like
\mathrm{D}L_{g}
can act on
Y^{v}
.

For the first summand in Eq. (
33
) we use

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\Bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\underbrace{\bigl(\Phi\circ\left(\mathds{1}_{N},R_{\sigma}\right)\bigr)}_{\mathclap{N*\mathcal{G}\ni(p,g)\mapsto p\cdot g\sigma_{x}=r_{\sigma}(p\cdot g)}}\bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}(r_{\sigma}\circ\Phi)\bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\Phi\bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\Phi\bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{p}(e\circ f)(X)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{(p,p)}\bigl(\Phi\circ(\mathds{1}_{N},e\circ f)\bigr)(X,X)\Bigr),

but on the diagonal
\mathrm{diag}(N\times N)
of
N\times N
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl[\mathrm{diag}(N\times N)\ni(p,p)\mapsto\bigl(\Phi\circ(\mathds{1}_{N},e\circ f)\bigr)(p,p)=\Phi\left(p,e_{f(p)}\right)=p\cdot e_{f(p)}=p\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\left.\mathrm{pr}_{i}\right|_{\mathrm{diag}(N\times N)},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{i}
is just the projection onto any of both
i
-th components (
i\in\{1,2\}
) in
N\times N
. Due to
(X,X)\in\mathrm{T}_{(p,p)}\bigl(\mathrm{diag}(N\times N)\bigr)
we derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,p)}\bigl(\Phi\circ(\mathds{1}_{N},e\circ f)\bigr)(X,X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,p)}\mathrm{pr}_{i}(X,X)=X.

So, we get in total

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\Bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X),

therefore

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}(Y^{v})}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}.

If
f
is a surjective submersion, then
x
is a regular value and thus
f^{-1}(\{x\})
is an embedded submanifold, and we can assure the existence of a smooth local section
\tau:U\to N
of
f
(
U
an open neighbourhood of
x\in M
),
i.e.

f\circ\tau=\mathds{1}_{U}
with
\tau_{x}=p
; in case of doubt, this can be shown as in
[
17
, §3.7, Lemma 3.7.4, page 152f.]
via the Regular Point Theorem. Recalling the arguments of Rem.
4.2
, we can rewrite the second summand of Eq. (
33
) instead to

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}(Y^{v})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}(Y)-\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}\left(\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}(Y)-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma})\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)

making use of that
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}
is linear map
\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}N
. In that case we would get in total

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{\tau}(Y)-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma})\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr).

Alternatively, we do not rewrite Eq. (
33
) with the Maurer-Cartan form and instead apply the same trick to
X
as for
Y
, that is,

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=X^{v}+\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{\tau}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr),

where

\displaystyle X^{v}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X-\mathrm{D}_{x}\tau(\omega)=X-\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{\tau}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr),

and
X^{v}
is vertical, too, that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X^{v})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)-\mathrm{D}_{x}(f\circ\tau)(\omega)=\omega-\omega=0,

especially
X^{v}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\left(f^{-1}(\{x\})\right)
and so we can apply a similar argument as in Rem.
4.2
to derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{\tau}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\left(r_{\sigma}\circ\Phi_{\tau}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr).

Finally, using these expressions, the total formula would look like

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\underbrace{\left(r_{\sigma}\circ\Phi_{\tau}\right)}_{\mathclap{\mathcal{G}\ni g\mapsto\tau_{\pi(g)}\cdot g\sigma_{\pi(g)}=(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma})(g)}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{p}(Y^{v})+\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\left(\Phi_{\tau}\circ R_{\sigma}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\omega)\bigr).

∎

Remarks 5.23
.

Eq. (
5.3
) is very similar to the ”classical” formula used in gauge theory, see
e.g.

[
17
, §3.5, Prop. 3.5.4, page 149]
: In the case of a Lie group action on
N
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X)+\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{(\mu_{G})_{g}(Y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{p\cdot g}

for all
p\in N
,
g\in G
,
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{g}G
.
However, in our general case the vector
Y
is deformed by
\omega
, due to the fact that the action
\Phi
has no ”constant” Lie group factor anymore. This will be important later when we are going to derive the gauge transformations. Furthermore, already the first summand is now different than the classical formula, because we need to use LGB sections in order to define the push-forward of tangent vectors which are not vertical, that is,
X
may not be a tangent vector of
f^{-1}(\{x\})
(which is in the general case not even an embedded submanifold) such that
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X)
is in general not well-defined anymore.

The other two equations in the case of
f
being a surjective submersion are mainly for reference; the last equation, Eq. (
5.3
), emphasises the contribution of non-vertical vectors measured by
\omega
. While the first two summands are the classical product rule on the vertical parts, the third summand shows the deformation of the product rule because of the new structure of an action without a ”constant” Lie group factor.

Eq. (
31
) may be the most elegant formulation, making use of local sections
\sigma
and
\tau
and their advantage that these can act on all tangent vectors, not just the vertical ones; the reader who knows Lie groupoids may recognize this equation’s structure with the one as given in
[
10
, §1.4, Thm. 1.4.14, page 28]
, where it is about the induced multiplication structure on the tangent bundle of a Lie groupoid making use of bisections playing a similar role like
\sigma
and
\tau
.

Those equations additionally show that we have a more general Leibniz rule with a third summand. However, by Ex.
3.2
we expect still a typical Leibniz rule once a trivialization of
\mathcal{G}
around
g
is fixed. Indeed, as we will understand and see also later, this is the case; fix such a trivialization, equip it with a canonical flat connection, and take
\sigma
to be a parallel section, that is,
g
as a constant section. Then one can calculate that the typical Leibniz rule is recovered. Hence, one can view this Leibniz rule as the ”covariantized” version of the ”classical” Leibniz rule, independent of a choice of ”coordinate”/section on
\mathcal{G}
.

6
Connections and curvature on principal LGB-bundles

6.1
Principal bundles with structural LGB

6.1.1
Definition

The principal bundle
\mathcal{P}
we are interested into is still a fibre bundle related to the same Lie group as the one behind the LGB
\mathcal{G}
, especially also
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{P})=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{G})
, but it is equipped with an LGB action.

Definition 6.1:
Principal bundles with structural LGB,

[
11
, simplification of the beginning of §5.7, page 144f.]

Let
G
be a Lie group,
M
a smooth manifold, and an LGB
\mathcal{G}\to M
which acts on the right on another
G
-fibre bundle
\mathcal{P}\to M
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}

where the right-action is defined on
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
given by
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}\mathcal{G}
.
Then we call
\mathcal{P}
a
principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle
if

1.

The right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}

is simply transitive on the fibres
, that is, the restriction of
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{P}
on
x\in M

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{x}

induces bijective orbit maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{x},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto p\cdot g

for
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
.

2.

There exist base-preserving
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{G}|_{U_{i}}
subordinate to an open covering
\left(U_{i}\right)_{i}
of
M
, that is,

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{P}},

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i}(p)\cdot g,

where the multiplication on the right hand side is inherited by
\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
, the multiplication on
\mathcal{G}
.

The LGB
\mathcal{G}
is the
structural LGB
of the principal bundle
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 6.2:
Discussion about the definition of
\mathcal{G}
-principal bundles

There are several things in need to be discussed:

1.

The mentioned reference,
[
11
, simplification of the beginning of §5.7, page 144f.]
, introduces these principal bundles in a different manner; we will come back later to this in Remark
6.13
.

2.

By Remark
3.1
the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
is fibre-preserving, thus, the restriction of
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{P}
is indeed a map
\mathcal{P}_{x}\times\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{x}
.

3.

The
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{i}
give rise to a
bundle atlas of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant bundle charts
\xi_{i}:\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\to U_{i}\times G
. Assume w.l.o.g. that
U_{i}
is small enough so that there is an LGB chart
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{G}|_{U_{i}}\to U_{i}\times G
of
\mathcal{G}
; recall Def.
2.1
. Then
\xi_{i}\coloneqq\phi_{i}\circ\varphi_{i}
, which clearly satisfies

\displaystyle\xi_{i}(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=\xi_{i}(p)\cdot\phi_{i}(g),

where the multiplication on the right hand side is the canonical one for
U_{i}\times G
as a trivial LGB (recall Ex.
2.1
),
and this can be viewed as
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance (under the trivialization induced by
\phi_{i}
). We usually then speak of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance w.r.t. the LGB morphism
\phi_{i}
.

We will call such an atlas and its charts a
principal bundle atlas
and
principal bundle charts
for
\mathcal{P}
, respectively. For simplicity we may also refer to
\varphi_{i}
as principal bundle chart giving rise to the principal bundle atlas.

4.

Due to the existence of
\varphi_{i}
one does not need to claim upfront that
\mathcal{P}
is a
G
-fibre bundle. However, for readability, we decided to structure the definition like this. We kept a similar style as for ”typical” principal bundles as provided in
[
17
, §4.2, Def. 4.2.1, page 207f.]
.

5.

Since
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is a surjective submersion we know by Remark
3.11
that right-translations
r_{g}
(
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
) are diffeomorphisms on
\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Furthermore, following
[
17
, §4.2, discussion after Def. 4.2.1, page 208f.]
, by definition we have a simply transitive
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-action (as a Lie group) on
\mathcal{P}_{x}
, and the isotropy group for each
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
is trivial; the isotropy group consists in general of
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
with
p\cdot g=p
, see
e.g.

[
17
, §3.2, third part of Def. 3.2.4, page 132]
. Therefore, and by
[
17
, §3.8, Thm. 3.8.8, page 165]
, the orbit map
\Phi_{p}
gives rise to a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{x},

where the action on
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the right-action on itself regarding the
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariance.

6.

Similarly by definition, for each
x\in U_{i}
we know that
\left.\left(\varphi_{i}\right)\right|_{x}:\mathcal{P}_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism. In fact, together with
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\varphi_{i}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
this clearly gives an equivalent definition of
\varphi_{i}
which we may make use in the following without further mention.

6.1.2
Examples

Let us provide examples of such principal bundles.

Example 6.3:
The ”classical” principal bundle

We recover principal
G
-bundles as principal
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq M\times G
-bundles. Recall Ex.
3.2
, the LGB action of a trivial LGB
\mathcal{G}\cong M\times G
is equivalent to a
G
-action, the right-translation with an element
g\in G
is then the right-translation of the corresponding constant section in
\mathcal{G}
; this action is clearly simply transitive. The principal
G
-bundle atlas is then naturally inherited by the existing principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle atlas. In fact, reverting this argument proves that principal
G
-bundles are equivalent to principal
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq M\times G
-bundles.

We often refer to principal bundles related to trivial LGBs as
typical
or
classical principal bundles
, or, as usual, principal
G
-bundle.

Example 6.4:
The ”trivial” principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle

\mathcal{G}
itself is a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, equipped with its canonical right-action inherited by its multiplication
\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
. The principal bundle atlas then just consists of the identity map
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

By Ex.
6.1.2
, it may be natural to call this the
trivial
\mathcal{G}
-principal bundle
even if
\mathcal{G}
itself might not be trivial. It will be clearer later why one may choose to do so.

The remarkable property is that this example shows that we are going to define a gauge theory for which LGBs themselves are allowed as principal bundles. One might have wondered why classical gauge theory uses classical principal bundles instead of LGBs (especially including non-trivial LGBs), because LGBs could be viewed as a more natural choice due to the fact that they are an analogue to how vector bundles are the ”bundle-construction” of vector spaces. The problems described in Subsection
6.2.1
show why it was easier to choose classical principal bundles, since these avoid the difficulties regarding the definition of a connection; however, we are going to solve these problems in such a way that one can either use LGBs or classical bundles or even something more general.

Our main example will be the inner bundle of a classical principal bundle, recall Ex.
2.2
. To explain the ”triviality” of Ex.
6.1.2
we need to introduce morphisms of principal bundles.

6.1.3
Morphism of principal LGB-bundles

Let us define morphisms of LGB-principal bundles

Definition 6.5:
Morphism of principal bundles with structural LGB

Let
M
and
N
be smooth manifolds,
\mathcal{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{H}}}}{{\to}}N
and
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
LGBs, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}}}{{\to}}N
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
principal
\mathcal{H}
- and
\mathcal{G}
-bundles, respectively. A
principal bundle morphism
between
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
is a triple of smooth maps
F:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
,
f:N\to M
and
H:\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}
such that the pair
(F,f)
is an LGB morphism as in Def.
2.1
and

\displaystyle\pi\circ H

\displaystyle=f\circ\pi^{\prime},

(36)

\displaystyle H(p\cdot h)

\displaystyle=H(p)\cdot F(h)

(37)

for all
(p,h)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}*\mathcal{H}=\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)^{*}\mathcal{H}
. We also speak of a
principal bundle morphism over
f
w.r.t. the LGB morphism
F
.

We speak of a
principal bundle isomorphism (over
f
, w.r.t.
F
)
if
H
is a diffeomorphism.

If
H
is a base-preserving isomorphism
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
w.r.t.
F=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}}
, then we say that
H
is a
(global) gauge transformation
or
principal bundle automorphism
, and the set of all such automorphisms is denoted by
\sAut(\mathcal{P})
. If
H
is defined on an open subset of
M
, then we may also speak of a
local gauge transformation
.

Remarks 6.6
.

\bullet
Observe that the right hand side of Eq. (
37
) is well-defined because of Eq. (
36
),
(p,h)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}*\mathcal{H}
and Def.
2.1
, that is,

\displaystyle\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ F\right)(h)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\ref{def:LGB morphism}}}}{{=}}\left(f\circ\pi_{\mathcal{H}}\right)(h)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}(p)=\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(h)}}{{=}}\left(f\circ\pi^{\prime}\right)(p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{PrincMorphoverBaseMap}}}{{=}}\left(\pi\circ H\right)(p),

thus,

\displaystyle\bigl(H(p),F(h)\bigr)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}.

Furthermore, by additionally using Remark
6.1.1
we know that LGB actions preserve the fibres of the principal bundles, therefore both,
H(p\cdot h)
and
H(p)\cdot F(h)
, are over the same base point
\left(f\circ\pi^{\prime}\right)(p)
, so that Eq. (
37
) as a whole is well-defined.

\bullet
Also observe that one can conclude that
(F,f)
has to be an LGB morphism in order to have a satisfied and well-defined Eq. (
37
), assuming that
H
is a map over
f
. To well-define the right hand side,
F
has to be a map over
f
, since
H
is defined over
f
. Assuming Eq. (
37
), for
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\prime}(p)}
we have

\displaystyle H\left(p\cdot h^{\prime}h\right)

\displaystyle=H(p)\cdot F\left(h^{\prime}h\right),

but we also get by associativity

\displaystyle H\left(p\cdot h^{\prime}h\right)

\displaystyle=H\left(p\cdot h^{\prime}\right)\cdot F(h)=H(p)\cdot F\left(h^{\prime}\right)\penalty\ F(h).

Using that
\mathcal{G}
acts simply transitive on
\mathcal{P}
, we derive

\displaystyle F\left(h^{\prime}h\right)

\displaystyle=F\left(h^{\prime}\right)\penalty\ F(h).

\bullet
Assume
H
is a diffeomorphism, then
F
is an LGB isomorphism. For this we only have to show that
F
is a diffeomorphism by Def.
2.1
; by Remark
2.5
, also
f
is then a diffeomorphism. Thence, let us show that
F
is a diffeomorphism. This follows by the fact that the LGB actions are simply transitive on the fibres of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
,
i.e.
orbit maps are diffeomorphisms, also recall Remark
6.1.1
. Denoting the orbit maps inherited by the action on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
by
\Phi^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
and
\Phi^{\mathcal{P}}
, respectively, we can write

\displaystyle F(h)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\Phi^{\mathcal{P}}_{H(p)}\right)^{-1}\circ H\circ\Phi^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}_{p}\right)(h)

for arbitrary
p
by rewriting Eq. (
37
). Hence,
F
is a diffeomorphism as the composition of diffeomorphisms.

Last but not least, it follows that
H^{-1}
is then
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant in the sense of

\displaystyle H^{-1}(q\cdot g)

\displaystyle=H^{-1}(q)\cdot F^{-1}(g)

for all
(q,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
, which is well-defined by similar arguments as before, especially because the inverses are maps over
f^{-1}
. To prove this, observe that there is a unique
(p,h)\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}*\mathcal{H}
such that
H(p)=q
and
F(h)=g
due to the fact that both are bijective maps over
f
.
9
9

9

In fact, it is easy to prove that
(H,F):\mathcal{P}^{\prime}*\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
is an LGB isomorphism (over
H
); also recall Cor.
3.1
.
Then

\displaystyle H^{-1}(q\cdot g)

\displaystyle=H^{-1}\bigl(\underbrace{H(p)\cdot F(h)}_{=H(p\cdot h)}\bigr)=p\cdot h=H^{-1}(q)\cdot F^{-1}(g).

Thence, Def.
6.1.3
is a valid definition for a principal bundle morphism, because it is easy to check that the principal bundle atlas on
\mathcal{P}
(consisting of
\varphi_{i}
related to an open covering
\left(U_{i}\right)_{i}
of
M
) is related to the principal bundle atlas on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\left(F^{-1}\circ\varphi_{i}\circ H\right)|_{f^{-1}(U_{i})}.

\bullet
Finally, observe that we have locally an isomorphism of every principal bundle
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{G}
: Fix a principal bundle chart
\varphi:\mathcal{P}|_{U}\to\mathcal{G}|_{U}
(
U
some open subset of
M
). Then take
H\coloneqq\varphi
,
F\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}|_{U}}
and
f\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{U}
; by the definition of a principal bundle chart this gives a principal bundle isomorphism. Therefore one could say that every principal bundle is locally ”trivial” in the sense of being an LGB; recall Ex.
6.1.2
.

Additionally using Remark
6.1.1
we have locally an isomorphism of
\mathcal{P}
to a trivial LGB; keeping the same notation as in Remark
6.1.1
, choose
H\coloneqq\xi_{i}
,
F\coloneqq\phi_{i}
and
f\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{U_{i}}
. Hence, locally every principal bundle is also classical in the sense of Ex.
6.1.2
.

As expected, there is a natural isomorphism induced by local sections of
\mathcal{P}
, which are, however, no trivializations in general. In other words, by Remark
6.1.1
we know that orbit maps through the fibres of
\mathcal{P}
are equivariant diffeomorphisms, we want to show the same for the orbit map through a section of
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 6.7:
Local sections of principal bundles induce isomorphisms to the structural LGB

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\to M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Let
s:U\to\mathcal{P}
be a smooth local section of
\mathcal{P}
over an open subset
U
of
M
. Then the orbit map
\Phi_{s}
through
s
, given as in Def.
3.1
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}|_{U}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}|_{U},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)}\cdot g,

is a base-preserving principal bundle isomorphism w.r.t.
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}|_{U}}
.

Remarks 6.8
.

As in the typical formulation of gauge theory, we have an isomorphism induced by sections, but it is not necessarily a trivialization as fibre bundle, so that we do not necessarily also have a ”classical” bundle; it is a trivialization in the sense of Ex.
6.1.2
. Due to the similarity with the ”classical” statement, we therefore were speaking of the ”trivial” principal bundle in Ex.
6.1.2
. Of course, since every LGB is locally trivial, we can find a typical trivialization by taking a ”local-enough” section.

The nomenclature about calling LGBs ”trivial” principal bundles is also introduced and discussed in
[
11
, §5.7, third and fourth part of Remark 5.34, page 145]
for groupoid-based principal bundles.

Proof of Lemma
6.1.3
.

The proof is similar to the ”classical” statement as
e.g.
given in
[
17
, §4.2, proof of Lemma 4.2.7, page 210ff.]
, but generalized since we have to treat a possible non-triviality of
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
. As already discussed, the orbit map
\Phi_{s}
is well-defined because of
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\circ s=\mathds{1}_{U}
such that
\left(s_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)},g\right)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
. Via Rem.
3.11
we also know that
\Phi_{s}
is also smooth,
and it is point-wise the orbit map
\Phi_{s_{x}}
through
s_{x}
which is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism by Remark
6.1.1
. So,
\Phi_{s}
is fibre-wise bijective and therefore bijective as a whole since it is base-preserving, and it is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant w.r.t.
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Now we want to use the inverse function theorem to show that its inverse is also smooth. Once we know that the tangent map/total derivative
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}:\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{T}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces for all
g\in\mathcal{G}|_{U}
, then we know by the inverse function theorem that
\Phi_{s}^{-1}
is smooth. Hence, we will now show that
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}
is injective, then it has to bijective by dimensional reasons (
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{G})=\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{P})
) so that we are done. Let us denote with
\Phi:\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
the right
\mathcal{G}
-action
(p,g)\mapsto p\cdot g
on
\mathcal{P}
, then

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}(g)

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(s_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)},g\right)=\bigl(\Phi\circ\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}s,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\bigr)(g,g).

Then
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}
is given by Thm.
5.3
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}(Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{s_{x}}r_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}s(\omega)\bigr)+\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{s_{x}\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{x}\left(r_{\sigma}\circ s\right)(\omega)+\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{s_{x}\cdot g}

for all
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
, where
x\coloneqq\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)
,
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G}|_{U})
10
10

10

W.l.o.g. we assume that
\sigma
is defined on
U
, otherwise ”make
U
smaller around
x
”.
with
\sigma_{x}=g
and
\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
. We want to decompose
\Phi_{s}
now. Observe that
Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
,
i.e.
it is vertical in
\mathcal{G}
due to the fact that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y)}_{=\omega}-\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\right)}_{=\omega}=0

(42)

because of

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{U}

such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\mathrm{D}\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{T}M|_{U}}.

(43)

Usually, we just make use of that without further mention; we repeat this trivial fact in order to emphasize that
\mathrm{D}\sigma
is injective because
\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{T}M|_{U}}
is bijective, and to emphasize that we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\right)\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)

\displaystyle=\{0\}

(the image of
\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma
intersects trivially with the kernel of
\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
). The injectivity of
\mathrm{D}\sigma
implies that the dimension of its image satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}\bigl(\mathrm{Im}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\right)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}(M),

so that, in total, we know by dimensional reasons

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Im}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\right)\oplus\mathrm{Ker}\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)=\mathrm{Im}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\right)\oplus\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}.

We can decompose
Y
accordingly by Eq. (
42
),

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)}_{\eqqcolon Y^{h}}+\underbrace{Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)}_{\eqqcolon Y^{v}}=Y^{h}+Y^{v},

v
stands for the vertical part and
h
for its complementary part (”horizontal”). In fact, as it is well-known, a section
\sigma
of a bundle induces a splitting of the short exact sequence of vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M|_{U},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}

and
\mathrm{D}\sigma
is a splitting/section of this sequence
so that we have
\sigma^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\cong\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{D}\sigma)\oplus\sigma^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.
11
11

11

\sigma
is actually an embedding, and thus
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{D}\sigma)
is a well-defined subbundle isomorphic to
\mathrm{T}M
.
Furthermore, again due to Eq. (
43
), we have
\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma)\cong\mathrm{T}_{x}M
as vector spaces by
Y^{h}\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(Y^{h}\right)=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y)=\omega
since
Y^{v}
is vertical. Hence, we can rewrite Eq. (
6.1.3
) as a map

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{x}M\oplus\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\left(\omega,\penalty\ Y^{v}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{x}\left(r_{\sigma}\circ s\right)(\omega)+\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y^{v}\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{s_{x}\cdot g}.

These arguments apply to any section of a bundle, hence, we repeat this now. Observe that
r_{\sigma}\circ s
is also a section of
\mathcal{P}|_{U}
due to Def.
3.1
, that is,

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\left(s_{y}\cdot\sigma_{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\left(s_{y}\right)=y

for all
y\in M
. As before, we split

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P}|_{U}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}(r_{\sigma}\circ s)\bigr)\oplus\mathrm{V}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

and we identify
\mathrm{T}_{x}M\cong\mathrm{Im}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}(r_{\sigma}\circ s)\bigr)
as vector spaces, now via
\omega\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{x}(r_{\sigma}\circ s)(\omega)
due to Eq. (
43
) but with
r_{\sigma}\circ s
playing the role as section. Additionally by Def.
5.2
and Remark
5.2
we know that fundamental vector fields are vertical, and so we can further rewrite Eq. (
6.1.3
) as a map

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{x}M\oplus\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}_{x}M\oplus\mathrm{V}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\left(\omega,\penalty\ Y^{v}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\omega,\penalty\ \left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y^{v}\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{s_{x}\cdot g}\right).

Thus,
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}
is the pair of two linear independent maps
\mathrm{T}_{x}M\to\mathrm{T}_{x}M
and
\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
. The former is clearly an isomorphism, and the latter is by definition of fundamental vector fields and the Maurer-Cartan form of the shape as in Eq. (
5.3
),
i.e.

\displaystyle Y^{v}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s_{x}}(Y^{v}).

By Remark
6.1.1
,
\Phi_{s_{x}}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism
\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{x}
, thence,
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s_{x}}:\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}_{x}=\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{T}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\mathrm{V}_{s_{x}\cdot g}\mathcal{P}_{x}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces. Finally we can conclude that
\mathrm{D}_{g}\Phi_{s}
is a linear isomorphism as the sum of two linear independent isomorphisms. As argued earlier, the inverse function theorem finishes now the proof.
∎

Usually, one likes to think about the choice of sections as a choice of a coordinate transformation as in
[
17
, §4.2, Remark 4.2.21, page 220]
. This is due to that the gauge theory usually corresponds to a formulation via a trivial LGB, which we will understand later, but already have an idea of by
e.g.
Ex.
3.2
and
6.1.2
. Then we have a local trivialization of
\mathcal{P}
such that one usually thinks of the choice of a section as a choice of coordinate system.

However, we now learned that on a more general scale this is not completely what is happening. The idea of LGBs and principal bundles are very similar; both are fibre bundles related to a Lie group and they carry an action which also restricts on each fibre. But the fibres of an LGB are Lie groups themselves, while the fibres of a principal bundle are ”just” diffeomorphic to a Lie group in an equivariant way as outlined in Remark
6.1.1
and as given in their definition Def.
6.1.1
. One could view the fibres of
\mathcal{P}
as having an ”almost” Lie group structure, a Lie group structure without a designated neutral element.

Lemma
6.1.3
shows that the choice of a section of
\mathcal{P}
is actually the choice of a designated neutral element, naturally inducing a Lie group structure in each fibre and thus an LGB structure, which may not be trivial. Aligning the more general definition of principal bundles with the definition of LGBs. For example set
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{G}=c_{G}(P)
as in Ex.
2.2
, where
P
is a non-trivial classical principal bundle and the underlying Lie group
G
is non-abelian; such an LGB is likely non-trivial but always carries a global section, for examples the neutral section
e
. We will see such an example later.

We want to use this now in order to study a certain pullback of principal bundles. For this we need to introduce general pullbacks of principal bundles; also recall again Cor.
3.1
.

Corollary 6.9:
Pullbacks of principal LGB-bundles are principal LGB-bundles

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
f:N\to M
a smooth map,
\mathcal{G}\to M
an LGB, and
\mathcal{P}\to M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then there is a unique (up to isomorphisms) principal
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-bundle structure on
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
, such that the projection
\pi_{2}:f^{*}\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
onto the second factor is a principal bundle morphism (over
f
) w.r.t. the projection
\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}:f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
onto the second factor as LGB morphism, and such that
\pi_{2}|_{x}:(f^{*}\mathcal{P})_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism w.r.t. the LGB isomorphism
\left.\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}\right|_{x}
.

Remarks 6.10
.

This was also stated in
[
11
, §5.7, second argument in Remark 5.34, page 145]
for an even more general type of principal bundle, but without proof.

Proof.

As in the previous statements about pullback structures, this is a rather trivial and canonical construction. We have a right
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-action on
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
defined by

\displaystyle(x,p)\cdot(x,g)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(x,p\cdot g)

for all
(x,p)\in f^{*}\mathcal{P}
and
(x,g)\in f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, that is,
x\in N
,
p\in\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}
. This is clearly an action
f^{*}\mathcal{P}*f^{*}\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\pi_{1}^{*}f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to f^{*}\mathcal{P}
, where
\pi_{1}
is the canonical projection of
f^{*}\mathcal{P}\to N
as fibre bundle. This action’s restriction onto the fibres is simply transitive: A fibre of
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
at
x
is
\{x\}\times\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}\cong\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}
, similarly
(f^{*}\mathcal{G})_{x}\cong\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}
. Hence, the restriction of that action at
x\in N
is the right
\mathcal{G}_{f(x)}
-action on
\mathcal{P}_{f(x)}
such that the action is simply transitive.

A principal bundle atlas can be constructed by a pullback of principal bundle charts of
\mathcal{P}
, that is, let
\left(U_{i}\right)_{i}
be an open covering of
M
over which we have
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\to\mathcal{G}|_{U_{i}}
. Then define

\displaystyle\left.f^{*}\mathcal{P}\right|_{f^{-1}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\to\left.f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right|_{f^{-1}(U_{i})},

\displaystyle(x,p)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(f^{*}\varphi_{i}\right)(x,p)\coloneqq\left(x,\left.\left(\varphi_{i}\right)\right|_{f(x)}(p)\right),

which is well-defined and by construction a base-preserving
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-equivariant smooth map, and this map is equivalent to
\left(\mathds{1}_{f^{-1}(U_{i})},\varphi_{i}\right):N\times\mathcal{P}\to N\times\mathcal{G}
restricted onto
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
as an embedded submanifold of
N\times\mathcal{P}
. Therefore it is clearly a diffeomorphism, so that we can conclude that
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
admits the structure as a principal
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-bundle.

The last part of the proof about the uniqueness of the structure is precisely as in the proof of Cor.
3.1
, just replace the property of being an LGB morphism with being a principal bundle morphism w.r.t. the LGB morphism
\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}
(essentially, replace homomorphism with equivariance). Keeping the same notation as in the proof of Cor.
3.1
, we get analogously

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}\circ f^{*}\varphi_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}.

Then start by making use of the point-wise behaviour of
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}
and proceed similarly as in the proof of Cor.
3.1
to conclude the proof.
∎

Definition 6.11:
Pullback principal bundle

Let
M,N
be smooth manifolds,
f:N\to M
a smooth map,
\mathcal{G}\to M
an LGB, and
\mathcal{P}\to M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then we call the principal
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
-bundle structure on
f^{*}\mathcal{P}
as given in Cor.
6.1.3
the
pullback principal bundle of
\mathcal{P}
(under
f
)
.

We will refer to this structure often without further mention.

We can actually show that
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
is not only
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
by definition but it is also isomorphic to
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}
. For this recall Ex.
6.1.2
,
i.e.
LGBs are ”trivially” also principal bundles, hence we know that
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
is a principal
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
-bundle.

Corollary 6.12:
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{P}
along its projection

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then we have a base-preserving principal bundle isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}

w.r.t. the LGB isomorphism given as the identity map on
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

We have a global section of the pullback principal bundle
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\pi^{*}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,p).

This is clearly a well-defined global section of
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}
, so that by Lemma
6.1.3
we achieve the desired isomorphism
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Remarks 6.13
.

By Lemma
6.1.3
this isomorphism is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\pi^{*}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,p)\cdot(p,g)=(p,p\cdot g).

In fact, the cited reference of Def.
6.1.1
,
[
11
, simplification of the beginning of §5.7, page 144f.]
, takes the existence of such a diffeomorphism as the essential sole part of defining principal bundles. Such an approach essentially avoids the second part of Def.
6.1.1
.

Remark 6.14:
Why ”principal”?

The last result and remark also outline why we are speaking of a
principal
bundle; this is similar to
[
17
, §4.2.2, page 212ff.]
. As in
[
17
, §3.7, Def. 3.7.24, page 159]
one could say that a
principal

\mathcal{G}
-action on a manifold
N
(recall Def.
3.1
) is a
free
action,
i.e.
orbit maps are injective, such that

\displaystyle N*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to N\times N,

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,p\cdot g)

is a closed map. In our case,
N=\mathcal{P}
, we clearly have a free action, and we just have shown that that map is closed, because we have the composition of maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,p\cdot g)\mapsto(p,p\cdot g).

By Remark
6.13
the first arrow is a diffeomorphism and thence a closed map. The second arrow is the inclusion, an embedding because
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}
is a closed embedded submanifold of
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
as the restriction of the fibre bundle
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\times\pi}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P}\times M
along the graph of
\pi
; see
e.g.

[
17
, §4.1, proof of Thm. 4.1.17, page 204ff.]
. Additionally, by the continuity of
\pi
, the graph
\Gamma
of
\pi
is closed and thus
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}=\left(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}}\times\pi\right)^{-1}(\Gamma)
, too. Using this, it is a quick exercise to show that a set closed in
\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}
is also closed in
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
, thus the second arrow as inclusion is also closed. Hence, the whole composition is closed.

This knowledge should allow us to carry over a lot of similar results related to principal actions, as in
[
17
, §3.7.5, page 159ff.]
and
[
17
, §4.2.2, page 212ff.]
. Essentially Remark
6.13
allows us to look at the quotient
\mathcal{P}\Big/\mathcal{G}
by using Godement’s Theorem as given in
[
17
, §3.7, Thm. 3.7.10, page 155]
, leading to a manifold structure on
\mathcal{P}\Big/\mathcal{G}
and maybe similarly leading to a principal bundle structure on
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}\Big/\mathcal{G}
.

Let us conclude our discussion about principal bundles by introducing a typical label.

Definition 6.15:
Gauges of a principal bundle

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\to M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. A
gauge
of
\mathcal{P}
is a section of
\mathcal{P}
. If the section is globally defined on
M
, then we speak of a
global gauge
, otherwise we may just say
local gauge
or just gauge.

By Lemma
6.1.3
, a gauge corresponds to a ”
\mathcal{G}
-ization” of
\mathcal{P}
, not necessarily a trivialization.

6.2
Generalized distributions and connections

Finally, let us now define the gauge theory, starting with horizontal distributions.
We expect a basic understanding of horizontal distributions and their relationship to what we call connections (on principal and vector bundles). The bare-bones start with horizontal distributions.

Definition 6.16:
Horizontal distribution,

[
17
, §5.1.2, Def. 5.1.6, page 260; without the symmetry along right-translations here]

Let
F\to M
be a fibre bundle over a smooth manifold
M
. Then a
horizontal distribution/bundle of
F
is a smooth subbundle
\mathrm{H}F
of
\mathrm{T}F
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}F\oplus\mathrm{V}F.

For
p\in M
the fibre is denoted by
\mathrm{H}_{p}F
, which we may call a
horizontal tangent space
.

As usual for gauge theory we will understand connections as horizontal distributions with a certain symmetry along the fibres in order to assure a certain behaviour of the gauge transformation of what physicists call minimal coupling; in mathematical words, in order to assure to be able to define a connection on associated vector bundles, and to assure a certain transformation of the associated curvature.
To do so it is useful if this symmetry is similar to the symmetry carried in the vertical structure; recall Def.
5.2
and its remark
5.2
. The following is a straightforward generalization of what one knows in the typical formulation of gauge theory, see
e.g.

[
17
, §5.1, part 2 to 4 of Prop. 5.1.3, page 258f]
.

Corollary 6.17:
The natural invariance of the vertical bundle of
\mathcal{P}

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
,
\mathcal{g}
its LAB, and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}

(44)

for all
(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
, and we have an isomorphism of vector bundles

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}

(45)

given by

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle(p,\nu)

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\nu}_{p}.

(46)

Remark 6.18:
Extending the notation of fundamental vector fields

For
\mu\coloneqq(p,\nu)
we may also write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{\nu}_{p},

which simplifies the notation in certain circumstances.

Proof of Cor.
6.2
.

Recall Rem.
6.1.1
for this proof. By definition it is clear that each fibre
\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
) is a principal
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-bundle over
\{x\}
whose
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-action is the
\mathcal{G}
-action restricted to
x
, and thus we know

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}_{x}\right)=\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}_{x}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
; see
e.g.

[
17
, §5.1, fourth part of Prop. 5.1.3, page 258f.]
for such statements about principal Lie group bundles. Due to that
\mathcal{P}_{x}
is a bundle over a point, we have
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}_{x}=\left.\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)=\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}.

Due to the fact that the
\mathcal{G}
-action is simply transitive we can derive that its induced
\mathcal{g}
-action
\rho
is a vector bundle isomorphism: By Rem.
5.2
this LAB action
\rho
is precisely (
46
) and
\rho
has values in
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
by Eq. (
24
). We know that the orbit maps
\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{x}
through
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
are
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms, so that
\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{g}_{x}\to\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
is a vector space isomorphism. But we also have

\displaystyle\rho(p,\nu)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}(\nu)

for all
\nu\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
, and therefore
\rho
is fibre-wise an isomorphism of vector spaces such that it is an isomorphism of vector bundles.
∎

By this result, we would like to have Eq. (
44
) also for a chosen horizontal distribution. However, its formulation leads to certain problems which we now want to discuss.

6.2.1
Idea and motivation

p
\cdot g
M
\mathcal{P}_{U}
(
x
)
U
\mathcal{P}
\pi
p\cdot g
g
\mathcal{G}
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
\mathcal{G}_{U}
(
x
)
U

For
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}\to M
an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle we fix a point
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and can multiply that with an element
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Infinitesimally, we are interested into how this multiplication by
g
affects tangent vectors, especially non-vertical ones.

However, as we have seen in Def.
3.1
, Rem.
4.2
and Thm.
5.3
(and its proof) the push-forward of horizontal vectors is not well-defined anymore on non-vertical vectors if one uses a fixed element of an LGB;
r_{g}
is just a map
\mathcal{P}_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{x}
. In order to study push-forwards of non-vertical vectors, we need information of the
\mathcal{G}
-action in an open neighbourhood
U
around the fibres over
x
. Hence we want to use a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G}|_{U})
with
\sigma_{x}=g
instead.

p
\cdot\sigma
M
\mathcal{P}_{U}
(
x
)
U
\mathcal{P}
\pi
p\cdot\sigma_{x}
\sigma
\mathcal{G}
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
\mathcal{G}_{U}
(
x
)
U

But there are in general a plethora of sections with
\sigma_{x}=g
, thenceforth one expects that a definition of connections based on that may depend on the choice of sections and thus leading to conflicts once one looks at push-forwards with all possible
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Especially the tangential behaviour of the section’s image (as an embedding of the base) may contribute to the push-forward; given a horizontal distribution, a horizontal vector may be still horizontal after a push-forward with one LGB section but not with respect to another LGB section. A similar problem may arise if we would work with local trivializations instead in order to use Ex.
3.2
. Thus, we need to adjust the typical definition of connections on principal bundles.

In order to understand what has to be changed, let us revisit the ”typical” situation, that is, let
\mathcal{P}
be a classical principal bundle as in Ex.
6.1.2
,
i.e.

\mathcal{G}=M\times G
is trivial with Lie group
G
. The
\mathcal{G}
-action is equivalent to a
G
-action on
\mathcal{P}
by Ex.
3.2
, a push-forward with
g\in G
w.r.t. the latter action is equivalent to the push-forward with a constant section in
\mathcal{G}
for which we may still simply write
g
.

Equip
\mathcal{P}
with a ”typical” (Ehresmann) connection as in
[
17
, §5.1, Def. 5.1.6, page 260]
, that is, a horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
of
\mathcal{P}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}_{p\cdot g}

(47)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
and
g\in G
as constant section. Recall that a connection has a 1:1 correspondence to a parallel transport, as presented in
[
17
, §5.8, page 286ff.]
. This means corresponding to a piece-wise smooth base curve
\alpha:[0,t]\to M
(
t>0
) with
\alpha(0)=x
we have a
parallel transport along
\alpha
as a map
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}_{x}\to\Gamma(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{P})
satisfying

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha*\alpha^{\prime}}\right|_{t}

\displaystyle=\left.\mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right|_{t}\circ\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}\right|_{t},

(48)

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha^{-}}^{\mathcal{P}}\right|_{t}

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}\right)^{-1}\right|_{t},

(49)

especially parallel transport is a diffeomorphism between the fibres, where
\alpha^{\prime}
is just another similarly defined base curve with
\alpha^{\prime}(0)=\alpha(t)
,
\alpha*\alpha^{\prime}
their concatenation (
\alpha
coming first and with a suitable parametrization such that
