\alpha*\alpha^{\prime}
is a map
[0,t]\to M
), and
\alpha^{-}
denotes
\alpha
traversed backwards. Moreover, Eq. (
47
) integrates and is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p\cdot g)\right|_{t}

\displaystyle=\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\right|_{t}\cdot g.

(50)

Thinking of the associated
\mathcal{G}
-action,
g
is an element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
such that the right hand side is in general not well-defined
12
12

12

Also here one could work with trivializations, especially since
\alpha^{*}\mathcal{P}
is trivial as a fibre bundle due to the fact that the image of
\alpha
is contractible. As before, this would just lead to other problems on a global scale, and we aim to provide a definition of connections on
\mathcal{P}
without making use of trivializations.
anymore due to the fact that
\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\right|_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha(t)}
; it is well-defined if interpreting
g
as a constant section of
\mathcal{G}=M\times G
, denoted now by
\widetilde{g}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
for bookkeeping reasons. The left hand side uses
\widetilde{g}_{\alpha(0)}=\widetilde{g}_{x}=(x,g)
, the right hand side
\widetilde{g}_{\alpha(t)}=(\alpha(t),g)
, and by Ex.
3.2
we rewrite the
G
-action to a
\mathcal{G}
-action:

\displaystyle p\cdot g

\displaystyle=p\cdot(x,g)=p\cdot\widetilde{g}_{x},

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot g

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot(\alpha,g)=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\alpha^{*}\widetilde{g},

where we recall that
\alpha^{*}\mathcal{P}
is a principal
\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}
-bundle in sense of Def.
6.1.3
.

Now we equip
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
with its
canonical flat connection

\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{T}M
which induces a parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\Gamma\left(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)
with
\left.\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}\right|_{t}(x,g)=(\alpha(t),g)
, especially
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\widetilde{g}_{x}\right)=\alpha^{*}\widetilde{g}
. In total, we can rewrite Eq. (
50
) to

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}\left(p\cdot\widetilde{g}_{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\alpha^{*}\widetilde{g}=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\widetilde{g}_{x}\right).

This opens a gateway to define Eq. (
50
) on general principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundles. That is, now let
\mathcal{P}
be again a general principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Fix any horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
inducing a parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}
, then a connection on
\mathcal{P}
should be equivalent to a parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathcal{P}
satisfying Eq. (
48
), (
49
) and

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)

(51)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
. The right hand side is now well-defined since both,
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)
and
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)
, are elements of
\Gamma(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{P})
and
\Gamma\left(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)
, respectively. We now want to derive its infinitesimal analogue similar to Eq. (
47
). Recall that we can view the parallel transports like
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)
also as a map
[0,t]\to\mathcal{P}
with
\pi\circ\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)=\alpha
(recall Subsection
1.3
; alternatively use Lemma
5.3
and project onto the second component for the following derivatives). Then by construction

\displaystyle Y\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{H}_{g}\mathcal{G}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, that is, it is horizontal in
\mathcal{G}
; similarly for the parallel transport on
\mathcal{P}
,

\displaystyle X\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}.

We differentiate both sides of Eq. (
51
) with
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t|_{t=0}
; the left hand side implies that the right hand side is an element of
\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
, while the right hand side gives

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\left(X,Y\right).

We can now already derive an infinitesimal definition of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
, that is, an Ehresmann connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
, given
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
, is precisely such a horizontal distribution such that
\mathrm{D}\Phi
restricts to the horizontal distributions:

\mathrm{D}\Phi|_{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}\mathbin{{}_{\mathrm{D}\pi}\times_{\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}\mathbin{{}_{\mathrm{D}\pi}\times_{\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P},

where
\Phi\colon\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{P}
denotes the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
;
one could say that “horizontal vectors are a closed algebra”.

We want to use Thm.
5.3
now in order to understand what Eq. (
51
) implies about the corresponding horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{P}
in sense of right-translations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\left(X,Y\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\Bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\bigl(\dot{\alpha}(0)\bigr)\Bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

where
\sigma
is any (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
with
\sigma_{x}=g
and

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}(0)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha=\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi(X)

because of
\pi\circ\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)=\alpha
(similarly w.r.t.
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}
so that it follows that
(X,Y)
is a tangent vector of
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
). As we already have shown several times, for example recall the beginning of the proof of Thm.
5.3
, we have

\displaystyle Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\bigl(\dot{\alpha}(0)\bigr)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G},

so that the canonical projection
\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
onto the vertical structure of
\mathcal{G}
acts as identity on it. By making use of the horizontality of
Y
, we can write

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\Bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\bigl(\dot{\alpha}(0)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{p}(\sigma\circ\pi)(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}(\sigma\circ\pi)(X)\bigr).

It may not surprise that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\mathcal{G}}
is by construction actually
the
connection 1-form on
\mathcal{G}
corresponding to
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
, therefore we will denote
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\mathcal{G}}
as the
total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
of
\mathcal{G}
. Hence we get in total (see also Figure
1
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}(\sigma\circ\pi)(X)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}

for all
X\in\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, and we would like to take this as the starting point of defining a connection on
\mathcal{P}
, denoting this by
\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X)
, the
modified right-pushforward
.

By construction,
\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X)
is independent of the chosen section
\sigma
because we have seen that it is equivalent to
\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi\left(X,Y\right)
, where
Y
is the unique element of
\mathrm{H}_{g}\mathcal{G}
lifting
\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi(X)
; and thence this gives a well-defined fundamental formula for the following parts of this paper. Last but not least, our starting point was Eq. (
44
); there is no contradiction between the approaches of Eq. (
44
) and (
6.2.1
): If
X
is a vertical vector, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi(X)

\displaystyle=0,

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X)

by Remark
4.2
. Thence, the symmetry behind Eq. (
6.2.1
) will be compatible with the one of Eq. (
44
) on the vertical bundle. Let us first study the second summand in Eq. (
6.2.1
) via the
Darboux derivative
.

\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
\cdot g
\mathcal{P}_{U}
X
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X)

\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\mathcal{P}
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
\cdot\sigma
\mathcal{P}_{U}
X
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)
\thicksim\mathrm{D}_{p}(\sigma\circ\pi)(X)

Figure 1:
Push-forward of a horizontal tangent vector
X
with constant section (left) and general section (right), where
\mathcal{P}
is a classical principal bundle as in Ex.
6.1.2
equipped with a ”typical” connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
of principal
G
-bundles (
G
the structural Lie group).

6.2.2
Darboux derivative on LGBs

By Cor.
6.2
we know that
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{g}
. Since connections are projections onto the vertical bundle we are interested into the pullback situation, and therefore we will put some remarks after some definitions in the following in order to discuss the situation of pullback LGBs. Hence let us start with a general remark about the pullback situation to which we will later refer in the other remarks regarding this situation.

Remark 6.19:
Pullback LGBs and their connections

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
, where we denote with
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
the corresponding (base-preserving) projection onto its vertical bundle; we will view
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}
as an element of
\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G};\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G})
. Furthermore, let
f:N\to M
be a smooth map defined on another smooth manifold
N
. By Lemma
5.3
we know that
\mathrm{T}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})
consists of pairs of tangent vectors
(X,Y)\in\mathrm{T}_{(p,g)}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})
(
(p,g)\in f^{*}\mathcal{G}
) with
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N
,
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}f(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y).

In the following we denote with
\mathrm{pr}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) the projections onto the
i
-th component of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
.

The projection of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}\to N
is
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
, thus
\mathrm{Dpr}_{1}(X,Y)=X
. The vertical bundle
\mathrm{V}(f^{*}\mathcal{G})
as the kernel of
\mathrm{Dpr}_{1}
then consists of pairs
(X=0,Y)
, and therefore
\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y)=0
which implies
Y\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
. Thence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{V}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\left(\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\right).

It is then trivial to check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}

\displaystyle\in\Omega^{1}\bigl(f^{*}\mathcal{G};\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\left(\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\right)\bigr)\cong\Omega^{1}\bigl(f^{*}\mathcal{G};\mathrm{V}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)\bigr)

is a projection onto
\mathrm{V}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)
, and gives therefore rise to a horizontal distribution on
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, the
pullback connection on
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
. Especially we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)_{(p,g)}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left(0,\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}_{g}(Y)\right),

and thus

\displaystyle\left.\left(\left(\mathrm{Dpr}_{1},\mathrm{Dpr}_{2}\right)\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\right|_{(p,g)}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left(0,\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}_{g}(Y)\right)=\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)_{(p,g)}(X,Y).

As we have seen, we need a slight adjustment of the vertical Maurer-Cartan as presented in Def.
4.3
.

Definition 6.20:
Total Maurer-Cartan form

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
, where we denote with
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
the corresponding projection onto its vertical bundle. Then we define the
total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\in\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{G};\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)
of
\mathcal{G}
as the connection 1-form corresponding to
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\right|_{g}(Y)=\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}L_{g^{-1}}\right)\left(\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}(Y)\right)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
.

This is clearly well-defined by construction; also recall our discussion of the vertical Maurer-Cartan form, especially Cor.
4.3
.

Remark 6.21:
Pullback situation: Part I

Given the situation as in Remark
6.2.2
, then we have

\displaystyle L_{(p,g)}\bigl((p,q)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(p,L_{g}(q)\bigr)

for all
(p,g),(p,q)\in f^{*}\mathcal{G}
, where the left-translation on the left and right hand side are the ones of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}
, respectively. Thence,

\displaystyle L_{(p,g)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{pr}_{1},L_{\mathrm{pr_{2}}(p,g)}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right),

and hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}L_{(p,g)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)}L_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(p,g^{-1}\right)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\right|_{\mathrm{V}_{(p,g)}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left.\left(0,\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\right|_{\mathrm{V}_{(p,g)}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)},

making use of that

L_{(p,g)}:\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}\to\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}
so that
\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}L_{(p,g)^{-1}}:\mathrm{V}_{(p,g)}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)\to\mathrm{V}_{(p,e_{x})}\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)=\left(f^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)_{p}
, where
x\coloneqq f(p)
and
e_{x}
is the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
Altogether we get

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{(p,g)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}L_{(p,g)^{-1}}\circ\left.\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right|_{(p,g)}

\displaystyle=\left(0,\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\circ\left.\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right|_{(p,g)}

\displaystyle=\left(0,\left.\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\right|_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(0,\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\right|_{(p,g)}.

Remark 6.22:
Total Maurer-Cartan form just typical form on trivial LGBs

Let
G
be a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq M\times G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{pr}_{1}}}{{\to}}M
be the trivial LGB equipped with its canonical flat connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathrm{T}M
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
is the projection onto the first component in
M\times G
. Its LAB
\mathcal{g}
is also a trivial bundle,
M\times\mathfrak{g}
, and we have several identities (recall Cor.
4.3
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathrm{T}M\oplus\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathrm{T}G\cong\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathrm{T}M\oplus\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathrm{T}M\oplus\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{g}=\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}\oplus\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:M\times G\to G
is the projection onto the second component,
and the projection onto the vertical bundle is then equivalent to
\mathrm{Dpr}_{2}\in\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{G};\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathrm{T}G\right)
.

Now let us view
G
as an LGB over a point
\{*\}
, then
\mathcal{G}=f^{*}G
, where
f:M\to\{*\}
. Making use of the uniqueness of
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{T}G}
for
G\to\{*\}
, we have for the pullback connection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}=\mathrm{Dpr}_{2}
which is precisely the projection for
\mathcal{G}
, and therefore we can use Remark
6.2.2
to derive

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathrm{tot}}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\mu_{G},

where
\mu_{G}
is the Maurer-Cartan form of
G
.

The Maurer-Cartan form is important for gauge transformations because it induces a derivative, which we also need now.

Remark 6.23:
Maurer-Cartan form inducing a natural derivative: Part I

If
G
is a Lie group and
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra, then there is actually some stance that the typical Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{G}\in\Omega^{1}(G;\mathfrak{g})
describes
the
generalization of the total derivative of smooth maps
M\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

(n\in\mathbb{N})
, given by the
Darboux derivative
\Delta
as given in
[
10
, §5.1, page 182ff.]
. For a smooth section
\sigma:M\to G
of the trivial LGB over
M
, this is
\Delta\sigma\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathfrak{g})
given by

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sigma^{!}\mu_{G},

that is,

\displaystyle(\Delta\sigma)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\sigma_{x}}L_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(X)\bigr)

for all
p\in M
and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
. For
G=\mathbb{R}^{n}
one usually shows that
\mathrm{D}\sigma
is the actual total derivative (Jacobian) by making use of
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
; however, if one views the triviality of
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the trivialization of general
\mathrm{T}G
as given by
G\times\mathfrak{g}
, then it is more natural to think of the total derivative as
\mathrm{D}\sigma
followed by
\mathrm{D}_{\sigma_{x}}L_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}
in order to receive information about the essential Lie algebra element. Then the classical total derivative of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued maps is actually more naturally given by the Darboux derivative.

We now have a similar behaviour in our case but related to arbitrary connections on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(as a trivial bundle over
M
).

Definition 6.24:
Generalised Darboux derivative

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
.
For
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
we define the
Darboux derivative
\Delta\sigma\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}=\left(\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\circ\mathrm{D}\sigma.

We may also write
\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}
instead of
\Delta
in order to accentuate the LGB.

Remarks 6.25
.

The notation with the pullback
\sigma^{*}
is only needed if one wants to view
\Delta\sigma
as a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear map
\mathfrak{X}(M)\to\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
; in this case it is a composition of base-preserving vector bundle morphisms
\mathrm{D}\sigma:\mathrm{T}M\to\sigma^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
and
\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}:\sigma^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\sigma^{*}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong\mathcal{g}
which can be extended to sections, where
\pi
is the projection of
\mathcal{G}
.

Of course one can view
\Delta\sigma
as a map
\mathrm{T}M\to\mathcal{g}
which one can also write as the composition of
\mathrm{D}\sigma:\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{g}
, that is,

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\circ\mathrm{D}\sigma.

If one wants to emphasize base points of the involved pullback bundles, that is, one views
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
as a map
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}
, then one can write point-wise

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}}\circ\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma

\displaystyle=\bigl(\sigma_{x},\left.\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{x}\bigr)

for all
x\in M
.

As a derivative we expect a Leibniz rule as for the classical Darboux derivative (see
[
10
, §5.1, Eq. 2, page 182]
). However, we cannot make a general statement about that yet, as long as we do not fix a more specific type of connection on
\mathcal{G}
. We will come back to this later; at this point just be sure of that there is of course a certain Leibniz rule, simply due to that it generalizes the ”classical” Darboux derivative, as we are going to see.

Let us now discuss the pullback situation for the Darboux derivative.

Remark 6.26:
Maurer-Cartan form inducing a natural derivative: Part II

We have now something similar to Remark
6.2.2
. Let
\mathcal{G}=M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) be the trivial abelian LGB, and
\nabla
a vector bundle connection on
M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for which we have the associated projection onto the vertical bundle
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
. For
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
we then have

\displaystyle\nabla_{X_{x}}\sigma

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma^{*}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\left(X_{x}\right)\bigr)

(54)

for all
x\in M
and
X_{x}\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
, by making use of
\sigma^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}\cong M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
as vector bundles such that the right hand side has again values in
\mathcal{G}
due to ”enough triviality” and equals the left hand side.

Alternatively, one could use the isomorphism as given in Cor.
4.3
.
This is also more natural in the sense of that one wants that
\nabla_{X_{x}}\sigma
is a section over
M
;
M
can be viewed as the image of the neutral section
e
(the zero vector here), so that
\nabla_{X_{x}}\sigma
should have values in
e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{g}
. Henceforth one could say it is more natural to ”pull
\left(\sigma^{*}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\left(X_{x}\right)\bigr)
back” to
\mathcal{g}
by left-translation in order to define
\nabla_{X_{x}}\sigma
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\sigma_{x}}L_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}\Bigl(\left(\sigma^{*}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\left(X_{x}\right)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\sigma^{*}\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)\right|_{x}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma\left(X_{x}\right)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.(\Delta\sigma)\right|_{x}(X_{x}).

Thus, as in Remark
6.2.2
one may say that
\Delta\sigma
is
the
generalization of vector bundle connections to general LGBs
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, this argument is not based on a given (local) trivialization to handle the arising pullback in Eq. (
54
).

Remark 6.27:
Pullback situation: Part II

Following Remark
6.2.2
and its notation and results (see Remark
6.2.2
for the initial setup), we can calculate
\Delta^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}\left(f^{*}\sigma\right)\in\Omega^{1}(N;f^{*}\mathcal{g})
for
\sigma\in\mathcal{G}
, first we write

\displaystyle\left.f^{*}\sigma\right|_{p}

\displaystyle=\left(p,\sigma_{f(p)}\right)

for all
p\in N
, and thus

\displaystyle\Delta^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}\left(f^{*}\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f^{*}\sigma\right)^{!}\mu_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=\left(f^{*}\sigma\right)^{!}\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ f^{*}\sigma\right)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma\circ f\right)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\sigma\circ f\right)^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\circ\mathrm{D}(\sigma\circ f)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(f^{*}\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\circ f^{*}\left(\mathrm{D}\sigma\right)}_{=f^{*}\left(\left(\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\circ\mathrm{D}\sigma\right)}\circ\penalty\ \mathrm{D}f

\displaystyle=f^{!}\left(\left(\sigma^{*}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\circ\mathrm{D}\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=f^{!}\left(\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\sigma\right),

(55)

where we rewrote the chain rule

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}(\sigma\circ f)

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(\mathrm{D}\sigma\right)\circ\mathrm{D}f,

but as in Remark
6.25
one can decide to omit this notation. Such Darboux derivatives related to the pullback connection on
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
we call the
pullback Darboux derivative
, and similar to the notation of pullback vector bundle connections we write

\displaystyle f^{*}\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Delta^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}.

Remark 6.28:
Canonical flat Darboux derivative

Given the situation as in Remark
6.2.2
we can use Remark
6.2.2
to derive

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=f^{*}\Delta^{0},

where
\Delta^{0}
on the right hand side is the Darboux derivative of the Lie group
G
as a bundle over point. Trivially,
\Delta^{0}g\equiv 0
for all
g\in G
, and thus

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}(f^{*}g)

\displaystyle=f^{!}\left(\Delta^{0}g\right)=0

for all
g\in G
viewed as a section of
G\to\{0\}
, so
f^{*}g
are the constant sections of
\mathcal{G}
. In sense of Remark
6.2.2
it makes sense to say that
\sigma
is parallel w.r.t.
\Delta
. Since constant sections generate all sections of
\mathcal{G}
, we then speak of the
canonical flat Darboux derivative
, and constant sections are its parallel sections.

However, just because
\Delta^{0}
is a zero map, does not mean that also
\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}
is zero. On one hand because of a Leibniz rule which we will discuss later in more detail, and on the other hand, as mentioned in Subsection
1.3
, we can view general sections
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
(not necessarily constant) equivalently as a smooth map
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma:M\to G
, denoted by
\widetilde{\sigma}
for bookkeeping reasons. By Remark
6.2.2
we then get

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma^{!}\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\mu_{G}=\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\right)^{!}\mu_{G}=\Delta^{G}\widetilde{\sigma}

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, where
\Delta^{G}
is the ”classical” Darboux derivative as in Remark
6.2.2
related to
\mu_{G}
. This emphasizes why we can speak of a canonical flat derivative due to the fact that
\mu_{G}
is flat (Maurer-Cartan equation), and we may simply write
\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}=\Delta^{G}
.

We can actually rewrite some important equations now; recall the notation introduced in Remark
6.2
.

Remark 6.29:
Darboux derivative in the infinitesimal LGB action

Recall Thm.
5.3
; keeping the same notation as in this theorem but denoting the projection of
\mathcal{P}
by
\pi
, we checked several times that
Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)
is vertical, and thus we can now write

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)_{g}\bigl(Y-\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)_{g}(Y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(\omega)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.(\Delta\sigma)\right|_{x}(\omega)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

If
\mathcal{G}
is a trivial LGB, then this emphasizes again that we recover the typical Leibniz rule by choosing a constant section
\sigma
, using Remarks
6.2.2
and
6.2.2
.

Remark 6.30:
Idea behind the notion of connection on principal bundles using the Darboux derivative

Recall the notation and discussion around the terms in Eq. (
6.2.1
) which will be important for our definition of a connection on principal LGB-bundles. The terms in Eq. (
6.2.1
) can be similarly rewritten as in

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}(\sigma\circ\pi)(X)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)_{x}\circ\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\bigr)(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}.

We will use this form for the definition of the principal bundle connection. As in Remark
6.2.2
, if
\mathcal{G}
is trivial and
\sigma
a constant section corresponding to a Lie group element
g
, then these terms are just

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X),

making use of Ex.
3.2
.

In fact, the Darboux derivative naturally induces a connection on
\mathcal{g}
as LAB of
\mathcal{G}
. One may have expected that since
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
should infinitesimally induce a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{g}
; recall the exponential map introduced in Subsection
4.4
. Also recall that by definition of vertical bundles we know for the vertical bundle of the LAB
\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{g}}}}{{\to}}M
that
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{g}}^{*}\mathcal{g}
, making use of that LABs are vector bundles; in the following we will use the natural projection onto the second component of
\pi_{\mathcal{g}}^{*}\mathcal{g}
but now defined on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}
, denoted by
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
.

Proposition 6.31:
LGB connection induces LAB connection

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
. Then the map
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}:\Gamma(\mathcal{g})\to\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
,
\nu\mapsto\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu
denoted as an element of
\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
by
X\mapsto\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu
, defined by

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)\Bigr)\right)

for all
x\in M
,
X\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
, is a well-defined vector bundle connection on
\mathcal{g}
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
is the projection onto the second component of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}
naturally viewed as pullback bundle.

Remarks 6.32
.

The notation of
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
is usually omitted in such constructions due to the fact that

\displaystyle t\mapsto\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)

is a curve with values in
\mathcal{g}_{x}
, a vector space, so that one canonically uses the identification of tangent spaces of vector spaces with itself to show that
\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
. We will keep
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
for the proof for the sake of rigorousness, but we will drop it after this proposition for simplicity without further mention.

In order to prove this we need to apply Schwarz’s Theorem in order to switch
\Delta
with
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t
. To do this rigorously we need to introduce the canonical involution/flip on double tangent bundles; since this does not completely fit into this paper’s subject and may be already known by the reader, you can learn about the double tangent bundle and its flip map in Appendix
A
if needed.

Proof of Prop.
6.2.2
.

Let us begin with well-definedness. Similar to Diagram (
A.1
) we have the double vector bundle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}

(56)

We also have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\circ\mathrm{D}_{x}\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)(X)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}(X)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\Bigl(S_{\mathcal{G}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}\nu(X)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)

(57)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{T}_{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)\right)}\mathcal{g}

for all
x\in M
and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
, where
e
is the neutral section of
\mathcal{G}
, and we viewed
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
as a map
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{g}
when applying the chain rule; recall Remark
6.25
.
S_{\mathcal{G}}
is the linear canonical flip map on
\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}
, especially see the last part of Remark
A
. We also introduced the notation
\nu_{T}
similar to Remark
A
for simplicity; in fact
\nu_{T}
is a vector field on
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
only defined over
\mathrm{T}M
which is canonically embedded into
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
by embedding
M
into
\mathcal{G}
via
e
. We also naturally embed
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{g}
into
\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}
. In total, we will work with these embeddings now so that everything is embedded into
\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}
as the ”total space”; hence, you will also see
e
and its total derivative
\mathrm{D}e
acting as an embedding several times.

We get by Eq. (
56
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}\left(\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\underbrace{\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}\circ\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)}_{=e\circ\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}e\circ\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\left(\nu_{x}\right)

\displaystyle=0\in\mathrm{T}_{e_{x}}\mathcal{G}

viewing
S_{\mathcal{G}}
as a base-preserving isomorphism from
\pi_{\mathrm{TT}G}:\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
to
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
, and using that
\mathcal{g}=e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
.
The projection of
\mathcal{g}\to M
is canonically the restriction of
\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}
, hence we can conclude that

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{V}_{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)\right)}\mathcal{g},

and so we can derive that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
is defined on this; in total
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu
is well-defined. By Eq. (
6.2.2
) we also trivially know that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)\bigr)\in\mathcal{g}_{x}.

Viewing
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}
naturally as the pullback of
\mathcal{g}
along its projection we can therefore write

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Eq.\ \eqref{EqForNablaConnectionWithFlip}}}}{{=}}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}(X)\Bigr)=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)\bigr),\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}\right),

so that smoothness of
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu
follows.
In order to understand whether
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is a vector bundle connection we are hence interested into the restriction of Diagram (
56
) onto
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{TT}\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}}

where
M
is canonically embedded into
\mathcal{G}
by
e
, and
\widetilde{M}
is the further canonical embedding of this (the image of
e
) into
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
by the zero section. As in Appendix
A
, the addition of vectors and the scalar multiplication of the left vertical arrow is denoted as usual, while the one of the upper horizontal arrow will be denoted by
\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}
and
\boldsymbol{\cdot}
, respectively. For
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
we now have

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\lambda X+\kappa Y}\nu\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\lambda X+\kappa Y)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(\lambda X+\kappa Y)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(\lambda X+\kappa Y)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nu_{T}(X)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\nu_{T}(Y)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(Y)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(Y)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)\Bigr)+\kappa\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(Y)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(Y)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\penalty\ \left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}+\kappa\penalty\ \left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{Y}\nu\right|_{x}

for all
\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, using Remark
A
,
A
and
A
. By these remarks we also derive for another section
\mu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}(\lambda\nu+\kappa\mu)\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\underbrace{(\lambda\nu+\kappa\mu)_{T}}_{\mathclap{=\lambda\nu_{T}+\kappa\mu_{T}}}(X)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)\Bigr)+\kappa\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\mu_{T}(X)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}+\kappa\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\mu\right|_{x},

and

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}(f\nu)\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl((f\nu)_{T}(X)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(f(x)\penalty\ \nu_{T}(X)+X(f)\penalty\ \nu^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=f(x)\penalty\ \left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu\right|_{x}+X(f)\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\nu^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)\right)

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, where
\left(\xi^{a}\right)_{a}
are fibre coordinates of
\mathcal{g}
.
It was well-defined to use the linearity of
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
, since we know by what we have shown earlier that both,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl((f\nu)_{T}(X)\bigr)
and
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(f\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)=f\penalty\ \mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\bigl(\nu_{T}(X)\bigr)
, are elements of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\nu^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)

is vertical, too.

Making use of the aforementioned isomorphism
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\cong\pi^{*}_{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{g}
, we can write similar as in Remark
A

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X),e_{a}\right),

where
\left(e_{a}\right)_{a}
is a local frame of
\mathcal{g}
dual to
\xi^{a}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)

\displaystyle=e_{a}.

By definition,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
acts as identity on
\mathcal{g}=e^{*}\mathrm{V}\mathcal{G}
, and so
\mathrm{D}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is the identity on
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{g}
. Thus, we derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\nu^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\nu^{a}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right|_{\mathrm{D}_{x}e(X)}\right)=\nu^{a}e_{a}=\nu,

thus, finally,

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}(f\nu)\right|_{x}

\displaystyle=f(x)\penalty\ \left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}(f\nu)\right|_{x}+X(f)\penalty\ \nu.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Definition 6.33:
LGB connection on its LAB

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we call the vector bundle connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
on
\mathcal{g}
of Prop.
6.2.2
, shortly denoted by

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
, the
\mathcal{G}
-connection (on the LAB
\mathcal{g}
)
.

Remarks 6.34
.

Such a construction of vector bundle connections on LABs also arises in
[
12
, §4.5, Prop. 4.22]
, but w.r.t. a more specific
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
, see Subsubsection
6.4.1
later; in this context that reference also shows that the parallel transport associated to
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is the infinitesimal version of the parallel transport associated with
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
.

Example 6.35:
Canonical flat
\mathcal{G}
-connection

If we again focus on trivial LGBs with their canonical flat connection as in Remark
6.2.2
and
6.2.2
, we can quickly derive that
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is then the canonical flat connection on the trivial LAB
\mathcal{g}=M\times\mathfrak{g}
of
\mathcal{G}=M\times G
,
\mathfrak{g}
the Lie algebra of the Lie group
G
. Let
\nu
be a constant section of
\mathcal{g}
, then
\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}
is a constant section of
\mathcal{G}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, so that by Remark
6.2.2

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}

\displaystyle=0,

and thus

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu

\displaystyle=0

for all constant sections
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. By the uniqueness of the canonical flat connection (w.r.t. a trivialisation) we conclude that
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is the canonical flat connection on
\mathcal{g}=M\times\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 6.36:
Pullback situation: Part III

Following Remark
6.2.2
(see Remark
6.2.2
for the initial setup) and all the involved notation we can again derive something related to the pullback LGB
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
. First of all, let us denote the exponential of
f^{*}\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathrm{e\;\!}_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}
, respectively, then we clearly have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e\;\!}_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{(p,\nu_{x})}

\displaystyle=\exp_{\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}}(p,\nu_{x})=\left(p,\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}(\nu_{x})\right)=\left(p,\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\nu_{x}}\right)

for all
(p,\nu_{x})\in\left(f^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)_{p}
, where
x\coloneqq f(p)
and so
\nu_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
, and where we made use of that
\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}\cong\mathcal{G}_{x}
as Lie groups via the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
onto the second component (recall Cor.
3.1
), that is, we have used the well-known relation

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\exp_{\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}}(p,\nu_{x})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\circ\penalty\ \mathrm{D}_{(p,e_{x})}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)(p,\nu_{x})=\exp_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}(\nu_{x}),

see
e.g.

[
17
, §1.7, Thm. 1.7.16, page 59]
for the general formula; then use that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}|_{\left(f^{*}\mathcal{G}\right)_{p}}
is bijective. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{e\;\!}_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{f^{*}\nu}

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\nu}\right)

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. Due to clatch of notation we relabel the projection
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
in Prop.
6.2.2
to
\pi_{2}
, and then observe, by using Eq. (
6.2.2
),

\displaystyle\left.\nabla^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}_{Y}(f^{*}\nu)\right|_{p}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}_{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{t\cdot f^{*}\nu}\right)_{p}(Y)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\Delta^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}\bigl(f^{*}\left(\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{t\nu}\right)\bigr)\right)_{p}(Y)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(f^{!}\left(\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{t\nu}\right)\right)_{p}(Y)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\biggl(p,\left(\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{t\nu}\right)_{f(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}f(Y)\bigr)\biggr)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(0,\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\biggl(\left(\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{t\nu}\right)_{f(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}f(Y)\bigr)\biggr)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\biggl(\left(\Delta^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{e\;\!}_{\mathcal{G}}^{t\nu}\right)_{f(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}f(Y)\bigr)\biggr)\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{D}_{p}f(Y)}\nu\right|_{f(p)}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
,
p\in N
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{p}N
. By the uniqueness of pullback vector bundle connections we therefore get

\displaystyle\nabla^{f^{*}\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=f^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}.

6.2.3
First step towards towards associated bundles

In order to provide a concise definition of connection 1-forms on principal bundles, we will need to introduce some canonical form of action which will be obviously related to the action needed for an analogue to the notion of associated bundles related to typical principal bundles. However, we will neither discuss nor introduce a more general notion of associated bundles in this paper; so, there will be just the ”first step”. The following action can be seen as another canonical action on the pullback of a principal bundle, but coming from
\mathcal{G}
itself instead of its pullback, if
\mathcal{G}
acts on the manifold one makes a pullback to.

Proposition 6.37:
Canonical LGB action on pullback bundles over principal LGB-bundles

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Furthermore let
N
be another smooth manifold and
f:N\to M
a smooth map on which
\mathcal{G}
acts on the left as in Def.
3.1
. Then on the pullback manifold
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\coloneqq f^{*}\mathcal{P}
, whose pairs of points are now reordered as in

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{(p,x)\in\mathcal{P}\times N\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \pi(p)=f(x)\right\},

we have a right
\mathcal{G}
-action given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\right)*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N,

\displaystyle(p,x,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,x)\cdot g\coloneqq\left(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot x\right)

where
\left(\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\right)*\mathcal{G}
is given as pullback of
\mathcal{G}
w.r.t. the map
\widetilde{\pi}:\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\to M
,
(p,x)\mapsto\pi(p)
.

Proof.

By Def.
3.1
it is clear that this action is well-defined, due to

\displaystyle\pi(p\cdot g)=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)=f\left(g^{-1}\cdot x\right)

for all
(p,x,g)\in\left(\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\right)*\mathcal{G}
,
and this also implies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}\bigl((p,x)\cdot g\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi(p\cdot g)=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g),

so that Eq. (
13
) is satisfied. By construction we also have a smooth action, since it is the composition of maps

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N\right)*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to(\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G})\times(\mathcal{G}*N)\to\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N,

\displaystyle(p,x,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl((p,g),(g,x)\bigr)\mapsto\left(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot x\right).

The first arrow is clearly an embedding, and the second arrow is just the restriction of the smooth diagonal action,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G})\times(\mathcal{G}*N)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}\times N,

\displaystyle\bigl((p,g),(q,p)\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(p\cdot g,q^{-1}\cdot p\right),

onto an embedded submanifold, and for its image make use of that
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}N
is an embedded submanifold of
\mathcal{P}\times N
, as we already did several times for pullback bundles.

Associativity follows simply by

\displaystyle\bigl((p,x)\cdot g\bigr)\cdot q

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot x\right)\cdot q=\left(p\cdot gq,(gq)^{-1}\cdot x\right)=\left(p,x\right)\cdot(gq)

for all
g,q\in\mathcal{G}_{y}
(
y\coloneqq\pi(p)=f(x)
); it is trivial to check that
\left(p,x\right)\cdot e_{y}=(p,x)
.
∎

As mentioned in Remark
6.1.3
, constructions of quotients related to the LGB action may be possible here. Thus, using the last proposition, one should be able to construct associated bundles in this more general setting. We mainly need this proposition for the following examples.

Example 6.38:
Adjoint action on the vertical bundle of
\mathcal{P}

Recall the adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}
on its LAB
\mathcal{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{g}}}}{{\to}}M
, Ex.
5.1
. Using the notation of Prop.
6.2.3
we have a right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{g}=\{(p,v)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{g}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \pi(p)=\pi_{\mathcal{g}}(v)\}
given by

\displaystyle(p,v)\cdot g

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(p\cdot g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}(v)\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
),
v\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Observe that
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{g}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
by Cor.
6.2
. We will denote this action shortly by

\displaystyle\sAd_{g^{-1}}(p,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(p,v)\cdot g,

the
adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
; not to be confused with the adjoint representation of
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
on
\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
. In fact, it is trivial to check that
\sAd:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
,
g\mapsto\sAd_{g}
, is a
\mathcal{G}
-representation on
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{g}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
in the sense of Cor.
5.1
.

The quotient of
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{g}
w.r.t. this group action should lead to a structure which is the generalization of the adjoint bundle in typical formulations of gauge theory.

Similarly, we can define the conjugation action on its integrated bundle; recall the conjugation defined in Def.
4.2
. Especially observe that the conjugation defines a left
\mathcal{G}
-action on itself by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle(g,q)

\displaystyle\mapsto c_{g}(q)=gqg^{-1}.

Example 6.39:
Conjugation action over
\mathcal{P}

Let us look at
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{G}\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}
. Then we have a right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{G}
given by

\displaystyle(p,q)\cdot g

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(p\cdot g,c_{g^{-1}}(q)\right)=\left(p\cdot g,g^{-1}qg\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
), and
q,g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
We will denote this also by

\displaystyle\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}(p,q)\coloneqq(p,q)\cdot g,

the
conjugation of
\mathcal{G}
on the integral of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
.

Taking a quotient of
\mathcal{P}\times_{M}\mathcal{G}
over this action should lead to a generalization of inner group bundles as introduced in Ex.
2.2
.

Let us finally define connections on principal bundles.

6.2.4
Generalized connection 1-forms on principal bundles

As also stated in
[
17
, §5.1, Prop. 5.1.5, page 260]
, for a given horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
of a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
over a smooth manifold
M
we have by construction that

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{T}_{x}M

is a vector space isomorphism for all
x\in M
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
; similarly for
\mathcal{G}
itself. Using that, one has some sort of identification between the horizontal tangent spaces; we want to provide another identification in the sense of Eq. (
6.2.1
), also recall Rem.
6.2.2
; especially recall the Darboux derivative introduced in Subsubsection
6.2.2
. The following proposition and definition will be needed.

Proposition 6.40:
The right-pushforward modified by the Darboux derivative is a well-defined isomorphism

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and we have horizontal a distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, for
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) define the map

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}

\displaystyle\to\left.\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}},

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g},

where
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
,
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, and
\sigma
is any (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
with
\sigma_{x}=g
. Then
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is independent of the choice of the local section
\sigma
, and it is a vector bundle automorphism over the right-translation
r_{g}
.

Furthermore, if we also have a horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{P}
, then
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
is isomorphic via
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
to a complement of
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
in
\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
(this complement is not necessarily
\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
).

Proof.

In the following we have
(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
,
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, and
\sigma
is any (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
with
\sigma_{x}=g
(
x\coloneqq\pi(p)
).

\bullet
Let
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
be the projection of
\mathcal{G}
, then as mentioned before Prop.
6.2.4
there is a unique
Y\in\mathrm{H}_{g}\mathcal{G}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi(X)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M.

By Remark
6.2.2
we can derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{(p,g)}\Phi(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X),

where we made use of that
Y
is horizontal in
\mathcal{G}
so that
\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y)=0
, and where
\Phi
denotes the right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
as a map
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{P}
. Therefore the independence of
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
w.r.t. the choice of
\sigma
follows.

\bullet

\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is clearly linear by construction. Let us now show fibre-wise that
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is injective, then it is also bijective by dimensional reasons. By Remark
3.11
we know that
r_{\sigma}
is a diffeomorphism, and thus
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}:\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
is a vector space isomorphism. Restricted onto the vertical subspace
\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
we have by Remark
4.2

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}:\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

which is a vector space isomorphism by Cor.
6.2
and dimensional reasons (which implies that
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}
is surjective and thus bijective). We rewrite
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\left(X^{\mathrm{H}},\penalty\ X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right)+\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(X^{\mathrm{V}}\right),

where we fix just any horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
.
By dimensional reasons and due to the bijectivity of
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}
and
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}
the image
\mathrm{Im}\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\right)
of
\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
has to be a complement subspace of
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
in
\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
;
\mathrm{Im}\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\right)
is not necessarily equal to
\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
in general but of the same dimension, especially of the same dimension as
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. Thus,
\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
is a vector space isomorphism onto its image which is complementary to
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, observe

\displaystyle\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{x}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}+X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)\right)=\left.\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{x}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right)\right)=\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right),

using that the vertical subbundle is given by the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\pi
, and
where we split again
X=X^{\mathrm{H}}+X^{\mathrm{V}}
for all
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. Hence, we get in total that
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
at
p
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Im}\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\right)\oplus\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\left(X^{\mathrm{H}},\penalty\ X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right),\penalty\ \mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}\right).

Let
\left(X^{\mathrm{H}},\penalty\ X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)
be now in the kernel of
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
, then
X^{\mathrm{H}}=0
by the bijectivity of
\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
onto its image. The second component of
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is then just
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}\left(X^{\mathrm{V}}\right)
; again by the bijectivity of
\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}:\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
we also get
X^{\mathrm{V}}=0
. Thus,
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is injective and therefore defines vector space isomorphisms
\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
. It follows that
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
is an automorphism of
\left.\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}
over
r_{g}
, independent of the choice of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
.

\bullet
It is then clear that
\left.\mathcal{r}_{g*}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Im}\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\right)\oplus\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle X^{\mathrm{H}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right),\penalty\ -\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}\right),

is also an isomorphism onto its image; its image is complementary to
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
because its intersection with
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
would require that

\displaystyle\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}\left(X^{\mathrm{H}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

which implies that
X^{\mathrm{H}}=0
due to the fact that
\left.\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
is a vector space isomorphism onto its image, as we discussed before. But then

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{r}_{g*}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}(0)

\displaystyle=0,

which implies that the image of
\left.\mathcal{r}_{g*}\right|_{\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}}
is a complement of
\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
in
\mathrm{T}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Hence, we formally define:

Definition 6.41:
Modified pushforward via right-translation

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and we have a horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
. Then we define the
modified right-pushforward
13
13

13

The font of
\mathcal{r}
is a calligraphic r.

\mathcal{r}_{g*}
(with
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
x\in M
)
as the vector bundle isomorphism
\left.\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\to\left.\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}
over
r_{g}
as given in Prop.
6.2.4
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot g}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, where
\sigma
is any (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
with
\sigma_{x}=g
.

Similarly, for a (local) section
\sigma
of
\mathcal{G}
we define the
modified right-pushforward
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}
with
\sigma
as a (local) vector bundle isomorphism
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}(X)=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}

for all
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
(
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
).

Remark 6.42:
Restriction onto vertical bundle gives typical right-pushforward

It is trivial to check that we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{g}(X)\eqqcolon r_{g*}(X)

for all
X\in\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
; we actually have proven this directly after Eq. (
6.2.1
). As also pinpointed in Remark
6.2.2
, if
\mathcal{G}
is a trivial LGB equipped with its canonical flat connection and
\sigma
a constant section, then it is easy to see that
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}=\mathcal{r}_{g*}=r_{g*}
, where
r_{g}
has to be understood as the right-translation of the canonical Lie group action inherited by
\mathcal{G}
in the sense of Ex.
3.2
.

Remarks 6.43
.

Prop.
6.2.4
trivially extends to
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}
, that is,
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}
is a (local) automorphism of
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}
over
r_{\sigma}
. Thus, we can view
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}
as an element of
\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{P};r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right)
.

Hence, the following definition makes sense, especially if thinking about what we discussed for Eq. (
6.2.1
), also recall Rem.
6.2.2
.

Definition 6.44:
Ehresmann connection on principal LGB-bundles

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and we have horizontal distributions
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{P}
, respectively. We call
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
an
Ehresmann connection
or a
connection on
\mathcal{P}
if it is
right-invariant (w.r.t. modified right-pushforward)
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\left(\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\coloneqq\pi(p)
).

Remarks 6.45
.

There is also a definition of connections on such and more general principal bundles in
[
11
, §5.7, paragraph before Prop. 5.38, page 148]
. However, this reference provides a different type of definition; translated to our situation, it is based on assuming that
\mathcal{G}
is defined over another base manifold
N
. In order to define the LGB action on
\mathcal{P}
this reference introduces a moment map
\mu:\mathcal{P}\to N
so that the action of an element
\mathcal{G}_{y}
(
y\in N
) is defined on
\mu^{-1}(\{y\})
, especially the infinitesimal action
r_{g}
of a fixed LGB element
g
acts on tangent vectors of
\mu^{-1}(\{y\})
, not necessarily on the vertical structure of
\mathcal{P}
. Hence, in order to circumvent the problem we discussed in Subsubsection
6.2.1
, the reference’s definition of a connection is then based on assuming that the ”fibres”
\mu^{-1}(\{y\})
are complementary to the vertical structure of
\mathcal{P}
, so these fibres’ tangent spaces define a horizontal distribution while the infinitesimal action
r_{g}
now acts well-defined on the horizontal structure. Henceforth, the reference does not need to look at using sections
\sigma
and their actions.

However, this is not a suitable definition for us, because our moment map is the projection of
\mathcal{P}
itself such that
r_{g}
acts on the vertical structure. The reference’s definition is rather restrictive, while our definition works for all principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundles by fixing a connection on
\mathcal{G}
.

Let us discuss several examples.

Example 6.46:
Recovering of the classical definition

As we already discussed several times, especially recall Remark
6.2.4
and Ex.
6.1.2
, but ultimately by the discussion for Eq. (
6.2.1
) which also applies to classical principal bundles and their notion of connection and parallel transport: We recover the typical definition of a connection on a principal bundle if
\mathcal{P}
is a classical principal bundle and if the trivial LGB
\mathcal{G}
is equipped with its canonical flat connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
. We call such a connection a
classical connection
.

Example 6.47:
Associated LGBs

Recall Def.
2.2
, and recall that LGBs themselves are principal bundles as in Ex.
6.1.2
. That is, let
G,H
be Lie groups,
P\to M
a principal
G
-bundle over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\psi
a
G
-representation on
H
. Then we have the LGB associated to the principal bundle
P
and the representation
\psi
on
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\coloneqq P\times_{\psi}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

Fix a typical classical connection on
P
; as also introduced in Subsubsection
6.2.1
we have an associated parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{P}:P_{x}\to\Gamma(\alpha^{*}P)
along a curve
\alpha:[0,t]\to M
(
t>0
), where
\alpha(0)\eqqcolon x
. As proven in
[
17
, §5.9, Thm. 5.9.1, page 289f.]
, we have a canonical well-defined parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}_{x}\to\Gamma(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{H})
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\bigl([p,h]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{P}(p),h\right]

for all
[p,h]\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
. This has a 1:1 correspondence to a horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{H}
. Observe that we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\Bigl([p,h]\cdot\left[p,h^{\prime}\right]\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\Bigl(\left[p,hh^{\prime}\right]\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{P}(p),hh^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{P}(p),h\right]\cdot\left[\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{P}(p),h^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\bigl([p,h]\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\Bigl(\left[p,h^{\prime}\right]\Bigr)

for all
[p,h],\left[p,h^{\prime}\right]\in\mathcal{H}_{x}
. Now recall that the whole motivation behind Def.
6.2.4
comes from Eq. (
6.2.1
) (also recall Remark
6.2.2
) which itself stems from Eq. (
51
). We see that that Eq. (
51
) is satisfied here, and thus Eq. (
6.2.1
) follows,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\left[p,h^{\prime}\right]*}(X)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{H}_{[p,h]\cdot\left[p,h^{\prime}\right]}\mathcal{H}

for all
X\in\mathrm{H}_{[p,h]}\mathcal{H}
.
By Prop.
6.2.4
and dimensional reasons (horizontal subspaces are of the same dimension) it follows immediately that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{H}
is an (Ehresmann) connection on
\mathcal{H}
in sense of Def.
6.2.4
.

We call such connections on LGBs associated to a classical principal bundle an
associated connection
.

As expected, we have a corresponding connection 1-form. For this we need to formally define the pullback of 1-forms with respect to the modified right-pushforward.

Definition 6.48:
The pullback of forms via modified right-pushforward

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and we have a fixed horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
. For
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
(
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) we define the
pullback via the modified right-pushforward
\mathcal{r}_{g}^{!}\left(\left.\omega\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\right)
(with
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, over
\mathcal{P}_{x}
,
x\in M
)
as an element of
\Gamma\left(\Lambda^{k}\left.\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathcal{P}\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\otimes\mathcal{g}_{x}\right)
by

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathcal{r}_{g}^{!}\left(\left.\omega\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\right)\right)\right|_{p}\left(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\omega_{p\cdot g}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{g*}(Y_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{r}_{g*}(Y_{k})\bigr)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. Similarly we define the
pullback via the modified right-pushforward
\mathcal{r}^{!}_{\sigma}\omega
with a (local) section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
as an element of
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
by

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}\omega\right)\right|_{p}\left(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}}^{!}\left(\left.\omega\right|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\right)\right)\right|_{p}\left(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\right)=\left.\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\omega\right)\right|_{p}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y_{k})\bigr)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
.

Remarks 6.49
.

By Prop.
6.2.4
(and Remark
6.43
) these definitions are well-defined. A short note about the notation on the very right hand side of the second definition: This notation allows us to extend it to vector fields, that is,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}\omega\right)\left(Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\right)=\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\omega\right)\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y_{k})\bigr)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{k}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
. Observe that we have
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y_{l})\in\Gamma\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right)
(
l\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}
) and
r_{\sigma}^{*}\omega\in\Gamma\left(\Lambda^{k}r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)
, using
r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong(\pi\circ r_{\sigma})^{*}\mathcal{g}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
, such that the right hand side is well-defined.

For the following definition recall the adjoint
\mathcal{G}
-representation on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
, Ex.
6.2.3
, and the isomorphism for
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
in Cor.
6.2
.

Definition 6.50:
Connection 1-forms on principal LGB-bundles

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and we have a fixed horizontal distribution
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
. A
connection 1-form
or
gauge field
on
\mathcal{P}
is a 1-form
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
satisfying:

•

(
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance, but w.r.t. modified right-pushforward)

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}A

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A

for all (local)
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.

•

(Identity on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
)

\displaystyle A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\nu

for all (local)
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
.

Remarks 6.51
.

\bullet
Due to the fact that we formulated the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance using Ex.
6.2.3
, one may already be able to show an analogue of the 1:1 correspondence of typical (classical) connection 1-forms to connection reforms and splittings of the Atiyah sequence; that is, a generalization of
[
10
, §3.2, page 90 ff.]
.

\bullet
The
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance reads point-wise for
g\coloneqq\sigma_{x}
(
x\in M
)

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathcal{r}_{g}^{!}\left(A|_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\right)\right)\right|_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\left(\hat{A}_{p}(X)\right)\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
,
where we wrote
A_{p}=\left(p,\hat{A}_{p}\right)
with
\hat{A}_{p}\in\Gamma\left(\mathrm{T}^{*}_{p}\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{g}_{x}\right)
, and
\mathrm{Ad}
is the adjoint representation of
\mathcal{G}
. In the typical formulation of gauge theory the base point component is usually omitted due to that
\mathcal{g}
is then trivial and so also
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}(\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
.

The identity on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
reads pointwise

\displaystyle A_{p}(\widetilde{v_{p}})

\displaystyle=(p,v_{p})

for all
v_{p}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
. For readability we may also omit the basepoint information and just write

\displaystyle A_{p}(\widetilde{v_{p}})

\displaystyle\equiv v_{p}.

Furthermore, recall the notation introduced in Remark
6.2
, then for
\mu\coloneqq(p,v_{p})
we can therefore write

\displaystyle A_{p}\left(\widetilde{\mu}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu.

Finally, we identify (Ehresmann) connections and connection 1-forms on principal LGB-bundles in the typical way; for this recall Cor.
6.2
.

Theorem 6.52:
1:1 correspondence of Ehresmann connections and connection 1-forms

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, and let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
. Then there is a 1:1 correspondence between Ehresmann connections and connection 1-forms on
\mathcal{P}
:

•

Let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
be an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{P}
. Then
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
defines a connection 1-form
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
by

\displaystyle A_{p}\bigl(\widetilde{v}_{p}+X_{p}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(p,v)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
),
v\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
and
X\in\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
.

•

Let
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then
A
defines an Ehresmann connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{P}
via its kernel
\mathrm{Ker}(A)
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}(A_{p})

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proof of ”typical connections”, as
e.g.
provided in
[
17
, §5.2, Thm. 5.2.2, page 262]
.

\bullet
