For the first bullet point we need to show that Def.
6.2.4
is satisfied, and the identity behaviour on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
quickly follows by definition: We have

\displaystyle\left.A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)\right|_{p}

\displaystyle=A_{p}\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{p}\right)=(p,\nu_{x})=\left.\left(\pi^{*}\nu\right)\right|_{p}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
). Therefore it is only left to show the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance. As mentioned in Remark
5.2
, for
v\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
the vector field
\widetilde{v}
on
\mathcal{P}_{x}
is a fundamental vector field coming from the
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-action on
\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Hence, we know by
[
17
, §3.4, Prop. 3.4.6, page 145f.]
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\left(\widetilde{v}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{g*}\left(\widetilde{v}\right)=\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}(v)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, also using Remark
6.2.4
.
14
14

14

This is very straightforward to prove.
Thus,

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathcal{r}^{!}_{\sigma}A\right)\right|_{p}\bigl(\widetilde{v}_{p}+X_{p}\bigr)

\displaystyle=A_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\bigl(\underbrace{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}\left(\widetilde{v}_{p}\right)}_{=\left.\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}\left(\widetilde{v}\right)\right|_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}(X_{p})}_{\in\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\mathcal{P}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot\sigma_{x},\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}(v)\right)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}(p,v)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}\left(A_{p}\bigl(\widetilde{v}_{p}+X_{p}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A\right)\right|_{p}\bigl(\widetilde{v}_{p}+X_{p}\bigr)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
,
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
,
v\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
and
X_{p}\in\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. This finishes the proof for the first bullet point.

\bullet
For the second bullet point we make use of Cor.
6.2
and

\displaystyle A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\nu

for all (local) sections
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. This implies that
A
has not only values in
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
, but acts also as identity on
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
. Thus,
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{Ker}(A_{p})
is a complementary subspace of
\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
. In order to show that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
is a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{P}
, we fix a local frame
\left(e_{a}\right)_{a}
of
\mathcal{g}
over
U
(an open subset of
M
), so that
\left(\pi^{*}e_{a}\right)_{a}
is a frame of
\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}|_{\pi^{-1}(U)}
. Therefore we write

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=A^{a}\otimes\pi^{*}e_{a},

where
A^{a}\in\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U}\right)
. Due to
A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)=\pi^{*}\nu
we get

\displaystyle A^{a}\left(\widetilde{e_{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta^{a}_{b},

where
\delta^{a}_{b}
is the Kronecker delta. Hence,
\left(A^{a}\right)_{a}
is the dual frame of
\left(\widetilde{e_{a}}\right)_{a}
, which is a frame of
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
by Cor.
6.2
, and so the
A^{a}
are linear independent to each other. Fix an auxiliary fibre metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}
, and denote with
\left(V^{a}\right)_{a}
the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
-dual frame to
\left(A^{a}\right)_{a}
,
i.e.

\displaystyle A^{a}

\displaystyle=\langle V^{a},\cdot\rangle.

Due to the smoothness of
A
and due to that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a fibre metric,
\left(V^{a}\right)_{a}
are smooth (local) vector fields on
\mathcal{P}
, linear independent to each other. Observe that any orthogonal frame
\left(W^{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha}
to
\left(V^{a}\right)_{a}
satisfies

\displaystyle A^{a}(W^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\langle V^{a},W^{\alpha}\rangle=0.

Thus, we derived that
\mathrm{Ker}(A)|_{U}
is spanned by such frames
\left(W^{\alpha}\right)_{\alpha}
, in total,
\mathrm{Ker}(A)
is spanned by a locally free sheaf of modules of constant rank. By the 1:1 correspondence of vector bundle and locally free sheaf of modules of constant rank, we can conclude that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{Ker}(A)
is a subbundle of
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}
; complementary to
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
due to what we have shown earlier.

It is only left to show the right-invariance of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
. So let
X\in\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
for
p\in\mathcal{P}
. Then

\displaystyle A_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}^{!}_{\sigma}(A)\right)_{p}(X)=\sAd_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}\bigl(A_{p}(X)\bigr)=0

for all (local)
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, where
x\coloneqq\pi(p)
. Thence,
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(X)=\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}(X)\in\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\mathcal{P}
, and therefore we derive by Prop.
6.2.4
and dimensional reasons that
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}\left(\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)=\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\mathcal{P}
. We eventually conclude that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
is an Ehresmann connection.
∎

We finish this subsection with the following technical and useful corollary.

Corollary 6.53:
Commutation of modified push-forward and projections

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\to M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{P}
; denote with
\pi_{h}
and
\pi_{v}
the associated projections on the horizontal and vertical bundle of
\mathcal{P}
, respectively. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\circ\pi_{h}

\displaystyle=\pi_{h}\circ\mathcal{r}_{g*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\circ\pi_{v}

\displaystyle=\pi_{v}\circ\mathcal{r}_{g*}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

Remarks 6.54
.

This extends of course to (local) sections
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, and as discussed in Remark
6.25
we can extend these equations to vector fields on
\mathcal{P}
via pullbacks, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\circ\pi_{h}

\displaystyle=r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi_{h}\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\circ\pi_{v}

\displaystyle=r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi_{v}\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*},

making use of that
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}
is an isomorphism over
r_{\sigma*}
, recall Prop.
6.2.4
.

Proof.

By Cor.
6.2
(also recall Remark
6.2.4
) and Def.
6.2.4
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\left(\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{V}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{g*}\left(\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{p\cdot g}\mathcal{P},

thus,
\mathcal{r}_{g*}
preserves the splitting
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathrm{V}\mathcal{P}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.3
Gauge transformations

Let us now look at how gauge transformations of
A
look like in this setting; for this recall the definition of gauge transformations in Def.
6.1.3
. As in Remark
3.2
we expect a relationship between gauge transformations and certain LGB valued maps. For the following also recall Ex.
6.2.3
.

Definition 6.55:
LGB-valued conjugation maps

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then we define the group
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{G}
-valued conjugation maps
as a set by

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\pi^{*}\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \sigma_{p\cdot g}=\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}(\sigma_{p})\text{ for all }(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\bigr\}.

Its group structure is inherited by the point-wise group structure of
\Gamma(\pi^{*}\mathcal{G})
; recall Cor.
3.1
.

Remark 6.56:
Group structure on
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}

It is trivial to check that
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
is indeed a subgroup of
\Gamma(\pi^{*}\mathcal{G})
.

C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
canonically acts on
\mathcal{P}
on the right via the given
\mathcal{G}
-action: Let
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
, then we define

\displaystyle p\cdot\sigma_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq p\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}\right)

(58)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component. In essence, we drop the notation of
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
, as if we view
\sigma
as a map
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{G}
of fibre bundles over
\pi
; recall Subsection
1.3
. If we denote the right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
by
\Phi
, then observe by
\sigma_{p}=(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\sigma_{p}))
that we could also write

\displaystyle p\cdot\sigma_{p}

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}\right)\bigr)=\Phi\left(\sigma_{p}\right).

As in the case of typical/classical principal bundles, we have the following statement.

Proposition 6.57:
Gauge transformations as
\mathcal{G}
-valued conjugation maps

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Then there is a well-defined group isomorphism of gauge transformations and
\mathcal{G}
-valued conjugation maps given by

\displaystyle\sAut(\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}},

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma^{H},

where
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
is defined by

\displaystyle H(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sigma^{H}_{p}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

This result can be proven similarly as for Lie group based principal bundles; however smoothness needs to be discussed a bit. In the following we make use of that pullback manifolds like
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
are embedded submanifolds of product manifolds like
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{G}
, we will not further mention it.

\bullet
First of all, due to the fact that
H
is base-preserving we know that
H(p)
is in the same fibre as
p
(
p\in\mathcal{P}
), and due to that the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
is simply transitive there is a unique element of
\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}|_{p}
, denoted by
\sigma^{H}_{p}
, such that

\displaystyle H(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sigma_{p}^{H}=p\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right),

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the smooth projection onto the second component.
Let us first show smoothness of
p\mapsto\sigma_{p}^{H}
: We can write

\displaystyle H(p)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{p}\Bigl(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)\Bigr),

where
\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{P}_{x}
is the orbit map through
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
). By Remark
6.1.1
,
\Phi_{p}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-equivariant diffeomorphism, especially invertible, so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)=\left(\Phi_{p}\right)^{-1}\bigl(H(p)\bigr).

Let us define the map

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi^{-1}\left(p,p^{\prime}\right)\coloneqq\left(\Phi_{p}\right)^{-1}\left(p^{\prime}\right)

which is a map over
\pi
by construction. If we can show smoothness of
\Phi^{-1}
, then smoothness of
\sigma^{H}
follows additionally due to smoothness of
H
and

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}^{H}

\displaystyle=\left(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)\right)=\left(p,\left(\Phi_{p}\right)^{-1}\bigl(H(p)\bigr)\right)=\Bigl(p,\Phi^{-1}\bigl(p,H(p)\bigr)\Bigr).

Observe that we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}(p,g)=g

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{p}\right)^{-1}\bigl(\Phi_{p}(g)\bigr)=\Phi^{-1}\bigl(p,\Phi(p,g)\bigr)

for all
(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
, where
\Phi
denotes the right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
. The map

\displaystyle L:\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\to\pi^{*}\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle(p,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(p,\Phi(p,g)\bigr),

is a base-preserving principal bundle isomorphism (w.r.t. the LGB isomorphism given as the identity map on
\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
), recall Cor.
6.1.3
and Remark
6.13
. Thus, we have in total

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}\circ L

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2},

and thus

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ L^{-1},

so
\Phi^{-1}
is smooth, and therefore
\sigma^{H}
is smooth, too.

\bullet
That
\sigma^{H}
is an element of
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
follows as usual:

\displaystyle(p\cdot g)\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p\cdot g}\right)

\displaystyle=H(p\cdot g)=H(p)\cdot g=p\cdot\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)\penalty\ g\right)

for all
(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
,
so that, by the simply transitivity of the action,

\displaystyle g\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p\cdot g}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)\penalty\ g,

and thus

\displaystyle\sigma^{H}_{p\cdot g}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(p\cdot g,\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p\cdot g}\right)\Bigr)=\Bigl(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)\penalty\ g\Bigr)=\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right).

Thus,
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
.

\bullet
The inverse of
H\mapsto\sigma^{H}
is clearly given by

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\to\sAut(\mathcal{P}),

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto H^{\sigma},

where

\displaystyle H^{\sigma}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq p\cdot\sigma_{p}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
. Smoothness here is now obvious, it is also clearly base-preserving, and we have

\displaystyle H^{\sigma}(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=p\cdot g\penalty\ \underbrace{\sigma_{p\cdot g}}_{\mathclap{=\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}\left(\sigma_{p}\right)}}=p\cdot g\penalty\ \underbrace{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}\left(\sigma_{p}\right)\right)}_{\mathclap{=c_{g^{-1}}\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\sigma_{p})\right)}}=p\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}\right)\penalty\ g=p\cdot\sigma_{p}\penalty\ g=H^{\sigma}(p)\cdot g

for all
(p,g)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}
. Thus,
H^{\sigma}\in\sAut(\mathcal{P})
, and so this finishes the proof.
∎

Using this, we can finally formulate the gauge transformations of connection 1-forms; for this recall Cor.
6.1.3
and the Darboux derivative, Def.
6.2.2
. Also recall the pullback Darboux derivative, Remark
6.2.2
.

Theorem 6.58:
Gauge transformations of connection 1-forms

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
H\in\sAut(\mathcal{P})
. We then have that
H^{!}A
is a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
and

\displaystyle H^{!}A

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}}}\circ A+\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H},

where
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
is defined as in Prop.
6.3
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component.

Similar to Def. (
58
) we may shortly just write

\displaystyle H^{!}A

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}}}\circ A+\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}.

Proof.

First of all observe that
H^{!}A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};H^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
, and trivially
H^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong(\pi\circ H)^{*}\mathcal{g}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
. We will make use of this and similar isomorphisms in the following without further mentioning it. We will often not bookkeep the basepoint component in the following pairs and triples; so, everything has to be read in such a way that we have again values in the correct space. It would just be cumbersome to keep track of all these natural isomorphisms, and we decided for readability to avoid this bookkeeping most of the time.

\bullet
We then have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}H^{!}A\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(H^{!}A\right)_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(H^{!}A\right)_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{H\left(p\cdot\sigma_{x}\right)}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}H\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\right)\right)

for all (local)
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
,
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. We also get by definition of
\sAut(\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle H(p\cdot\sigma_{x})

\displaystyle=H(p)\cdot\sigma_{x},

that is,

\displaystyle H\circ r_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=r_{\sigma}\circ H

and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}H\circ\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}\left(H\circ r_{\sigma}\right)=\mathrm{D}_{p}\left(r_{\sigma}\circ H\right)=\mathrm{D}_{H(p)}r_{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{D}_{p}H.

Now also observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}H\left(\widetilde{\nu}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}H\circ\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}\right)(\nu)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\underbrace{\left(H\circ\Phi_{p}\right)}_{\mathclap{\mathcal{G}_{x}\ni g\mapsto H(p\cdot g)=H(p)\cdot g}}(\nu)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{H(p)}(\nu)=\widetilde{\nu}_{H(p)}

(59)

for all
\nu\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
, where
\Phi_{p}
is the orbit map through
p
. Due to that
H
is base-preserving we derive

\displaystyle\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(H^{!}\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)_{p}(X)=\left((\pi\circ H)^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)_{p}(X)=\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)_{p}(X).

In total we get

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}H^{!}A\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=A_{H(p)\cdot\sigma_{x}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{H(p)}r_{\sigma}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)\cdot\sigma_{x}}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{H(p)\cdot\sigma_{x}}\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}A\right)_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A\right)_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(H(p)\cdot\sigma_{x},\penalty\ \mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{x}^{-1}}\left(\widehat{A}_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(H(p),\penalty\ \widehat{A}_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(H^{!}A\right)_{p}(X)\right),

where we wrote
A=\left(p,\widehat{A}_{p}\right)
with
\widehat{A}\coloneqq\pi_{2}\circ A
(
\pi_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
the projection onto the second component), and thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}H^{!}A

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\penalty\ H^{!}A.

By Eq. (
59
) we also get
\mathrm{D}H(\widetilde{\nu})=H^{*}\widetilde{\nu}
for all (local)
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and thus

\displaystyle\left(H^{!}A\right)\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(H^{*}A\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}H\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)\bigr)=\left(H^{*}A\right)\left(H^{*}\widetilde{\nu}\right)=H^{*}\bigl(A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)\bigr)=H^{*}\pi^{*}\nu=(\pi\circ H)^{*}\nu=\pi^{*}\nu.

Hence,
H^{!}A
is a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
.

\bullet
For the last part recall Prop.
6.3
, especially we have a unique
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
such that

\displaystyle H(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\sigma^{H}_{p}=p\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)=\Phi\left(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)\right)=\Bigl(\Phi\circ\left(\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}},\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{H}\right)\Bigr)(p)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
), where
\Phi
is the right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
, also recall Def.
58
, that is,
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component. Define
\widetilde{\sigma}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{H}
,
\mathcal{P}\ni p\mapsto\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}\in\mathcal{G}
; by Remark
6.2.2
,
6.2.2
and Thm.
5.3
, especially Remark
6.2.2
, we can calculate

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\left(p,\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}\right)}\Phi\bigl(X,\mathrm{D}_{p}\widetilde{\sigma}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}(X)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}\widetilde{\sigma}(X)\bigr)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p\cdot\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)}\left(\left(\mathrm{D}_{\sigma^{H}_{p}}\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\mathrm{D}_{p}\sigma^{H}\right)(X)\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma^{H}_{p}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\sigma^{H}(X)\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma^{H}_{p}}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\sigma^{H}(X)\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\Delta^{\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}}\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

for all
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, where
\sigma
is a (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
so that
\sigma_{x}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)
.

Then

\displaystyle\left(H^{!}A\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=A_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=A_{H(p)}\left(\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{p\cdot\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}\left(\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)\right)+\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}^{!}A\right)_{p}(X)+\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}^{-1}}\bigl(A_{p}(X)\bigr)+\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)^{-1}\right)}\bigl(A_{p}(X)\bigr)+\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X),

which finishes the proof, where we used the trivial relation (alternatively recall Cor.
3.1
)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\left(\sigma_{p}^{H}\right)^{-1}\right).

∎

Of course there is also the sense of gauge transformation with respect to gauges as in typical gauge theory (see
e.g.

[
17
, §5.4, page 270ff.]
), recall Def.
6.1.3
.

Definition 6.59:
Local gauge field

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
s\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{U})
be a (local) gauge over an open subset
U\subset M
. Then we define the
local connection 1-form
of
local gauge field
A_{s}\in\Omega^{1}\left(U;\left.\mathcal{g}\right|_{U}\right)
(w.r.t.
s
)
by

\displaystyle A_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq s^{!}A.

Remarks 6.60
.

A
has values in
\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}
, and thus
A_{s}
has values in
s^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong(\pi\circ s)^{*}\mathcal{g}=\mathds{1}_{U}^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong\left.\mathcal{g}\right|_{U}
.

Gauge transformations now naturally arise in a change of the gauge
s
. So, let
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
be two open subsets of
M
so that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
. For two gauges
s_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\right)
and
s_{j}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{j}}\right)
there is then a unique
\sigma_{ji}\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
such that

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{j}\cdot\sigma_{ji}=\Phi_{s_{j}}\circ\sigma_{ji}

on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
,
where
\Phi_{s_{j}}
is the orbit through
s_{j}
, especially also recall Lemma
6.1.3
.
The unique existence is clear by the definition of principal bundles, while the smoothness follows by Lemma
6.1.3
so that we can write
\sigma_{ji}
as the composition of smooth maps

\displaystyle\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle=\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}\circ s_{i}.

In the following we also introduce the notation

\displaystyle\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}\right)^{-1}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}(p)\right)^{-1}

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{U_{j}}
, that is,
\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}
is the inverse of the orbit map
\Phi_{s_{j}}
, but
(\cdot)^{-1}
is the inverse of
\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}(p)
as an element of
\mathcal{G}_{U_{j}}
.

Instead of calculating directly how
A_{s_{i}}
and
A_{s_{j}}
are related we want to use Thm.
6.3
. For this it will be useful to understand that gauge transformations
\sAut(\mathcal{P})
are locally isomorphic to the group of sections
\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
via a gauge, analogously to the typical formulation of gauge theory as
e.g.
illustrated in
[
17
, §5.3.2, page 268f.]
. While the isomorphism of
\sAut(\mathcal{P})
to
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}\subset\Gamma(\pi^{*}\mathcal{G})
is global, the following isomorphism is in general only local.

Proposition 6.61:
Gauge transformations and sections of the structural LGB

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Also let
s\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U}\right)
be a gauge defined over some open subset
U
of
M
. Then we have a group isomorphism given by

\displaystyle C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right),

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s,

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component,
with inverse

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}},

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma^{\tau},

where

\displaystyle\sigma^{\tau}_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(p,c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\tau_{\pi(p)}\right)\right)=\left(p,\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}\penalty\ \tau_{\pi(p)}\penalty\ \Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{U}
,
with
\Phi_{s}
being the orbit map through
s
.

Remark 6.62:
Other notation

Due to the fact that
s^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\cong(\pi\circ s)^{*}\mathcal{G}=\mathds{1}_{U}^{*}\mathcal{G}\cong\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}
, and due to what we discussed in Subsection
1.3
about pullback sections, we could rewrite the first map to

\displaystyle C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}

\displaystyle\to\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right),

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto s^{*}\sigma.

Proof of Prop.
6.3
.

For
\sigma\in C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}\subset\Gamma\left(\pi^{*}\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)
observe that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s
is by construction a section of
\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}
due to

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\sigma\circ s=\pi\circ s=\mathds{1}_{U},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{P}
is the projection onto the first component.
For the supposed inverse we have similarly that
\sigma^{\tau}
is a section of
\Gamma(\pi^{*}\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U})
by construction; then observe, using Lemma
6.1.3
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{p\cdot g}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot g,c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p\cdot g)\right)^{-1}}\left(\tau_{\pi(p\cdot g)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot g,c_{g^{-1}\penalty\ \left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\tau_{\pi(p)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p\cdot g,\left(c_{g^{-1}}\circ c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\right)\left(\tau_{\pi(p)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}\left(p,c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\tau_{\pi(p)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{c}_{g^{-1}}\left(\sigma^{\tau}_{p}\right)

for all
(p,g)\in\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U}*\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}
, thus,
\sigma^{\tau}\in C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}
. Recall

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=s_{\pi(p)}\cdot\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)

for all
p\in\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U}
,
so that

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}_{s}\circ s

\displaystyle=e|_{U},

where
e
is the neutral section of
\mathcal{G}
. On one hand

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{pr_{2}}\circ\sigma^{\tau}\right)(s_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{\tau}_{s_{x}}\right)=c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(s_{x})\right)^{-1}}\left(\tau_{\pi(s_{x})}\right)=c_{e_{x}}\left(\tau_{x}\right)=\tau_{x}

for all
x\in M
and
\tau\in\Gamma(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U})
, and by Def.
6.3
and Ex.
6.2.3
we have one the other hand

\displaystyle\sigma^{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s}_{p}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(p,\left(c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\left(\sigma_{s_{\pi(p)}}\right)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(p,\left(c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\left(\sigma_{p\cdot\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\right)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(p,\left(c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\mathcal{c}_{\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)}\right)\left(\sigma_{p}\right)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(p,\left(c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)\right)^{-1}}\circ c_{\Phi^{-1}_{s}(p)}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{2}\right)\left(\sigma_{p}\right)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\sigma_{p})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{p}

for all
p\in\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U}
and
\sigma\in C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}
. Thus,
\sigma\mapsto\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s
is bijective with inverse
\tau\mapsto\sigma^{\tau}
, and so it is only left to show that
\sigma\mapsto\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s
is a group homomorphism. For
\sigma^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U};\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}\right)^{\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U}}
we have

\displaystyle\left.\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\right|_{p}

\displaystyle=\left(p,\mathrm{pr}_{2}(\sigma_{p})\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{p}\right)\right),

thus,

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\right)\circ s\right)_{x}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma_{s_{x}}\right)\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{s_{x}}\right)=\left(\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma\circ s\right)\cdot\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ s\right)\right)_{x}

for all
x\in M
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.63:
Change of gauge as a local bundle automorphism

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle. Also let
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
be two open subsets of
M
so that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
, two gauges
s_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\right)
and
s_{j}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{j}}\right)
, and the unique
\sigma_{ji}\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
with
s_{i}=s_{j}\cdot\sigma_{ji}
on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
.

Then the map
H_{ji}
defined by

\displaystyle\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}

\displaystyle\to\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}},

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto\left.s_{i}\right|_{\pi(p)}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p),

is an element of
\sAut\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
,
where
\Phi_{s_{j}}
is the orbit map through
s_{j}
.

Using the notation of Prop.
6.3
, we also get that
\sigma^{H_{ji}}
is related to
\sigma_{ji}
via
s_{j}
in sense of Prop.
6.3
, that is,

\displaystyle\sigma^{H_{ji}}_{p}

\displaystyle=\left(p,c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\left.\sigma_{ji}\right|_{\pi(p)}\right)\right)

for all
p\in\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
, and

\displaystyle\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{H_{ji}}\circ s_{j},

where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component.

Proof.

Recall Lemma
6.1.3
for the following proof.

We have
\left.s_{i}\right|_{\pi(p)}\in\mathcal{P}_{\pi(p)}
and
\Phi_{s_{j}}
is base-preserving, and thus
\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)\in\mathcal{G}_{\pi(p)}
, so that
H_{ji}
is well-defined and also base-preserving because the right
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
preserves the fibres.

H_{ji}
is clearly smooth by construction. Finally,

\displaystyle H_{ji}(p\cdot g)

\displaystyle=\left.s_{i}\right|_{\pi(p\cdot g)}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p\cdot g)=\left.s_{i}\right|_{\pi(p)}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)\cdot g=H_{ji}(p)\cdot g

for all
(p,g)\in\left.\left(\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}\right)\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
, thus,
H_{ji}
is a principal bundle morphism. The inverse
H^{-1}_{ji}
is given by

\displaystyle H^{-1}_{ji}

\displaystyle=H_{ij}

due to

\displaystyle\left(H_{ij}\circ H_{ji}\right)(p)

\displaystyle=\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi^{-1}_{s_{i}}\left(\left.s_{i}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)\right)=\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\cdot{\underbrace{\Phi^{-1}_{s_{i}}\left(\left.s_{i}\right|_{x}\right)}_{=e_{x}}}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)=\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)=p

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
, where
x\coloneqq\pi(p)
; by symmetry of course similarly when swapping
j
and
i
. Thus,
H_{ji}
is bijective and its inverse smooth, so that
H_{ji}\in\sAut\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
.

Now write

\displaystyle H_{ji}(p)

\displaystyle=\left.s_{i}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)

\displaystyle=\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\cdot\left.\sigma_{ji}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)

\displaystyle=\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)\cdot\left(\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)\right)^{-1}\cdot\left.\sigma_{ji}\right|_{x}\cdot\Phi_{s_{j}}^{-1}(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\left.\sigma_{ji}\right|_{x}\right),

and due to
H_{ji}(p)=p\cdot\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}_{p}\right)
we can immediately derive

\displaystyle\sigma^{H_{ji}}_{p}

\displaystyle=\left(p,c_{\left(\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}(p)\right)^{-1}}\left(\left.\sigma_{ji}\right|_{\pi(p)}\right)\right),

thus,
\sigma^{H_{ji}}
is related to
\sigma_{ji}
w.r.t.
s_{j}
in sense of Prop.
6.3
and so the remaining part of the proposition’s statement follows by Prop.
6.3
.
∎

Using this gauge transformation we can now relate
A_{s_{i}}
and
A_{s_{j}}
.

Theorem 6.64:
Gauge transformations as a change of gauge in the local gauge field

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Also let
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
be two open subsets of
M
so that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
, two gauges
s_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\right)
and
s_{j}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{j}}\right)
, and the unique
\sigma_{ji}\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
with
s_{i}=s_{j}\cdot\sigma_{ji}
on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
.

Then we have over
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
that

\displaystyle A_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}\circ A_{s_{j}}+\Delta\sigma_{ji}.

Proof.

Define
H_{ji}\in\sAut\left(\left.\mathcal{P}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
as in Prop.
6.3
(also following its notation), then observe

\displaystyle\left(H_{ji}\circ s_{j}\right)_{x}

\displaystyle=\left.s_{i}\right|_{x}\cdot{\underbrace{\Phi^{-1}_{s_{j}}\left(\left.s_{j}\right|_{x}\right)}_{=e_{x}}}=\left.s_{i}\right|_{x}

for all
x\in U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, and thus

\displaystyle s_{j}^{!}H_{ji}^{!}A

\displaystyle=\left(H_{ji}\circ s_{j}\right)^{!}A=A_{s_{i}}.

By Thm.
6.3
we also have

\displaystyle H_{ji}^{!}A

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)^{-1}}}\circ A+\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H_{ji}}.

We now want to apply
s_{j}^{!}
on both hand sides.
Recall Remark
6.60
and
6.3
, we will now use several natural isomorphisms without further mention, especially the base-point component will be treated in a ”sloppy” way; the advantage will be a cleaner notation, and this is the reason why
\sAd
becomes
\mathrm{Ad}
by applying
s_{j}^{!}
on the right hand side.
Using Prop.
6.3
and Remark
6.2.2
we can derive

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\left(\pi\circ s_{j}\right)^{*}\Delta\bigr)\left(s_{j}^{*}\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)=\bigl(s_{j}^{*}\pi^{*}\Delta\bigr)\left(s_{j}^{*}\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)=s_{j}^{!}\left(\bigl(\pi^{*}\Delta\bigr)\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)\right),

and

\displaystyle s_{j}^{!}\left({\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)^{-1}}}\circ A\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)^{-1}\circ s_{j}}}\circ s_{j}^{!}A={\mathrm{Ad}_{\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{H_{ji}}\circ s_{j}\right)^{-1}}}\circ s_{j}^{!}A={\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}}\circ A_{s_{j}},

where we used again the trivial relation (alternatively recall Cor.
3.1
)

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H_{ji}}\right)^{-1}.

Altogether, concluding the proof with

\displaystyle A_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=s_{j}^{!}H_{ji}^{!}A={\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}}\circ A_{s_{j}}+\Delta\sigma_{ji}.

∎

This allows us to finally show the first relation to the infinitesimal gauge theory developed by Alexei Kotov and Thomas Strobl.

Remark 6.65:
Integrating curved Yang-Mills gauge theories, part I

Let the situation be as in Thm.
6.3
, then set

\displaystyle\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\varepsilon}

for a
\varepsilon\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{g}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we define the
infinitesimal gauge transformation
\delta_{\varepsilon}A_{s_{j}}
of
A
along
\varepsilon
(w.r.t.
s_{j}
)
as an element of
\Omega^{1}\left(U_{i}\cap U_{j};\left.\mathcal{g}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
by

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}A_{s_{j}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}A_{s_{i}}.

The corresponding derivative related to the adjoint representation can be calculated as usual, and for the corresponding term related to the Darboux derivative we use the vector bundle connection introduced in Def.
6.2.2
. Thus, we get
15
15

15

Rigorously, one has to define
\delta_{\varepsilon}A_{s_{j}}
via the natural projection
\mathrm{V}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
as we did in Prop.
6.2.2
.

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}A_{s_{j}}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\varepsilon-\left[\varepsilon,A_{s_{j}}\right]_{\mathcal{g}}.

This is
precisely
the infinitesimal gauge transformation developed by Alexei Kotov and Thomas Strobl, see
[
1
]
for a concise summary or
[
6
]
for an extended introduction. These references are for the very general situation using Lie algebroids, hence see alternatively
[
5
]
for this type of gauge theory restricted to Lie algebra bundles.

Thus, we successfully integrated this definition of infinitesimal gauge transformations.

Remark 6.66:
Closure of gauge transformations

The gauge transformations close simply by construction, that is,

\displaystyle H^{\prime!}H^{!}A

\displaystyle=(H\circ H^{\prime})^{!}A

for all
H^{\prime},H\in\sAut(\mathcal{P})
. The reader might wonder why, given that existing literature for the infinitesimal version (like
[
6
, Thm. 4.3.37 and Thm. 4.3.43]
,
[
19
, Prop. 8, Thm. 1]
and
[
20
, Eq. 9, 10 and 11]
) prove that one needs a
multiplicative connection
on
\mathcal{G}
, that is, the parallel transport on
\mathcal{G}
has to be a homomorphism of the
\mathcal{G}
-multiplication, while we did not assume this in this Subsection. However, the culprit is that
A
as connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
exists if and only if the connection on
\mathcal{G}
is a multiplicative connection. It is not in the scope of this paper to prove this but the proof of existence of
A
is similar to ordinary existence arguments because multiplicative connections are indeed Ehresmann connections, and for the other direction one only has to show that
A
implies a parallel transport as in (
51
) which is also straightforward to show. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p\cdot g\cdot q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(gq)

for all
(p,g,q)\in\mathcal{P}*\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
, but also

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p\cdot g\cdot q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{P}}(p)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)\cdot\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{G}}(q).

Due to the fact that we have a simply transitive
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
, it follows that the connection on
\mathcal{G}
is multiplicative. Multiplicative connections will be introduced later in more detail when we discuss the field strength. We conclude that the closure of gauge transformations is equivalent to the existence of
A
, which is equivalent to the multiplicativity of the connection on
\mathcal{G}
.

Alexei’s and Thomas’s theory also generalizes the curvature/field strength related to gauge theory. Hence, let us now turn to this notion.

6.4
Generalized curvature/field strength

6.4.1
multiplicative Yang-Mills connections on LGBs

In the following we will introduce the curvature; in order to achieve gauge invariance later in the physical gauge theory, we will now need to assume what we will call
compatibility conditions
on the horizontal distribution of the structural LGB
\mathcal{G}
. Here in this work we will just present these compatibility conditions, and you will be able to see in the proofs why this leads to gauge invariance later. However, if you want to understand the following conditions coming from a point of view not knowing the solution beforehand, then either see
[
1
]
or
[
6
]
; the latter also provides a physical motivation using what I called the
field redefinitions
which naturally motivate the gauge theory presented here by transforming a classical gauge theory in such a way that the physics is preserved; now recall Def.
6.2.2
for the following.

We start with the infinitesimal version of the compatibility conditions, already known and pointed out in the previously-mentioned references.

Definition 6.67:
Infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connection

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we say that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an
infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connection (on
\mathcal{G}
)
if there is a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
such that the
\mathcal{G}
-connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
on the associated LAB
\mathcal{g}
with bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
satisfies the
infinitesimal compatibility conditions

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\left[\mu,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\mu,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\left[\mu,\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}},

(74)

\displaystyle R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(X,Y)\mu

\displaystyle=\left[\zeta(X,Y),\mu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

(75)

for all
\mu,\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, where
R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}
is the curvature of
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
. We then also call
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
an
(infinitesimal) multiplicative Yang-Mills connection
and denote it by
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
.

In the following,
\zeta
is always such a 2-form as given here, and even though it is not uniquely given we will treat it as if it is fixed for a given
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
.

Remarks 6.68
.

As illustrated in
[
6
, §5.1]
and
[
5
]
such connections also arise in
[
10
, §7.2, page 271ff.]
: There these connections are called
Lie derivation laws covering a coupling
due to the fact that these are important in the construction of ”coupling” an LAB to a Lie algebroid via a Whitney sum in such a way that the outcome is again a Lie algebroid. There is a strong relationship of this study with the gauge theory we are going to define, however, we will not need this so that we will not repeat what has been discussed in
[
6
, §5.1]
and
[
5
]
.

One may want to write that
\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}},\zeta\right)
is a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection, giving an emphasis on the non-uniqueness of
\zeta
. However, we are not going to do so, and the mentioned references and relations to Mackenzie’s work actually show that there is just one object behind all of that describing this multiplicative Yang-Mills connection and its relation to the following gauge theory. It would take too much time introducing all of this again; see the references.

We want to integrate these infinitesimal compatibility conditions to conditions on
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
directly.
Let us initially focus on the first infinitesmal compatibility condition (
74
). In the following we will also view
\mathcal{G}
as a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle which is equipped with the same horizontal distribution as
\mathcal{G}
as LGB; related notions and notations carry over. One can show that Ehresmann connections satisfy (
74
), and in fact Ehresmann connections on LGBs (and Lie groupoids) were already discussed in
[
12
]
; and there is also a rather recent preprint related to a similar subject, see
[
13
]
.
Special thanks to Camille Laurent-Gengoux for pointing out these references and discussing related subjects, this helped me tremendously.

Lemma 6.69:
Ehresmann connections induce Lie bracket derivations,

[
12
, §4.5, Prop. 4.21]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
. If
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an (Ehresmann) connection on
\mathcal{G}
as principal bundle, then the infinitesimal compatibility condition (
74
) is satisfied.

Proof.

This can be quickly answered by recalling how the parallel transport behaves; recall that Def.
6.2.4
comes from Eq. (
6.2.1
) which itself stems from Eq. (
51
); that is, the associated parallel transport to an Ehresmann connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
satisfies Eq. (
51
). This means that the corresponding parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{G}
(omitting the notation of the involved curve) is a homomorphism,
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{G}}(gq)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{G}}(g)\penalty\ \mathrm{PT}^{\mathcal{G}}(q)

for all
(g,q)\in\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
. Thus, infinitesimally the associated parallel transport on
\mathcal{g}
is a homomorphism w.r.t. the Lie bracket, and thus
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is a Lie bracket derivation.
∎

Remark 6.70:
Base manifold a horizontal leave

Observe that the proof’s argument implies that a parallel transported neutral element is again the neutral element of the corresponding final fibre, if
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection. Thus,
M
naturally embedded via the neutral section
e
can then be viewed as a horizontal leave of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
.

Due to this, given compatibility condition (
74
), the following is clear by definition.

Corollary 6.71:
The total Maurer-Cartan form as connection 1-form

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
. Then the total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is the connection 1-form corresponding to
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

This simply follows by Def.
6.2.2
and Thm.
6.2.4
.
∎

Remark 6.72:
Darboux derivative as local gauge field

If
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
, we know by Cor.
6.4.1
that the Darboux derivative
\Delta\sigma
of a (local) section
\sigma
of
\mathcal{G}
(Def.
6.2.2
) is a local gauge field (w.r.t.
\sigma
) corresponding to
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
, recall Def.
6.3
. That is,

\displaystyle\Delta\sigma

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma}.

Furthermore, following
[
12
]
, we can derive that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is a multiplicative form if and only if
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection; let us introduce multiplicative 1-forms. Recall Lemma
5.3
.

Definition 6.73:
Multiplicative forms,
[
14
, §2.1, special situation of Def. 2.1]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
. Then we call an
\omega\in\Omega^{1}\left(\mathcal{G};\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)
a
multiplicative form
if

\displaystyle\omega_{gq}\left(\mathrm{D}_{(g,q)}\Phi(X,Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{q^{-1}}\bigl(\omega_{g}(X)\bigr)+\bigl(gq,\widehat{\omega}_{q}(Y)\bigr)

for all
(g,q)\in\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
and
(X,Y)\in\mathrm{T}_{(g,q)}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G})
,
where
\Phi:\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the multiplication in
\mathcal{G}
, and
\widehat{\omega}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\omega
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\to\mathcal{g}
is the natural projection onto the second component.

Remarks 6.74
.

The shape of the second summand is again just because of technical reasons due to the definition of pullback bundles and their base points. If we do not care so much about the involved base points since these are clear by context, then we can also shortly write

\displaystyle\left(\Phi^{!}\omega\right)_{(g,q)}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{q^{-1}}\bigl(\omega_{g}(X)\bigr)+\omega_{q}(Y).

Also recall what we mentioned in Subsection
1.3
about pullback sections.

Theorem 6.75:
Ehresmann connection 1-forms on LGBs are multiplicative,

[
12
, §4.4, implication of Lemma 4.14]

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
with corresponding total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
. Then
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an (Ehresmann) connection on
\mathcal{G}
as principal bundle if and only if
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is multiplicative.

Proof.

The general approach in the mentioned reference is different to ours; hence, let us provide a proof suitable to our constructions. We will view
\mathcal{G}
as a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle whose horizontal distribution is also
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
. We will also use the same notation as in Def.
6.4.1
.

To answer whether or not
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection we just need to discuss whether
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{G}
due to the fact that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is the kernel of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
by definition (see also Cor.
6.4.1
), also using Thm.
6.2.4
. Also by definition,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
already satisfies

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nu.

By additionally using Thm.
5.3
(especially the associated Rem.
6.2.2
) we show

\displaystyle\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{gq}\left(\mathrm{D}_{(g,q)}\Phi(X,Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{gq}\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}R_{\sigma}(X)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{g}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{gq}+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{q}(Y)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{gq}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{gq}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{q*}(X)\bigr)+\left(gq,\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{q}(Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{q}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(X)+\left(gq,\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{q}(Y)\right)

for all
(g,q)\in\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
and
(X,Y)\in\mathrm{T}_{(g,q)}(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G})
, where
\sigma
is a (local) section of
\mathcal{G}
such that
\sigma_{x}=q
. Thus,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is multiplicative if and only if

\displaystyle\sAd_{q^{-1}}\left(\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(X)\right)+\left(gq,\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{q}(Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{q}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(X)+\left(gq,\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{q}(Y)\right)

if and only if

\displaystyle\sAd_{q^{-1}}\left(\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(X)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{q}^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(X)

if and only if
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is a connection 1-form. This finishes the proof.
∎

This Theorem also implies the Leibniz rule of the Darboux derivative.

Proposition 6.76:
Leibniz rule of the Darboux derivative

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\tau^{-1}}\circ\Delta\sigma+\Delta\tau

and

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\Delta\sigma

for all
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.

Remarks 6.77
.

These are precisely the formulas one expects, see
e.g.

[
10
, §5.1, Eq. (2) and (3), page 182]
for the ”classical” case; beware that the reference uses right- instead of left-invariant vector fields to describe the Lie algebra, so that the referenced formulas look explicitly a bit different. Nevertheless, the similarity of the structure should be obvious.

The mentioned reference for Thm.
6.4.1
,
[
12
, §4.4, Lemma 4.14]
, actually uses the Leibniz rule of the Darboux derivative as an equivalent description of Ehresmann connections.

Proof of Prop.
6.4.1
.

For this calculation we will take care about base points of pullback bundles w.r.t. the Darboux derivative, thus recall the last part of Remark
6.25
. Using the same notation as in Def.
6.4.1
, this proposition follows simply by Thm.
6.4.1
, that is, set
Y\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{x}\sigma(X)
and
Z\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{x}\tau(X)
for
X\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
(
x\in M
), then

\displaystyle\bigl(\sigma_{x}\tau_{x},\Delta(\sigma\tau)_{x}(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{x}(\sigma\tau)(X)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{(\sigma_{x},\tau_{x})}\Phi(Y,Z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\tau_{x}^{-1}}\left(\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}}(Y)\right)+\left(\sigma_{x}\tau_{x},\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{\tau_{x}}(Z)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma_{x}\tau_{x},\mathrm{Ad}_{\tau_{x}^{-1}}\left(\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{\sigma_{x}}(Y)\right)+\left(\widehat{\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}}\right)_{\tau_{x}}(Z)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma_{x}\tau_{x},\mathrm{Ad}_{\tau_{x}^{-1}}\bigl(\left.\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\right|_{x}(X)\bigr)+\left.\left(\Delta\tau\right)\right|_{x}(X)\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle\Delta(\sigma\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\tau^{-1}}\circ\Delta\sigma+\Delta\tau.

Now recall Remark
6.4.1
; this implies for the neutral element section
e
that

\displaystyle\Delta e

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

and therefore, using the previous result just derived,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\Delta e=\Delta\left(\sigma\sigma^{-1}\right)=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\Delta\sigma+\Delta\left(\sigma^{-1}\right),

thus, concluding the proof with

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\Delta\sigma.

∎

The Leibniz rule of the Darboux derivative extends to
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
.

Lemma 6.78:
The conjugation of the canonical LAB connection is a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}+\mathrm{ad}_{\Delta\sigma}

(76)

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
, that is,

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\right)\nu

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\nu+\left[\Delta\sigma(X),\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.
If
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connection,
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}=\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
, especially
R_{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}=\mathrm{ad}_{\zeta}
for
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
, then
\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}\coloneqq\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}
is also an infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connection with

\displaystyle R_{\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ad}_{\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}(\zeta)},

in particular we get

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta,\penalty\ \nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=0

(77)

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
.

Proof.

By Prop.
6.4.1
we get

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\mathrm{e\;\!}^{\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}(\nu)}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta\left(\sigma\mathrm{e\;\!}^{\nu}\sigma^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\Delta\left(\sigma\mathrm{e\;\!}^{\nu}\right)+\Delta\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-\nu}}\circ\Delta\sigma+\Delta e^{\nu}\right)-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\Delta\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-\nu}}\circ\Delta\sigma-\Delta\sigma+\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{\nu}\right)

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
, where the first equality is a well-known fact, see
e.g.

[
17
, §1.7, Thm. 1.7.16, page 59]
. Then

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}(\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\Delta\left(\mathrm{e\;\!}^{\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}(t\nu)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\circ\Delta\sigma-\Delta\sigma+\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\left(\mathrm{ad}_{-\nu}\circ\Delta\sigma+\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\circ\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\Delta\sigma}(\nu)+\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right)

making use of that this calculation is pointwise at
x\in M
just a standard derivative in the vector space
\mathcal{g}_{x}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}+\mathrm{ad}_{\Delta\sigma}.

In order to show
R_{\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}}=\mathrm{ad}_{\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}(\zeta)}
one uses the definition
\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}\coloneqq\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}
to calculate the curvature,

\displaystyle R_{\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}}(X,Y)\nu

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}_{X}\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}_{Y}\nu-\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}_{Y}\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}_{X}\nu-\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}_{[X,Y]}\nu

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{X}\circ\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{Y}-\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{Y}\circ\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{X}-\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{[X,Y]}\right)\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\right)(\nu)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(X,Y)\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\right)(\nu)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left[\zeta(X,Y),\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}(\nu)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\bigl(\zeta(X,Y)\bigr),\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. The remaining parts of the statement are trivial and straight-forward calculations, making use of Def.
6.4.1
; in fact, we have proven all the remaining statements already in
[
5
, §3, part of Thm. 3.6]
and in a more general manner in
[
6
, §4.6, Thm. 4.6.9]
: That
\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}
is a Lie bracket derivation is trivial to show, and one uses identity (
76
) to show another identity of its curvature,

\displaystyle R_{\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}.

Combining both identities for
R_{\widetilde{\nabla}^{\sigma}}
we conclude with a proof of Eq. (
77
).
∎

We also get a generalization of statements like
[
17
, §5.5, Lemma 5.5.5, page 276]
:

Lemma 6.79:
Lie bracket of horizontal and vertical vector

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then we have

\displaystyle A\left(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\left(X\right)\circ r_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\Bigr)

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and horizontal vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
. If there is an
\omega\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
such that
\mathrm{D}\pi(X)=\pi^{*}\omega
, then

\displaystyle A\bigl(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X}(\pi^{*}\nu)=\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\omega}\nu\right).

If
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection, then we can conclude for all horizontal vector fields
X
that

\displaystyle A\bigl(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X}(\pi^{*}\nu).

Remarks 6.80
.

The last statement got also stated in
[
13
, Eq. (2.8)]
in the case of the LGB as principal bundle; we were unaware of this proof, which is why our proof is a bit different.

Proof of Lemma
6.4.1
.

First of all recall the very basic fact (see
e.g.

[
17
, §A.1, Thm. A.1.46, page 615]
) that for two vector fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
we have
16
16

16

Rigorously, similarly to Prop.
6.2.2
one has a projection involved, but as explained in Remark
6.32
we will omit notating this projection; in the context of this proof we can allow ourselves to take the typical ”less rigorous” approach for simplicity.

\displaystyle\left[X,Y\right]_{p}

\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{D}_{\phi_{t}(p)}\phi_{-t}\left(X_{\phi_{t}(p)}\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\phi_{t}
is the local flow of
Y
(with parameter
t
in an open interval of
\mathbb{R}
containing 0). Now let
X
be a horizontal vector field and
Y=\widetilde{\nu}
. For such a
Y
we know by Def.
5.2
that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}\left(\nu_{x}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}\right)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\Phi_{p}
is the orbit map through
p
,
x\coloneqq\pi(p)
, and
e_{x}
the neutral element of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}=r_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}}(p),

and by rewriting in sense of Def.
6.2.4
we achieve

\displaystyle\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]_{p}

\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}r_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}r_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)-\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)\right|_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)\right|_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}*}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)\right|_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{p}\right).

The first summand,
t\mapsto\mathcal{r}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}*}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)
, is a curve with values in
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
due to the fact that
X
is a horizontal vector field. Thus, the velocity of this curve at
t=0
will also be an element of
\mathrm{H}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, and the first summand is therefore in the kernel of
A
. Similarly,

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\eta(t)\coloneqq\left.\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)\right|_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)=\left.\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)\right|_{x}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\pi\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu_{x}}}\right)\right)

is a curve with values in
\mathcal{g}_{x}
, and therefore its fundamental vector field at
p
is a curve with values in
\mathrm{V}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. Hence, we can calculate in the sense of vector spaces to derive

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\widetilde{\eta(t)}_{p}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}\bigl(\eta(-t)\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}\Phi_{p}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\eta(-t)\right)=\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\eta(-t)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{p}

where we substituted
t\leftrightarrow-t
after the first equality sign. In total we achieve

\displaystyle A_{p}\left(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=A_{p}\left(\left.\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\eta(-t)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits\right|_{p}\right)=\left(p,\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left.\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\right|_{x}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\pi\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\right)\right)\Bigr)\right),

and thus

\displaystyle A\left(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\left(X\right)\circ r_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\Bigr).

If there is an
\omega\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
such that
\mathrm{D}\pi(X)=\pi^{*}\omega
, then

\displaystyle A_{p}\left(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p,\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left.\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\right|_{x}\left(\omega_{x}\right)\Bigr)\right),

and therefore by Def.
6.2.2

\displaystyle A_{p}\left(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p,\left.\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\omega}\nu\right|_{x}\right)=\left.\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\omega}\nu\right)\right|_{p}.

By the definition of pullback connections we could also write

\displaystyle A\bigl(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X}(\pi^{*}\nu).

If
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection, then recall Remark
6.4.1
; this implies for the neutral element section
e
that

\displaystyle\Delta e

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

and, again,
t\mapsto\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)_{x}
and
t\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\pi\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\right)
are curves with values in the vector spaces
\mathrm{T}^{*}_{x}M\otimes\mathcal{g}_{x}
and
\mathrm{T}_{x}M
, respectively. Thus, we can just apply typical Leibniz rules to calculate the derivative of the contraction

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\left.\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\right|_{x}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\pi\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\right)\right)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left.\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu}\right)\right|_{x}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\left(X_{p}\right)\bigr)+{\underbrace{\left.\left(\Delta e\right)\right|_{x}}_{=0}}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\pi\left(X_{p\cdot\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu_{x}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi\left(X_{p}\right)}\nu,

and as before we conclude

\displaystyle A\bigl(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X}(\pi^{*}\nu).

∎

This concludes our discussion about the infinitesimal compatibility condition (
74
) and its integrated version, ”
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}=
Ehresmann connection”’. Via Thm.
6.4.1
and Lemma
6.4.1
we therefore concluded that compatibility condition (
74
) holds if the total Maurer-Cartan form is multiplicative. Let us therefore now turn to compatibility condition (
75
), which we can actually integrate to an equivalent statement.

Theorem 6.81:
Generalized Maurer-Cartan equation

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
with connected fibres,
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
, and
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}\left(M;\mathcal{g}\right)
. Then
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
satisfies the infinitesimal compatibility condition (
75
) w.r.t.
\zeta
if and only if the total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta\right)\right|_{g}

\displaystyle=\left(g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\zeta_{x}\circ\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}},\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)

(78)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
). We then say that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
solves the
generalized Maurer-Cartan equation (w.r.t.
\zeta
)
.

Remarks 6.82
.

The structure on the right hand side is again due to denoting the base point in pullback bundles; one may decide to drop the base point for simplicity in the notation. In that case, since the base point is clear by context, one may also just write

\displaystyle\sAd_{g^{-1}}\circ\left.\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta\right|_{g}\penalty\ \text{ or }\penalty\ \mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\left.\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta\right|_{g}

on the right hand side.

Remark 6.83:
Classical Maurer-Cartan equation recovered

Recall Rem.
6.2.2
. If
\mathcal{G}
is a trivial LGB,
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
its canonical flat connection and
\zeta\equiv 0
, then
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is the canonical flat connection on the trivial LAB
\mathcal{g}
(recall Ex.
6.2.2
) and it clearly satisfies the compatibility conditions in Def.
6.4.1
w.r.t.
\zeta\equiv 0
. Furthermore, the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation then reduces to the classical Maurer-Cartan equation.

Proof of Thm.
6.4.1
.

The idea of the proof is similar to the proof of a statement about when two multiplicative 2-forms
17
17

17

See Rem.
6.4.1
later; multiplicativity is related to closedness w.r.t. a differential. However, we will not need this general notion.
are equal, see
e.g.

[
21
, §3, Cor. 3.4]
. That is, we want to discuss under what conditions

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\ni g

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega_{g}\coloneqq\left.\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta\right)\right|_{g}-\left(g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\zeta_{x}\circ\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}},\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)

is constant along the fibres of
\mathcal{G}
, that is, constant along the flows of vertical tangent vectors, the left-invariant vector fields (recall Cor.
4.3
).

\bullet
First of all observe that
\omega
vanishes if at least one tangent vector is vertical. Let
Y\in\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
(
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
x\in M
), and write
Y=\left.\widetilde{\nu_{x}}\right|_{g}
for a
\nu_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
. Due to
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(Y\right)=0
it is clear that the contraction of
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta
with
Y
is 0. Let
Z\in\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}
for which we write

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\widetilde{\mu_{x}}\right|_{g}+X_{g}

for a
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
and
X_{g}\in\mathrm{H}_{g}\mathcal{G}
;
then by Cor.
6.4.1

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left.\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right|_{g}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y),\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Z)\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\left(g,\left[\nu_{x},\mu_{x}\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}\right).

Extend
Y
and
Z
naturally to vector fields on
\mathcal{G}
(for simplicity also denoted by
Y
and
Z
, respectively) by viewing
\nu_{x}
and
\mu_{x}
as values of sections
\nu
and
\mu
, respectively, of
\mathcal{g}
at
x
, and extending
X_{g}
(locally) to a horizontal vector field
X
; thence,

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{Y_{g}}\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}(Z)\right)-\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{Z_{g}}\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}(Y)\right)-\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}\left([Y,Z]\right)

\displaystyle={\underbrace{\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{\widetilde{\nu}_{g}}\left(\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\mu\right)}_{\mathclap{=\left.\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\left(\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)}\mu\right)\right|_{g}=0}}}-\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{\widetilde{\mu}_{g}+X_{g}}\left(\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}\left([\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}]\right)-\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{g}\left([\widetilde{\nu},X]\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X_{g}}\left(\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right)-\left(g,\left[\nu_{x},\mu_{x}\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}\right)+\left(\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\right)_{X_{g}}\left(\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right)
