\displaystyle=-\left(g,\left[\nu_{x},\mu_{x}\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}\right)

using Rem.
5.2
(bracket of fundamental/left-invariant vector fields is fundamental/left-invariant) and Lemma
6.4.1
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left.\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)\right|_{g}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle=0.

It follows that
\omega
vanishes if contracted with a vertical tangent vector.

\bullet
Therefore we can study
\omega
just w.r.t. tangent vectors complementary to the vertical structure, these do not necessarily need to be elements of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
. For a given
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) define

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\to\Gamma(\mathcal{G}),

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma(t)\coloneqq\sigma\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu},

where
\nu
is any section of
\mathcal{g}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
(local) with
\sigma_{x}=g
; pointwise we denote
\gamma(t)
as a map
M\ni x\mapsto\gamma_{x}(t)
. By Cor.
4.3
the flows of elements of
\mathrm{V}_{g}\mathcal{G}
can be precisely described by curves like
\gamma
, so that the derivative w.r.t.
t
of pullbacks of tensors with
\gamma
characterizes any derivative along a fibre of
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore,
\gamma(t)
is by definition a section of
\mathcal{G}
for all
t
and thence describes an embedding of
M
into
\mathcal{G}
. Thus, the
t
-derivatives of terms like
\gamma(t)^{!}\omega
describe the derivatives of
\omega
along fibres of
\mathcal{G}
contracted with tangent vectors complementary to the vertical structure. Therefore let us calculate
\gamma(t)^{!}\omega
; we have

\displaystyle\gamma(t)^{!}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta

\displaystyle={\underbrace{\bigl(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\gamma(t)\bigr)^{!}}_{\equiv\mathds{1}_{M}^{!}}}\zeta=\zeta

and under the identification
\gamma^{*}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\gamma)^{*}\mathcal{g}=\mathds{1}_{M}^{*}\mathcal{g}\cong\mathcal{g}
we get similarly

\displaystyle\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\left(g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\zeta_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(g)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\right)_{x}

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}_{x}(t)}\circ\zeta_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\left(\gamma_{x}(t)\right)}\circ\mathrm{D}_{x}\bigl(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\gamma(t)\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}_{x}(t)}\circ\zeta_{x}

for all
x\in M
, where
\gamma^{-1}(t)
is the
\mathcal{G}
-inverse of
\gamma(t)
. It is also a straight-forward calculation to show that

\displaystyle\gamma(t)^{!}\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\gamma(t)^{*}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)=\mathrm{d}^{\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ\gamma(t)\right)^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right),

using the definition of pullback vector bundle connections; alternatively see the explicit calulations in
[
5
, Appendix, Prop. A.1, Eq. (A.1)]
or
[
6
, Appendix, Prop. A.1.1, Eq. (A.2)]
, and we have

\displaystyle\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)\right)_{x}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left[\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\gamma_{x}(t)}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\bigl(\gamma(t)\bigr)(X)\right),\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{\gamma_{x}(t)}\left(\mathrm{D}_{x}\bigl(\gamma(t)\bigr)(Y)\right)\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left[\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{x}(X),\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)_{x}(Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)_{x}(X,Y)

for all
x\in M
and
X,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M
. Thus, we have so far

\displaystyle\gamma(t)^{!}\omega

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left[\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\gamma(t)^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta-\mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}(t)}\circ\zeta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\bigl(\Delta\gamma(t)\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\gamma(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\gamma(t)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta-\mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}(t)}\circ\zeta.

(79)

By Prop.
6.4.1
we get

\displaystyle\Delta\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\circ\Delta\sigma+\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{t\nu},

and via Lemma
6.4.1
we can derive

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\circ\Delta\sigma\right)\right)(X,Y)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\left(\Delta\sigma(Y)\right)\bigr)-\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{Y}\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\left(\Delta\sigma(X)\right)\bigr)-\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\bigl(\Delta\sigma([X,Y])\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\Bigl(\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{X}\bigl(\Delta\sigma(Y)\bigr)+\left[\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)(X),\Delta\sigma(Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt-\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}_{Y}\bigl(\Delta\sigma(X)\bigr)-\left[\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)(Y),\Delta\sigma(X)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt-\Delta\sigma([X,Y])\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma\right)(X,Y)+\left[\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)(X),\Delta\sigma(Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\left(\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\right)(Y),\Delta\sigma(X)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\circ\left(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma+\left[\Delta\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)\right)(X,Y)

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, hence,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\gamma(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu

making use of that this calculation is pointwise at
x\in M
just a standard derivative in the vector space
\mathcal{g}_{x}
and that
\Delta e\equiv 0
by Remark
6.4.1
; we also used Prop.
•
‣
1.3
. In a similar fashion,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left[\Delta\gamma(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\gamma(t)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=2\left[\left[\Delta\sigma,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+2\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}},

which we can rewrite by the Jacobi identity

\displaystyle\left[\left[\Delta\sigma,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left[\left[\Delta\sigma(X),\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}},\Delta\sigma(Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\left[\Delta\sigma(Y),\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}},\Delta\sigma(X)\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=\left[\left[\Delta\sigma(X),\Delta\sigma(Y)\right]_{\mathcal{g}},\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}},\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}(X,Y)

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and we also trivially have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}(t)}\circ\zeta

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{e\;\!}^{-t\nu}}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta=\left[\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}.

Collecting everything we derive

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\gamma(t)^{!}\omega

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left[\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}},\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\left[\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu+\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

(80)

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.

\bullet
On the one hand, if
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
satisfies Eq. (
78
), then
\omega\equiv 0
. As previously mentioned, we have
\Delta e\equiv 0
, so that we then get in total for Eq. (
6.4.1
) w.r.t.
\sigma\coloneqq e
that

\displaystyle R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu

\displaystyle=\left[\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
, which is the infinitesimal compatibility condition (
75
).

\bullet
On the other hand, if
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
satisfies the infinitesimal compatibility condition (
75
),
R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu=\left[\zeta,\cdot\right]_{\mathcal{g}}
, then
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connection by Lemma
6.4.1
,
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}=\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
, and we know by Eq. (
77
) (Lemma
6.4.1
) that

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta,\penalty\ \nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=-\left[\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}},

so that Eq. (
6.4.1
) then has the form

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\gamma(t)^{!}\omega

\displaystyle=R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\nu-\left[\zeta,\nu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{CondKruemmungmitBLAB}}}{{=}}0

for all
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.
As aforementioned in the discussion around the definition of
\gamma
, also recall that
\omega
vanishes if contracted with a vertical vector, we can conclude that
\omega
is constant along the fibres of
\mathcal{G}
which are connected, so that this implies

\displaystyle\omega=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\xi

for a
\xi\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
, especially

\displaystyle e^{!}\omega

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\circ e\right)^{!}\xi=\xi.

But we already have calculated this in Eq. (
6.4.1
), set
t=0
and
\sigma\coloneqq e
, then also again by
\Delta e=0
we have

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=e^{!}\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{PullbackofMCGeneralCurvPlusExtra}}}{{=}}\zeta-\zeta=0.

Finally we get

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\xi=0,

which finishes the proof.
∎

Trivially, this theorem is an extension of Eq. (
77
) (Lemma
6.4.1
).

Corollary 6.84:
Pullback of generalized Maurer-Cartan equation

Let
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
,
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{G}
so that the total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
satisfies the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation (
78
) w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}\left(M;\mathcal{g}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

Keeping the same notation as in the proof of Thm.
6.4.1
, we have derived Eq. (
6.4.1
), and due to Thm.
6.4.1
we get
\omega=0
; altogether Eq. (
6.4.1
) at
t=0
gives then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta-\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta.

∎

Thus, we can finally define the connection we need on the LGB.

Definition 6.85:
multiplicative Yang-Mills connection

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a horizontal distribution of
\mathcal{G}
. Then we say that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is a
multiplicative Yang-Mills connection (w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
)
, if it satisfies the
compatibility conditions
:

1.

\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection,

2.

the total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
satisfies the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation (w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
).

By Thm.
6.4.1
we can equivalently define
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
as a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection if

1.

\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is multiplicative,

2.

\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
satisfies the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation (w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
).

We then label
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
as a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection, too.

Remark 6.86:
Multiplicative Yang-Mills connections integrate infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connections

By Lemma
6.4.1
and Thm.
6.4.1
, every multiplicative Yang-Mills connection is also an infinitesimal multiplicative Yang-Mills connections,
i.e.
the infinitesimal compatibility conditions are also satisfied.

Remark 6.87:
Relation to simplicial differential

In fact, Def.
6.4.1
comes from a simplicial differential (w.r.t. points on
\mathcal{G}
) defined in
[
15
, beginning of §1.2]
. We do not have time to introduce it completely, but we give a short sketch about its definitions, also following the style of
[
13
, appendix]
. On degree one the cited differential is a map

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{G};\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G};\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\right),

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\delta\omega,

with

\displaystyle\left(\delta\omega\right)_{(g,q)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sAd_{q^{-1}}\circ\left(\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!}\omega\right)_{(g,q)}+\left(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}\omega\right)_{(g,q)}-\left(\Phi^{!}\omega\right)_{(g,q)}

for all
(g,q)\in\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}
, where
\Phi:\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the
\mathcal{G}
-multiplication, and
\mathrm{pr}_{i}:\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
are the canonical projections onto the
i
-th component (
i\in\{1,2\}
);
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
is canonically extended to
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}
. Recall Def.
6.4.1
and its Rem.
6.74
,
\omega
can be defined to be multiplicative if
\delta\omega=0
,
i.e.

\omega
is closed w.r.t.
\delta
.

On degree 0
\delta
is a map
\Omega^{\bullet}(M;\mathcal{g})\to\Omega^{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{G};\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{g}}\right)
given for
\xi\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;\mathcal{g})
by

\displaystyle\left(\delta\xi\right)_{g}\coloneqq\bigl(g,\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\circ\xi_{x}\circ{\underbrace{\left(\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}},\dotsc,\mathrm{D}_{g}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\right)}_{\text{times the degree of $\xi$}}}\bigr)-\left.\left(\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\xi\right)\right|_{g}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Then observe that we can rewrite the generalized Mauer-Cartan equation to

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\delta\zeta,

where

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}}\in\Omega^{2}\left(\mathcal{G};\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}\right).

It is straight-forward to check that
\delta F_{\mathcal{G}}=0
(see
e.g.

[
12
, §4.6, Thm. 4.27, Eq. (53)]
or
[
13
, §2.5, Prop. 2.22]
) by making use of that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection. In total we concluded that compatibility condition (
74
) is implied, if
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is multiplicative, which also implies that
F_{\mathcal{G}}
is multiplicative and thus
\delta
-closed; additionally with compatibility condition (
75
) we achieve
\delta
-exactness of
F_{\mathcal{G}}
. This is no wonder, the compatibility conditions already tell us by definition that the connection-1-form of
\nabla
is closed w.r.t. the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex, which also implies the same for the curvature, however the curvature has to be exact by (
75
). We depict that as
\delta^{\mathrm{CE}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}=0
and
R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}=\delta^{\mathrm{CE}}\zeta
; a summary is shown in Table
4
.

Table 4:
Infinitesimal compatibility conditions on the LAB
\mathcal{g}

and integrated compatibility conditions on the overlying LGB
\mathcal{G}

\mathcal{g}

\mathcal{G}

Closedness

\delta^{\mathrm{CE}}\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}=0

\left(\Rightarrow\delta^{\mathrm{CE}}R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}=0\right)

\delta\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}=0

\left(\Rightarrow\delta F_{\mathcal{G}}=0\right)

Exactness

R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}=\delta^{\mathrm{CE}}\zeta

F_{\mathcal{G}}=\delta\zeta

To conclude this subsubsection, let us provide canonical examples of multiplicative Yang-Mills connections; we will make use of Thm.
6.4.1
in order to prove that the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation is satisfied.

Example 6.88:
(Pre-)Classical multiplicative Yang-Mills connection

Let us look at a classical principal bundle
P
with trivial LGB
\mathcal{G}=M\times G
,
G
a Lie group; recall Ex.
6.1.2
and
6.2.4
. The canonical flat connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{G}
induces the canonical flat connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
on its LAB
\mathcal{g}=M\times\mathfrak{g}
, recall Ex.
6.2.2
. Thus,

\displaystyle R_{\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}}

\displaystyle=0

and so we can set
\zeta\equiv 0
. By Ex.
6.2.4
(viewing
\mathcal{G}
also as a principal bundle equipped with the same horizontal distribution) we know that
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is an Ehresmann connection, so that its total Maurer-Cartan form is multiplicative.

We therefore call flat
\nabla^{\mathrm{cYM}}\coloneqq\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
a
classical multiplicative Yang-Mills connection
, regardless of whether or not
\mathcal{G}
is trivial.

However, observe that
\zeta
can be centre-valued and non-zero while
\nabla^{\mathcal{G}}
is still flat. In such an occasion we speak of a
pre-classical multiplicative Yang-Mills connection
, denoted by
\nabla^{\mathrm{pYM}}
.

Example 6.89:
Associated connections of inner group bundles are multiplicative Yang-Mills connections

Recall Ex.
6.2.4
, its setup and notation. We have discussed a canonical connection on associated LGBs
\mathcal{H}=P\times_{\psi}H
, called associated connection which is an Ehresmann connection.

As it is well-known, the curvature measures the holonomy, that is, the parallel transport over closed (contractible) curves. Together with Eq. (
75
), the parallel transport over a closed (contractible) curve
\alpha
should act as conjugation with some group element. Let us see whether this holds here; there is a unique
g\in G
for
p\in P
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}^{P}_{\alpha}(p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot g,

and thus for
[p,h]\in\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\bigl([p,h]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left[p\cdot g,h\right]=\left[p,\psi_{g}(h)\right].

Thus, if
\mathcal{H}=c_{G}(P)
, the inner group bundle of Ex.
2.2
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{H}}\bigl([p,h]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left[p,ghg^{-1}\right]=\left[p,g\right]\cdot\left[p,h\right]\cdot\left[p,g^{-1}\right],

the conjugation of
[p,h]
with
[p,g]
in
c_{G}(P)
. It follows that there is a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
such that Eq. (
75
) is satisfied. This also implies that the associated connection of an inner group bundle is a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection. We will not prove this approach, instead the next approach provides an alternative proof with an explicit expression for
\zeta
.

Example 6.90:
multiplicative Yang-Mills connections on inner group bundles: Alternative point of view and explanation

Alternatively one can prove that the total Maurer-Cartan form solves the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation in Ex.
6.4.1
by using the aforementioned relationship of the compatibility conditions with Mackenzie’s study about extending Lie algebroids via LABs; recall Remark
6.68
. The following is an information for the experienced reader: The LAB of
c_{G}(P)
is given by the adjoint bundle
\mathrm{Ad}(P)
; recall Ex.
4.2
.
\mathrm{Ad}(P)
is the kernel of the Atiyah sequence (see
e.g.

[
10
, §3.2, page 90ff.]
), a short exact sequence of Lie algebroids over
M
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ad}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{At}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

where
\mathrm{At}(P)
is the Atiyah bundle of
P
. A connection on
P
in the ”classical” sense (that is, related to a canonical flat connection on
M\times G
in our sense) has a 1:1 correspondence to a splitting
\chi:\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{AT}(P)
to that sequence,
i.e.
a section of the right non-trivial arrow. We can then construct a vector bundle connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
on the adjoint bundle by using the Lie algebroid bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathrm{At}(P)}
on
\mathrm{At}(P)
, that is,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{X}\nu

\displaystyle=\left[\chi(X),\nu\right]_{\mathrm{At}(P)}

for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Ad}(P))
. It follows by straight-forward calculations as in
[
10
, §7.3, Prop. 7.3.2 and Lemma 7.3.3, page 278]
that
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
is indeed a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection with
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathrm{Ad}(P))
for example given by

\displaystyle\zeta(Y,Z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[\chi(Y),\chi(Z)\right]_{\mathrm{At}(P)}-\chi([Y,Z])

for all
Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. In other words,
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
is the
adjoint connection
induced by a connection on
P
(see for example
[
10
, §5.3, especially Prop. 5.3.13, page 199]
). Thence, by the discussion in
[
17
, §5.9, page 289ff.]

\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
corresponds to the natural parallel transport
\mathrm{PT}^{\mathrm{Ad}(P)}
on
\mathrm{Ad}(P)
(omitting the notation of the corresponding curve) given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}^{\mathrm{Ad}(P)}\bigl([p,v]\bigr)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[\mathrm{PT}^{P}(p),v\right]

for all
[p,v]\in\mathrm{Ad}(P)
. This is clearly just the infinitesimal parallel transport inherited by
\mathrm{PT}^{c_{G}(P)}
, so that we can conclude that
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
corresponds to the multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
c_{G}(P)
in sense of Def.
6.2.2
(also recall Rem.
6.34
).

This proof gives a certain interpretation of
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
and
\zeta
: In the case of
\mathcal{G}
being the inner group bundle of a typical gauge theory related to a principal
G
-bundle
P
, one can view
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
as the adjoint connection on the adjoint bundle, induced by
\chi
a classical connection on
P
, this implies by Eq. (
75
) that
\zeta
is the field strength related to
\chi
(as a field strength with values in the adjoint bundle). Thus one may say in this case that the generalised sense of connection as in Def.
6.2.4
is modified by a classical connection from a classical principal bundle, thence also the label as multiplicative Yang-Mills connection to reflect its relation to the classical theory.

6.4.2
Field strength related to multiplicative Yang-Mills connections

Let us now introduce and discuss the field strength.

Definition 6.91:
(Generalized) Field strength

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
) and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then we define the
(generalized) curvature
or
(generalized) field strength
F
(of
A
)
as an element of
\Omega^{2}\left(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}\right)
by

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ\left(\pi^{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}},\pi^{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}}\right)+\pi^{!}\zeta,

where
\pi^{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
is the canonical projection onto the associated Ehresmann connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{P}
; that is,

\displaystyle F(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\left(\pi^{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}}(X),\pi^{\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}}(Y)\right)+\left(\pi^{*}\zeta\right)\bigl(\mathrm{D}\pi(X),\mathrm{D}\pi(Y)\bigr)

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
.

Of course, we expect certain properties, naturally generalized by our previous discussions:

Proposition 6.92:
Properties of the generalized field strength

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
) and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then we have the following properties of the field strength:

•

(Form of type
\sAd
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}F

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ F

for all (local)
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.

•

(Horizontal form)

For
X,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
(
p\in\mathcal{P}
) we have

\displaystyle F(X,Y)

\displaystyle=0

if either of
X
and
Y
is vertical.

However, despite the easy proof in the classical theory, the proof of the first property in Prop.
6.4.2
will be rather involved; most of the proof is straight-forward calculation, and then use our discussion about multiplicative Yang-Mills connections; the quintessence will be about using Lemma
6.4.1
(for first order terms w.r.t.
\sigma
) and Cor.
6.4.1
(for second order terms w.r.t.
\sigma
).

Proof of Prop.
6.4.2
.

For this proof we will neglect caring for the base point information in the involved pullback bundles since the base point information is clear by context; recall Subsection
1.3
. In that sense we also have
\sAd_{\sigma}\cong\pi^{*}\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}\cong\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})

etc.
; this will simplify the following notation a bit.

\bullet
The second bullet point quickly follows by construction: The first summand of
F
(recall Def.
6.4.2
) is clearly zero if either of
X
and
Y
is vertical; similar holds for the second summand because the vertical bundle is the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\pi
.

\bullet
Thence, let us focus on proving the first bullet point, and denote with
\pi_{h}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
the canonical projection onto the associated Ehresmann connection
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{P}
, so that we write

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ\left(\pi_{h},\pi_{h}\right)+\pi^{!}\zeta.

We have for
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}\pi^{!}\zeta\right)_{p}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{x}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\pi\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}(X)\right),\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\pi\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}(Y)\right)\bigr),

\displaystyle=\zeta_{x}\bigl({\underbrace{\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\pi\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(X\right)\right)}_{\mathclap{=\mathrm{D}_{p}\left(\pi\circ r_{\sigma}\right)=\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi}}},\mathrm{D}_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\pi\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}r_{\sigma}\left(Y\right)\right)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{!}\zeta\right)_{p}(X,Y)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and
X,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
, making use of that fundamental vector fields are in the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\pi
so that
\mathrm{D}\pi\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma_{x}*}=\mathrm{D}\pi\circ r_{\sigma_{x}*}
. For the first term in the field strength use Cor.
6.2.4
and Remark
6.54
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ\left(\pi_{h},\pi_{h}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\circ\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi_{h}\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*},r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi_{h}\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\circ\pi_{h},\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\circ\pi_{h}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\circ(\pi_{h},\pi_{h}).

Let us denote
X^{h}\coloneqq\pi_{h}(X)
and
Y^{h}\coloneqq\pi_{h}(Y)
, where
X
and
Y
are now elements of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
; since we are looking at tensorial equations we can w.l.o.g. assume that
X^{h}
and
Y^{h}
are the horizontal lifts of vector fields
\omega^{X}
and
\omega^{Y}
, respectively, on
M
, especially
\mathrm{D}\pi\left(X^{h}\right)=\mathrm{D}\pi\left(X\right)=\pi^{*}\omega^{X}
and
\mathrm{D}\pi\left(Y^{h}\right)=\mathrm{D}\pi\left(Y\right)=\pi^{*}\omega^{Y}
.
We also rewrite

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}(X)-r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\in\Gamma\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right)\cong\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P}).

In the following we will make use of the canonical identification
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})\cong\Gamma\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\right)
,
X\mapsto r_{\sigma}^{*}X
, especially in order to calculate Lie brackets.

Then make use of Def.
6.2.4
and Thm.
6.2.4
to show

\displaystyle r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=r_{\sigma}^{*}\biggl(\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right)}{\underbrace{\left((A\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*})\left(Y^{h}\right)\right)}_{=0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(Y^{h}\right)}\left((A\circ\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*})\left(X^{h}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-A\left(\left[\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]\right)\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=r_{\sigma}^{*}\biggl(-A\left(\left[\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]\right)\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=r_{\sigma}^{*}\Biggl(-A\left(\left[\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+A\left(\left[\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+A\left(\left[r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-A\left(\left[r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)\Biggr).

Let us look at each summand individually; for the first summand we use the very well-known fact (see
e.g.

[
17
, §A.1.11, Cor. A.1.51, page 615]
) that

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right),

making use of that
r_{\sigma}
is an isomorphism, and thus we get for the first summand by using Def.
6.2.4

\displaystyle r_{\sigma}^{*}\biggl(-A\left(\left[\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]\right)\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)\right)-\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}A\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)-r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)-r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X^{h},Y^{h}\right)\right)-r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)\right),

where we used a similar argument for the last equality as for the beginning of the calculation for Eq. (
6.4.2
).
We know by Remark
5.2
that the map to fundamental vector fields is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, so that

\displaystyle\left[r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left[\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right),\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]_{\mathcal{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}}}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),

and therefore we can derive for the fourth term in Eq. (
6.4.2
) that

\displaystyle\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(\left[r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left[\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right),\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]_{\mathcal{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}}\right).

For the second and third summands in Eq. (
6.4.2
) we want to use Lemma
6.4.1
and our result of the fourth summand to show

\displaystyle\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(\left[\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(\left[\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\left(r_{\sigma}^{*}A\right)\left(\left[r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right),r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=r_{\sigma}^{*}\Biggl(\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right)}\biggl(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)\biggr)\Biggr)+r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left[\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right),\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)\right]_{\mathcal{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}}\right)

using that
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right)
is horizontal due to the fact that
X^{h}
is horizontal, and, last but not least, we can write for terms like

\displaystyle\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\mathrm{D}\pi\left(Y^{h}\right)\right)=\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\pi^{*}\omega^{Y}\right)=\pi^{*}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{Y}\right)\Bigr),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}\pi\left(\mathrm{D}r_{\sigma}\left(X^{h}\right)-r_{\sigma}^{*}\left({\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(X^{h}\right)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right)\right)=\mathrm{D}{\underbrace{\left(\pi\circ r_{\sigma}\right)}_{=\pi}}\left(X^{h}\right)=\pi^{*}\omega^{X},

using that fundamental vector fields are in the kernel of
\mathrm{D}\pi
,
and therefore

\displaystyle\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right)}\biggl(\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(Y^{h}\right)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{\omega^{X}}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{Y}\right)\Bigr)\right).

Collecting all these results and using Cor.
6.4.1
we can write Eq. (
6.4.2
) as

\displaystyle r_{\sigma}^{*}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(X^{h}\right),\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}\left(Y^{h}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X^{h},Y^{h}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+r_{\sigma}^{*}\Biggl(\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{\omega^{X}}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{Y}\right)\Bigr)\right)-\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{\omega^{Y}}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{X}\right)\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-{\underbrace{\left(\pi^{!}\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\left[X^{h},Y^{h}\right]\right)}_{=\pi^{*}\left(\Delta\sigma\left(\left[\omega^{X},\omega^{Y}\right]\right)\right)}}+\left[\pi^{*}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{X}\right)\Bigr),\pi^{*}\Bigl(\left(\Delta\sigma\right)\left(\omega^{Y}\right)\Bigr)\right]_{\mathcal{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X^{h},Y^{h}\right)\right)+{\underbrace{r_{\sigma}^{*}\pi^{*}}_{=\pi^{*}}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\Delta\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\Delta\sigma\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right)\left(\omega^{X},\omega^{Y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X^{h},Y^{h}\right)\right)+\bigl(\pi^{*}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta-\zeta\right)\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{D}\pi(X),\mathrm{D}\pi(Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\left(\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X^{h},Y^{h}\right)\right)+\left(\left(\pi^{!}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\zeta-\zeta\right)\right)(X,Y)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ(\pi_{h},\pi_{h})+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)-\pi^{!}\zeta\right)(X,Y)

where we used that
\mathrm{D}\pi(X)=\pi^{*}\omega^{X}
and
\mathrm{D}\pi(Y)=\pi^{*}\omega^{Y}
, which immediately implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi\left([X,Y]\right)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\left(\left[\omega^{X},\omega^{Y}\right]\right),

which is a well-known fact, as also given in
[
17
, Proposition A.1.49; page 615]
, but also straight-forward to check.

Finally, we can conclude this proof by combining the previous result with our first calculation of this proof, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{!}F

\displaystyle=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ(\pi_{h},\pi_{h})+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)-\pi^{!}\zeta+\pi^{!}\zeta=\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ F.

∎

We achieve of course also a structure equation.

Theorem 6.93:
Structure equation of the generalized field strength

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
) and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then we have the
structure equation

\displaystyle F=\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A+\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta.

Remark 6.94:
The generalized field strength of the total Maurer-Cartan form

Again viewing
\mathcal{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}}{{\to}}M
as the principal bundle itself with
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
, we know by Cor.
6.4.1
that the total Maurer-Cartan form
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is the connection 1-form corresponding to
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
. Denoting the associated generalized field strength by
F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)
, the structure equation and the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation, Thm.
6.4.1
(also recall Rem.
6.82
), imply

\displaystyle F_{g}\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sAd_{g^{-1}}\circ\left.\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\zeta\right|_{g}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
). This implies that
F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)
is fully encoded by
\zeta
, especially we have

\displaystyle e^{!}\Bigl(F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\zeta,

(89)

where
e
is the neutral section of
\mathcal{G}
. Thus,
\zeta
is the curvature of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
restricted on
M
, and

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A+\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+(e\circ\pi)^{!}\Bigl(F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\Bigr).

Following similar calculations as in the proof of Prop.
6.4.2
, it is trivial to show that the first two summands measure the failure of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
being a foliation, hence this meaning carries over from the classical theory as also argued in
[
13
, §2.5, Cor. 2.23]
for LGBs. Due to the third summand,
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
is involutive if and only if

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=(e\circ\pi)^{!}\Bigl(F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)\Bigr).

In that sense Eq. (
89
) reflects the fact that
M
is a horizontal leave of
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore,
F
can be non-zero while
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
is involutive, if
F\left(\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right)
is non-zero along
M
.

Proof of Thm.
6.4.2
.

The idea of the proof is similar to the proof of the ”classical” statement; following the structure of
[
17
, §5.5, Lemma 5.5.5, page 276]
, see also works like
[
12
, §4.6, Lemma 4.25]
and
[
13
, §2.5, Prop. 2.24]
where something similar is shown on Lie groupoids instead of principal bundles, but with no
\zeta
. That is, we will look at vertical and horizontal tangent vectors and their mixed terms; recall Def.
6.2.4
and Thm.
6.2.4
.

\bullet
We have by Prop.
6.4.2

\displaystyle F\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=0

for all
\mu,\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
. Regarding the right hand side of the structure equation, this trivially also holds for the third summand
\left(\pi^{!}\zeta\right)\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right)=0
. We also have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right),A\left(\widetilde{\mu}\right)\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\left[\pi^{*}\nu,\pi^{*}\mu\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}=\pi^{*}\left(\left[\nu,\mu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle={\underbrace{\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\widetilde{\nu}}\bigl(A\left(\widetilde{\mu}\right)\bigr)}_{\mathclap{=\pi^{*}\left(\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}_{\mathrm{D}\pi\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)}\mu\right)=0}}}-\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{\widetilde{\mu}}\bigl(A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)\bigr)-A{\underbrace{\bigl(\left[\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right]\bigr)}_{=\widetilde{[\nu,\mu]}}}=-\pi^{*}\left(\left[\nu,\mu\right]_{\mathcal{g}}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A+\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=0=F\left(\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}\right).

\bullet
Let
X
and
Y
be horizontal vector fields of
\mathcal{P}
, then clearly

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\bigl[A\left(X\right),A\left(Y\right)\bigr]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}=0,

then clearly

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A+\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\circ\left(\pi_{h},\pi_{h}\right)+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)(X,Y)=F(X,Y),

where
\pi_{h}:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
denotes the canonical projection onto the horizontal bundle
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{P}
(especially,
\pi_{h}(X)=X
,
\pi_{h}(Y)=Y
).

\bullet
Now let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{P})
be again horizontal and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{g})
; then again by Prop.
6.4.2

\displaystyle F\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

as also clearly
\left(\pi^{!}\zeta\right)\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right)=0
,
furthermore

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}\right)\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[A(X),A\left(\widetilde{\nu}\right)\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}=0,

and by Lemma
6.4.1

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A\right)\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}\right)_{X}\left(\pi^{*}\nu\right)-A\bigl(\left[X,\widetilde{\nu}\right]\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{lem:BracketVertHor}}}{{=}}0,

which concludes the proof with

\displaystyle F\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A+\frac{1}{2}\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta\right)\left(X,\widetilde{\nu}\right).

∎

Concluding this subsubsection, we also achieve a generalized Bianchi identity, which we already have proven in
[
5
, §7, Thm. 7.3]
and
[
6
, §5, Thm. 5.1.42]
; the proof is straightforward to show, making use of the infinitesimal compatibility conditions, Def.
6.4.1
.

Theorem 6.95:
Generalized Bianchi identity,
[
6
, §5, Thm. 5.1.42]

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
) and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Then we have the
(generalized) Bianchi identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}F+\left[A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}F\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}

\displaystyle=\pi^{!}\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\zeta.

6.4.3
Gauge transformation of the generalized field strength

Similar to Subsection
6.3
we will now discuss the gauge transformation of the generalized field strength
F
; recall Thm.
6.3
.

Theorem 6.96:
Gauge transformation of the generalized field strength

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
H\in\sAut(\mathcal{P})
. We then have that
H^{!}F
is the field strength related to
H^{!}A
and

\displaystyle H^{!}F

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}}}\circ F,

where
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
is defined as in Prop.
6.3
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component.

Similar to Def. (
58
) we may shortly just write

\displaystyle H^{!}F

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}}}\circ F.

Proof.

That
H^{!}F
is the field strength related to
H^{!}A
follows quickly by the same calculation as for proving Eq. (
6.4.1
), using
\pi\circ H=\pi
,

\displaystyle H^{!}F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{(\pi\circ H)^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\left(H^{!}A\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left[H^{!}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}H^{!}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+(\pi\circ H)^{!}\zeta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\left(H^{!}A\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left[H^{!}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}H^{!}A\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta,

which is the field strength of
H^{!}A
. Now recall Eq. (
6.3
), that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X)+\left.{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\braceld\leaders{\vrule height=0.39998pt,depth=0.0pt}{\hfill}\braceru$\crcr\kern 3.0pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\left(\left(\pi^{*}\Delta\right)\sigma^{H}\right)_{p}(X)}\hfil$\crcr}}}\limits}\right|_{H(p)}

for all
X\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
(
p\in\mathcal{P}
), where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\sigma^{H}_{p}\right)
. Thus, by Prop.
6.4.2
,

\displaystyle\left(H^{!}F\right)_{p}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=F_{H(p)}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p}H(X),\mathrm{D}_{p}H(Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=F_{p\cdot\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}}\bigl(\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(X),\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}*}(Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{r}_{\widetilde{\sigma}}^{!}F\right)_{p}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\left(\sAd_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}^{-1}}\circ F\right)_{p}(X,Y)

for all
X,Y\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
; this finishes the proof.
∎

We will now extend this again to a local change of gauges (sections of
\mathcal{P}
).

Definition 6.97:
Local field strength

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
s\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{U})
be a (local) gauge over an open subset
U\subset M
. Then we define the
local curvature
of
local field strength
F_{s}\in\Omega^{2}\left(U;\left.\mathcal{g}\right|_{U}\right)
(w.r.t.
s
)
by

\displaystyle F_{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq s^{!}F.

In previous calculations for the total Maurer-Cartan form and the generalized Maurer-Cartan equation we have basically already shown the pullback of the structure equation.

Corollary 6.98:
Pullback of the structure equation

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
s\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{U})
be a (local) gauge over an open subset
U\subset M
. Then we can express the local field strength as the pullback of the structure equation, that is,

\displaystyle F_{s}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}A_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\left[A_{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A_{s}\right]_{\mathcal{g}}+\zeta.

Proof.

This follows quickly by the same calculation as for proving Eq. (
6.4.1
).
∎

The gauge transformation of the field strength
F
describes the behaviour of
F
under a change of gauge.

Theorem 6.99:
Gauge transformations again as a change of gauge

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
and
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle, also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Also let
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
be two open subsets of
M
so that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
, two gauges
s_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\right)
and
s_{j}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{j}}\right)
, and the unique
\sigma_{ji}\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
with
s_{i}=s_{j}\cdot\sigma_{ji}
on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
.

Then we have for the fields strength of
A
over
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
that

\displaystyle F_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}\circ F_{s_{j}}.

Proof.

The proof is precisely as for Thm.
6.3
, just without catering for the Darboux derivative in the gauge transformation due to that one uses Thm.
6.4.3
instead of Thm.
6.3
.
∎

Remark 6.100:
Integrating curved Yang-Mills gauge theories, part II

Similar to Rem.
6.3
, Cor.
6.4.3
and Thm.
6.4.3
show that we recover the field strength and its gauge transformation as developed by Alexei Kotov and Thomas Strobl, see
[
1
]
for a concise summary or
[
6
]
for an extended introduction. These references are for the very general situation using Lie algebroids, hence see alternatively
[
5
]
for this type of gauge theory restricted to Lie algebra bundles.

7
Curved Yang-Mills gauge theory

7.1
Definition and gauge invariance

We eventually are able to conclude this paper with the definition of what we will call
curved Yang-Mills gauge theory
, highlighting that this theory is an integral of the infinitesimal gauge theory originally developed by Alexei Kotov and Thomas Strobl. For the following we say that a fibre metric
\kappa
of
\mathcal{g}
is
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant
if

\displaystyle\kappa\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(\nu),\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(\mu)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\kappa(\nu,\mu)

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
(
x\in M
) and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{g}_{x}
.

Corollary 7.1:
Contraction of local field strength with Ad-invariant fibre metric is well-defined

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a spacetime
M
so that its LAB
\mathcal{g}
admits an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant fibre metric
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle; also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let
\left(U_{i}\right)_{i}
be an open covering of
M
so that there are subordinate gauges
s_{i}\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{P}|_{U_{i}}\right)
.

Then the top-degree form
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A]\in\Omega^{\mathrm{dim}(M)}(M;\mathbb{R})
, defined locally by

\displaystyle\left.\bigl(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A]\bigr)\right|_{U_{i}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\kappa\left(F_{s_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}*F_{s_{i}}\right),

is well-defined and independent of the choice of gauge, where
*
is the Hodge star operator w.r.t. the spacetime metric of
M
.

Proof.

Let
s_{j}
be another gauge corresponding to
U_{j}
so that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
, and we have a unique
\sigma_{ji}\in\Gamma\left(\left.\mathcal{G}\right|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}\right)
with
s_{i}=s_{j}\cdot\sigma_{ji}
on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
. Then it follows by Thm.
6.4.3
and the definition of the Hodge star operator (which is just a certain contraction w.r.t.
M
) that

\displaystyle\kappa\left(F_{s_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}*F_{s_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}\circ F_{s_{j}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}*\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma_{ji}^{-1}}\circ F_{s_{j}}\right)\right)=\kappa\left(F_{s_{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}*F_{s_{j}}\right),

using the
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariance of
\kappa
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 7.2:
Curved Yang-Mills gauge theory

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a spacetime
M
so that its LAB
\mathcal{g}
admits an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant fibre metric
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle; furthermore let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
.

Then the functional
A\mapsto\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A]
is called the
curved Yang-Mills Lagrangian
, where
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A]
is defined in Cor.
7.1
.

By construction, this Lagrangian is gauge-invariant.

Theorem 7.3:
Gauge invariance of the curved Yang-Mills Lagrangian

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a spacetime
M
so that its LAB
\mathcal{g}
admits an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant fibre metric
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle; also let
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
.

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}\left[H^{!}A\right]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A]

for all
H\in\sAut(\mathcal{P})
.

Proof.

By Thm.
6.4.3
we have

\displaystyle H^{!}F

\displaystyle={\sAd_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}}}\circ F,

where
\sigma^{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G})^{\mathcal{G}}
is defined as in Prop.
6.3
and
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\pi^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the second component. Similar to the argument in the proof of Thm.
6.3
we have

\displaystyle\left(H^{!}F\right)_{s}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\left(\sigma^{H}\right)^{-1}\circ s}\circ F_{s}

for a given gauge
s\in\Gamma(\mathcal{P}|_{U})
, where
U
is an open subset of
M
. With the same argument as in Cor.
7.1
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}\left[H^{!}A\right]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathrm{CYM}}[A].

∎

Remark 7.4:
Infinitesimal gauge invariance

As we have seen previously, the compatibility conditions (Def.
6.4.1
) were important to derive how
F
behaves under modified right push-forwards. Recall Rem.
6.3
, we have a corresponding formulation of infinitesimal gauge transformation and it is straight-forward to show that we achieve
infinitesimal gauge invariance
as in the classical theory. However, as argued by Alexei Kotov and Thomas Strobl in
[
1
]
(for a concise summary;
[
6
]
for an extended introduction, or alternatively
[
5
]
for a simplified language) the corresponding infinitesimal gauge invariance just needs the infinitesimal compatibility conditions (Def.
6.4.1
).

7.2
Field redefinitions

As developed in
[
5
,
6
, the latter reference developed the following for Lie algebroids and groupoids as structure]
: There is an equivalence relation of curved Yang-Mills gauge theories, preserving the dynamics and kinematics of this theory. Let us recapitulate and integrate these:

Definition 7.5:
Field redefinitions on the LGB

Let
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
with LAB
\mathcal{g}
; furthermore let
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
). We then define
field redefinitions w.r.t.
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
by

\left.\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}\right|_{g}\coloneqq\left.\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right|_{g}+\sAd_{g^{-1}}\circ\left.\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda\right|_{g}-\left.\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda\right|_{g}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}

and of
\zeta
by

\zeta^{\lambda}\coloneqq\zeta+\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\left[\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\lambda\right]_{\mathcal{g}}.

Remarks 7.6
.

Equivalently, since we introduced
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
as a composition of the vertical Maurer-Cartan form with the projection
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}
onto the vertical part w.r.t. the horizontal distribution on
\mathcal{G}
, we can define the field redefinition of
\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}
by

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathrm{vert},\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\pi^{\mathrm{vert}}+\widetilde{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda}^{l}+\widetilde{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda}^{r},

where
\widetilde{\pi_{\mathcal{G}^{!}}\lambda}^{r}
denotes the fundamental vector field of
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda
via right-multiplication, that is,

\displaystyle\left.\widetilde{\pi_{\mathcal{G}^{!}}\lambda}^{r}\right|_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{e_{x}}R_{g}\circ\lambda_{x}\circ\mathrm{D}_{x}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}

for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and
\widetilde{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{!}\lambda}^{l}
is just the typical definition for fundamental vector fields, with an emphasis on that it is via left-multiplication.

Proposition 7.7:
Field redefined multiplicative Yang-Mills connection a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection

Given the context of Def.
7.2
, the field redefinition of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection w.r.t.
\widetilde{\zeta}^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

It is a straight-forward check to see that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
is still multiplicative, and well-known in the mentioned literature regarding multiplicative connections; a quick way to see this is by following Rem.
6.4.1
: We actually add an exact term,

\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\delta\lambda\penalty\ .

Thus,
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
stays closed, and hence it is multiplicative. That it satisfies the generalised Maurer-Cartan equation w.r.t.
\zeta^{\lambda}
is a straight-forward but a bit lengthy calculation, which is why the keep this as an exercise for the reader; for the infinitesimal, i.e. in the connected case by Thm.
6.4.1
, see
[
5
,
6
]
.
∎

In fact, a simple field redefinition of an Ehresmann connection preserves the compatibilities with the new multiplicative connection, and this is precisely one of the ideas of the field redefinitions. As usual, the difference of two connection 1-forms
A-A^{\prime}
should be a one-form which is horizontal and transforms via the Adjoint representations under the modified push-forward. However, we have seen here that the modified push-forward depends on a connection on the group bundle. Thus, is it possible to vary
A
with a 1-form which is horizontal
and
does not transform via the Adjoint transformation such that
A^{\prime}
is a connection 1-form but w.r.t. a different connection on the group bundle? The answer to this are the field redefinitions; for the following observe that the field redefinition of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
induces a field redefinition of
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
given by

\nabla^{\mathrm{YM},\lambda}=\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}+\mathrm{ad}\circ\lambda\penalty\ .

Theorem 7.8:
Field redefinition of Ehresmann connections

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be an LGB over a smooth manifold
M
so that its LAB
\mathcal{g}
admits an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant fibre metric
\kappa
, and let
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}M
be a principal
\mathcal{G}
-bundle; furthermore let
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
(w.r.t. a
\zeta\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathcal{g})
) and
A\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P};\pi^{*}\mathcal{g})
be a connection 1-form on
\mathcal{P}
w.r.t.
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
. We define the
field redefinitions w.r.t.
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
of
A
by

A^{\lambda}\coloneqq A-\pi^{!}\lambda\penalty\ .

Then
A^{\lambda}
is a connection 1-form w.r.t.
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
and

\displaystyle F^{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{d}^{\pi^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM},\lambda}}A^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}\left[A^{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}A^{\lambda}\right]_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{g}}+\pi^{!}\zeta^{\lambda}\equiv F

for all
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

That
F^{\lambda}=F
follows by similar calculations as in
[
5
,
6
]
. Therefore let us only prove that
A^{\lambda}
is still a connection 1-form.
A^{\lambda}
is clearly still the identity on the vertical structure, and henceforth let us check its behaviour w.r.t. the modified pushforward induced by
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
, denoted by
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{\lambda}
for
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
: We have to study

\displaystyle\left(\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{\lambda}\right)^{!}A^{\lambda}\right)_{p}(Y)

for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{x}
and
Y\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathcal{P}
. First of all, similar to the beginning of the proof of Prop.
6.4.2
we have

\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{\lambda}\right)^{!}\left(\pi^{!}\lambda\right)=\pi^{!}\lambda\penalty\ ,

and thus we essentially only need to study the field redefined Darboux derivative:

\Delta^{\lambda}\sigma\coloneqq\sigma^{!}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}=\Delta\sigma+\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\lambda-\lambda\penalty\ .

In total,

\displaystyle\left(\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}^{\lambda}\right)^{!}A^{\lambda}\right)_{p}(Y)

\displaystyle=A_{p\cdot\sigma_{x}}\left(\mathcal{r}_{\sigma*}(Y)\right)-\left(\pi^{!}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\lambda\right)\right)_{p}(Y)+\lambda_{x}(X)-\lambda_{x}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A-\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ\pi^{!}\lambda\right)_{p}(Y)

\displaystyle=\left(\sAd_{\sigma^{-1}}\circ A^{\lambda}\right)_{p}(Y)\penalty\ ,

where
X\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{p}\pi(Y)
,
\mathcal{r}_{\sigma}
denotes the modified pushforward via
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
as usual, and we made use of
A
being a connection 1-form w.r.t.
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
.
∎

We see there is a transformation keeping the Lagrangian invariant such that the dynamics are preserved;
\mathcal{P}
itself does not change and thus the gauge transformations in form of (local) principal bundle automorphisms also stay the same, and so the kinematics are preserved, too. However, field redefinitions non-trivially transform data like
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}
so that it may become flat, and we may end up at a pre-classical theory describing the same physics.
\zeta
itself might survive as shown in
[
5
,
6
]
. Let us summarize these results from the point of view as in Rem.
6.4.1
.

Remark 7.9:
Meaning of Field redefinitions

Recall Rem.
6.4.1
and its notation,
i.e.
there is a simplicial differential
\delta
so that

\displaystyle\delta\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\delta\zeta,

where
F_{\mathcal{G}}
denotes the curvature of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
in a classical approach, that is,

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\pi_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\mathcal{g}},

where
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
is the projection of
\mathcal{G}
.
The field redefinition of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}+\delta\lambda\penalty\ .

Prop.
7.2
and Thm.
7.2
has now several interpretations and implications, where we denote with
\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\delta}
the equivalence class of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
inherited by
\delta
:

1.

The field redefinition of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
is about picking any other representative for
\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\delta}
.

2.

Every other representative of
\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\delta}
is not just multiplicative, but also a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection, that is,

\displaystyle\delta\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle F_{\mathcal{G}}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\delta\zeta^{\lambda},

where
F_{\mathcal{G}}^{\lambda}
is defined like
F_{\mathcal{G}}
but using
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
instead of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
.

3.

Regarding the choice of
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
, the Lagrangian only depends on the choice of class
\left[\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}\right]_{\delta}
. However, the explicit choice of
\zeta
may still matter: One could vary
\zeta
with centre-valued forms while keeping
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
fixed, this is in general not totally covered by the field redefinitions and may affect the Lagrangian as discussed in
[
6
]
(or
[
5
]
).

Last but not least, as also mentioned in the introduction, these field redefinitions are not just any transformation preserving the physics. Curved Yang-Mills gauge theory is a reformulation of classical gauge theory in such a way that gauge theory is form-invariant w.r.t. the field redefinitions. This reformulation introduces extra terms,
\zeta
and connection 1-forms of
\nabla
, that is, every classical gauge theory is equivalent to a possibly curved theory with a non-zero
\zeta
contributing to the field strength. From that point of view it is tempting to ask whether there are examples which cannot be flattened by field redefinitions:

7.3
Examples

First examples which cannot be flattened by field redefinitions were found in
[
5
,
6
]
; but many more were found after the upload of this work: It turned out that connections like
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
help classifying singular foliations (
[
8
]
), and in return singular foliation theory provides a full theory of examples and even a classification of such examples as long as the group bundle is semisimple as worked out in
[
7
]
; motivated by Ex.
6.4.1
(recall also
6.4.1
).

Let us first point out the obvious:

Example 7.10:
Classical examples

By Ex.
6.4.1
, we just recover the classical formalism of gauge theory if having a classical principal bundle
P
on which a trivial LGB
\mathcal{G}=M\times G
acts on the right, where
G
is a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
\mathrm{H}\mathcal{G}
is the canonical flat multiplicative Yang-Mills connection,
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}=\nabla^{\mathrm{cYM}}
is the canonical flat connection on
\mathcal{g}=M\times\mathfrak{g}
, and
\zeta\equiv 0
. Then every example known in the classical formalism carries over.

Furthermore, starting with such a classical example, by
[
5
, §6, Cor. 6.2]
we know that if
\mathfrak{g}
has a non-zero centre (and if
M
is at least three-dimensional), then one can always add a centre-valued
\zeta
to this gauge theory, transforming it into a flat pre-classical gauge theory,
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM}}=\nabla^{\mathrm{pYM}}
, with non-trivial
\zeta
. If
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{pYM}}}\zeta\neq 0
, then there is no
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
with
\widetilde{\zeta}^{\lambda}=0
.

This shows that there are examples with non-trivial
\zeta
, stable w.r.t. field redefinitions. However, as shown in
[
5
, §5.2, Thm. 5.16]
(or alternatively
[
6
, §5.1.5, Thm. 5.1.33]
), if
M
is contractible, then there is
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
so that
\nabla^{\mathrm{YM},\lambda}
is flat. Thus, if
M
is contractible, then every curved Yang-Mills Lagrangian is equivalent to a Lagrangian of a pre-classical gauge theory (
\zeta
may be non-vanishing). This may be due to that
\mathcal{G}
is a trivial fibre bundle, and then the LGB action breaks down to Lie group action; recall all our discussions related to classical principal bundles equipped with an LGB action of a trivial LGB as in the previous example.
Hence we conjecture:

Conjecture 7.11:
Trivial LGBs are associated with pre-classical theories

Let
\mathcal{G}\to M
be a trivial LGB over a smooth manifold
M
; furthermore let
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot}}
be a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
\mathcal{G}
. Then there is a
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathcal{g})
so that
\mu_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{tot},\lambda}
is flat.

For semisimple Lie group bundles this turns out to be true, see
[
7
]
. Obviously, non-trivial LGBs are not used in the classical formalism, and by the previous discussion one may ask if there is a (global) example with a curved multiplicative Yang-Mills connection starting with a non-trivial LGB. Let us describe these examples, already presented in
[
6
,
7
]
.

There is a natural class of multiplicative Yang-Mills connections which cannot be flattened via field redefinitions. In order to understand this, we follow similar arguments as in the construction recipe I of
[
8
, see Ex. 1.12]
: Assume an ordinary principal
{H}
-bundle
P
, where
{H}
is a Lie group. Also assume that we have a manifold
{T}
on which
{H}
acts (from the left).
18
18

18

{T}
and its related constructions and assumptions are only needed to provide an extra geometric interpretation as in
5
.
Then we have two associated bundles:

\displaystyle c_{H}(P)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(P\times{H})\Big/{H}\penalty\ ,

(90)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(P\times{T})\Big/{H}\penalty\ .

As discussed, we have a canonical left-action,
c_{H}(P)\curvearrowright\mathcal{T}
, and an Ehresmann connection on
P
(in the usual sense) induces associated connections on
c_{H}(P)
and
\mathcal{T}
. The one on
c_{H}(P)
is a multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
c_{H}(P)
whose primitive is given by the corresponding curvature on
P
, and the one on
\mathcal{T}
is a connection that has been called
compatible Yang–Mills connection

[
8
]
. That is, this is an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{T}
w.r.t. the
c_{H}(P)
-action and the adjustment on
c_{H}(P)
, which satisfies a certain curvature equation.

The following remark provides further motivation for considering Lie group bundles like
c_{H}(P)
beyond the existence of natural multiplicative Yang-Mills connections.

Remarks 7.12
.

For similar reasons as in ordinary gauge theory, it is useful to assume that the structural Lie group bundle has a compact Lie group
{H}
as structural Lie group. It is then natural to study abelian and semi-simple
{H}
separately, ignoring possible mixed terms appearing in the extra terms as a first step. The abelian part requires flatness of the multiplicative Yang-Mills connection, and we are therefore led to focusing on semi-simple situations in order to find curved examples. As proven in
[
10
, Prop. 7.3.6, Cor. 7.3.9 and the comment afterwards, Cor. 8.3.7]
every Lie group bundle with semi-simple structure group is the inner group bundle of some principal
{H}
-bundle, unique up to principal bundle automorphisms.

As a result of the discussion in
[
10
]
we have the following, extending
7.12
:

Lemma 7.13:
Semi-simple Lie group bundles,
[
10
]

If
{H}
is semi-simple, then all multiplicative Yang-Mills connections on
c_{H}(P)
are associated to an Ehresmann connection on
P
, and their primitive is the corresponding curvature on
P
.

Semi-simplicity also implies that the correspondence in
7.3
is one-to-one. Slightly generalized, assuming that
{H}
has a trivial center implies that the associated connection on
c_{H}(P)
is in one-to-one correspondence with the Ehresmann connection on
P
. Thus, let us now assume that
{H}
and hence
c_{H}(P)
have a trivial center; furthermore assume that
{T}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and that
{H}
acts faithfully on
{T}
with 0 as fixed point. Then there is a canonical singular foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathcal{T}
with
M
as a leaf generated by vector fields of the form

\chi_{\mathcal{T}}(X)+\overline{\nu}

(91)

for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
\nu\in\Gamma(\mathrm{ad}(P))
, where
\chi_{\mathcal{T}}
is the projectable horizontal lift of the associated connection on
\mathcal{T}
, and
\nu\mapsto\overline{\nu}
denotes the inherited Lie algebra bundle action. The connection on
\mathcal{T}
is then also what one calls an
\mathcal{F}
-connection.

Because the action is faithful, there is the following one-to-one correspondence between:

•

the multiplicative Yang-Mills connection on
c_{H}(P)
;

•

the Ehresmann connection on
P
;

•

the
\mathcal{F}
-connection on
\mathcal{T}
.

The major result of
[
8
]
implies that (in a formal setting, and in a neighborhood around a leaf) every foliation can be modeled like this; however
P
is then in general infinite-dimensional.
19
19

19

In our context we simply assume a finite-dimensional
P
so that the arguments of
[
8
]
carry over to a smooth setting.
Altogether, we can study multiplicative Yang-Mills connections on such
c_{H}(P)
as structure group bundle by looking at
\mathcal{F}
and
P
. In particular,
c_{H}(P)
admits a flat multiplicative Yang-Mills connection if and only if
\mathcal{F}
admits a flat
\mathcal{F}
