-connection, which is the case if and only if
P
is flat. Furthermore,
[
8
]
provides a clear geometric interpretation of the field redefinitions, extending the discussion of
[
5
]
and
[
6
, §5.1]
.

Table 5:
Comparing the geometry of multiplicative Yang-Mills connections (YM conn.) with the geometry of singular foliations.

Lie group bundle
c_{H}(P)

Singular foliations
\mathcal{F}

principal
{H}
-bundle
P

multiplicative YM conn.
\mu^{\mathrm{tot}}

\mathcal{F}
-connection

Ehresmann connection

c_{H}(P)
admits flat
\mu^{\mathrm{tot}}

\mathcal{F}
is flat

P
is flat

field redefinition of
\mu^{\mathrm{tot}}

change of
\mathcal{F}
-connection

change of Ehresmann connection

Thus, in order to find non-trivial multiplicative Yang-Mills connections which cannot be flattened by field redefinitions one only needs to apply the previous construction on principal bundles
P
which do not admit flat connections; alternatively search for foliations
\mathcal{F}
which do not admit flat
\mathcal{F}
-connections. There is plenty of literature on both cases.

Example 7.14:
Cooking recipe

Using non-flat principal bundles, for example a non-trivial principal bundle
P
over a simply connected base manifold, such as the Hopf fibration
\mathbb{S}^{7}\to\mathbb{S}^{4}
, leads to curved multiplicative Yang-Mills connections on
c_{H}(P)
in the above construction which cannot be flattened by field redefinitions.

What about the full theory? For this we need to require the existence of an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant connection on
c_{H}(P)
which is simply given by assuming that
H
is semisimple what is covered by the previous discussion; recall Rem.
7.12
. Thus, it is only left to find a principal
c_{H}(P)
-bundle
\mathcal{P}
; for this we have several canonical choices:

•

\mathcal{P}=c_{H}(P)
itself;

•

\mathcal{P}=P
via the action of gauge transformations; recall Rem.
3.2
;

•

\mathcal{P}=(P\times P)\Big/H
, the Atiyah groupoid of
P
, where
H
acts diagonally on
P\times P
.

About the last bullet point, for the experienced reader: We have a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{H}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P\times P)\Big/H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)\penalty\ ,}

where
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)
is the pair groupoid of
M
. It is a straightforward task to show that the Atiyah groupoid is a principal
c_{H}(P)
-bundle,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}=(P\times P)\Big/H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{H}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)}}

where
s_{\mathcal{P}}
and
s_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)}
are the source arrows of
(P\times P)\Big/H
and
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{air}(M)
, respectively. Here one needs the notion of LGB-actions along a moment map, here
s_{\mathcal{P}}
, which we did not discuss here, but it is straight-forward to extend the previous constructions to such setups; see
[
7
]
. Observe however, that the base manifold of the Atiyah groupoid as such a principal bundle is the pair groupoid.

Using such principal bundles thence lead to a curved Yang-Mills gauge theory which cannot be described in a classical way.

7.4
Future prospects

As a next step one could now define minimal couplings. Given a vector bundle
V\to M
, equipped with a LGB representation
\rho\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{Aut}(V)
, we should be able to define associated vector bundles
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq(\mathcal{P}\times_{M}V)\Big/\mathcal{G}
, as we already mentioned before. W.r.t. a local gauge
s
of
\mathcal{P}
every section
\Psi\in\Gamma(\mathcal{V})
has locally the form

\displaystyle[s,\psi_{s}],

where
\psi
is a local section of
V
and
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the equivalence class related to the construction in
\mathcal{V}
. Then one can define the
minimal coupling
\nabla^{A}\Psi
locally by

\displaystyle\left[s,\nabla\psi_{s}+\rho_{*}(A_{s})(\psi_{s})\right]

where
\rho_{*}
is the LAB representation inherited by
\rho
, and
\nabla
is a vector bundle connection with

\displaystyle\rho\left(\sigma^{-1}\right)\circ\nabla\circ\rho(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\nabla+\rho_{*}(\Delta\sigma),

\displaystyle R_{\nabla}

\displaystyle=\rho_{*}\circ\zeta

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathcal{G})
.
Observe the similarity with the infinitesimal compatibility conditions Def.
6.4.1
and Lemma
6.4.1
; the first condition should lead to that that
\nabla^{A}\Psi
is well-defined and independent of the choice of
s
. The second condition should imply that the curvature of
\nabla^{A}
is given by the generalised field strength of
A
. After showing this one could try to construct all the other obvious analogues of the classical formalism and discuss their applications. Essentially, one achieves a special type of curved Yang-Mills-Higgs theory as introduced in
[
7
]
.

國家理論科學研究中心), which is why I also want to thank the NCTS.

Appendix A
Double tangent bundle and its canonical flip map

We will follow
[
10
, §9.6, page 363]
. For a smooth manifold
M
we denote the projection of its tangent bundle by
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}:\mathrm{T}M\to M
; similarly we have the projection of the
double tangent bundle
\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}:\mathrm{TT}M\to\mathrm{T}M
, the tangent bundle of
\mathrm{T}M
. However, there is also
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}:\mathrm{TT}M\to\mathrm{T}M
, and in fact there is another vector bundle structure on
\mathrm{TT}M
rendering
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}
a projection; see
e.g.

[
10
, §3.4
et seq.
; page 110ff.]
. Let us give a very rough sketch:

Let
\xi,\eta\in\mathrm{TT}M
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{Y_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\eta)\eqqcolon\omega,

where
X_{0}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi)
and
Y_{0}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\eta)
,
and due to the fact that
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}
is a vector bundle morphism over
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}
we get

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(X_{0})=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(Y_{0}),

where
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\omega)\eqqcolon p
.
Thus, one can take curves
X,Y:I\to\mathrm{T}M
(
I\subset\mathbb{R}
an open interval around 0) with

\displaystyle X(0)

\displaystyle=X_{0},

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}X

\displaystyle=\xi,

\displaystyle Y(0)

\displaystyle=Y_{0},

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}Y

\displaystyle=\eta,

such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y,

because the condition on
\xi
and
\eta
imply on the base paths
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X,\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y:I\to M
that

\displaystyle(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X)(0)

\displaystyle=p=(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y)(0),

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)=\omega=\mathrm{D}_{Y_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\eta)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y\bigr).

Then the addition and scalar multiplication with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
for
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
is defined by

\displaystyle\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\eta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(X+Y),

\displaystyle\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(\lambda X),

where the addition of curves is well-defined because of
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y
which implies
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ(X+Y)=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ Y
; so, one can take the sum of the curves and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\eta)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\underbrace{\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ(X+Y)}_{=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X}\bigr)=\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)=\omega.

The operations of the linear structure in
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
is still denoted in the same manner as usual, and by definition one also gets

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\eta)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi)+\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\eta),

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi)

\displaystyle=\lambda\penalty\ \pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi).

In total, we have a double vector bundle given by the following commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TT}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}

(A.1)

i.e.
each horizontal and vertical line is a vector bundle so that the horizontal and vertical scalar multiplications on
\mathrm{TT}M
commute; see
e.g.

[
22
, §3ff.]
or
[
10
, §9.1, page 340ff.]
for a definition on double vector bundles in general.

Now observe that the flow
X
of
\xi
has values in
\mathrm{T}M
, that is, for all
t\in I
we have a curve
\alpha_{t}:J\to M
(
J
another open interval containing 0),
s\mapsto\alpha_{t}(s)
, so that

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\bigl(X(t)\bigr),

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha_{t}

\displaystyle=X(t),

and the first equation implies

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)=\omega.

So, in total we have for all
\xi\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}M
a smooth map
\alpha:I\times J\to M
,
(t,s)\mapsto\alpha(t,s)=\alpha_{t}(s)
, such that

\displaystyle\alpha(0,0)

\displaystyle=p=\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}\right)(\xi)=\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\right)(\xi),

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha(t,0)

\displaystyle=\omega=\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi),

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha(0,s)

\displaystyle=X_{0}=\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi),

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha

\displaystyle=\xi.

As for tangent vectors, the class
[\alpha]
of all such
\alpha
uniquely describes
\xi
, in fact giving rise to an equivalence relation so that
[\alpha]
is an equivalence class.

In the context of Schwarz’s Theorem one may find it natural to define the
canonical involution (or flip) on
\mathrm{TT}M
as a map
S:\mathrm{TT}M\to\mathrm{TT}M
by

\displaystyle S(\xi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha.

(A.2)

By
[
10
, §9.6, Thm. 9.6.1, page 363; but without proof]
one has that
S
is an isomorphism of double vector bundles with certain ”special” properties; we will not need the general definition of these. What we need of this, is that
S
is a base-preserving vector bundle isomorphism as both maps, from
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
to
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
and vice versa. Let us prove this, by starting with showing the well-definedness of
S
:

W.l.o.g. we can put
J=I
for simplicity. By construction, the equivalence class
[\beta]
of
S(\xi)
is represented by
\beta:I^{2}\to M
given by
\beta(t,s)\coloneqq\alpha(s,t)
with

\displaystyle\beta(0,0)

\displaystyle=p,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\bigl(S(\xi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\beta(t,0)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha(0,t)=\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}(\xi)=X_{0},

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}\bigl(S(\xi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\beta(0,s)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha(s,0)=\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)=\omega,

\displaystyle S(\xi)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\beta.

With this, we can express
S(\xi)
in coordinates: Fix local coordinates
\left(x^{i}\right)_{i}
on
M
, then
\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)_{i}
and
\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)_{i}
are local coordinates on
\mathrm{T}M
. Then by chain rule

\displaystyle\xi\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\xi\left(x^{i}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\right)=\mathrm{d}_{p}x^{i}\bigl(\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\xi)\bigr)=\omega^{i},

and

\displaystyle\xi\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha\right)\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\underbrace{\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\circ\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha\right)}_{=\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha\right)\left(x^{i}\right)}=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i},

thus we have in total

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\omega^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{X_{0}}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{X_{0}}.

(A.3)

Similarly we can proceed with
S(\xi)
and then apply the ”classical” Schwarz’ Theorem on the derivatives of
\alpha^{i}
(recall the properties of
\beta
), so that we get

\displaystyle S(\xi)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\beta^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}.

(A.4)

It is now clear that
S(\xi)
is independent of the choice of
\alpha
and just depends on
[\alpha]
. Furthermore, due to what we have shown for
\beta
, it is also clear that
S
is a base-preserving map either from
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
to
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
or vice versa.
Bijectivity follows by
S\circ S=\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{TT}M}
.

It is only left to show that we have linearity for
S
as a map from
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
to
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
and vice versa. For this we use the derived coordinate expressions. Let
\eta\in\mathrm{TT}M
be defined as before with associated area function
\gamma
such that
\eta=[\gamma]
. Then one can derive by using what we have shown in the discussion about
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
as vector bundle that

\displaystyle\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta

\displaystyle=\omega^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda X_{0}+\kappa Y_{0}}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left(\lambda\alpha^{i}+\kappa\gamma^{i}\right)\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda X_{0}+\kappa Y_{0}}

(A.5)

for all
\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
,
and so

\displaystyle S(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta)

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda X_{0}^{i}+\kappa Y_{0}^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left(\lambda\alpha^{i}+\kappa\gamma^{i}\right)\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\lambda S(\xi)+\kappa S(\eta).

In the same fashion, let
\zeta=[\delta]\in\mathrm{T}_{X_{0}}\mathrm{T}M
with
\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}(\zeta)\eqqcolon\varphi
, then
\lambda\xi+\kappa\zeta
is just the typical sum of tangent vectors and we get

\displaystyle S(\lambda\xi+\kappa\zeta)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda\omega+\kappa\varphi}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left(\lambda\alpha^{i}+\kappa\delta^{i}\right)\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda\omega+\kappa\varphi}

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left(X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\omega}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\left(X_{0}^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\varphi}+\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\delta^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}S(\xi)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}S(\zeta).

This finishes the proof, so we have:

Remark A.1:
The canonical involution/flip map an isomorphism,

[
10
, §9.6, Thm. 9.6.1, page 363; but without proof]

The canonical involution/flip
S
is a base-preserving vector bundle isomorphism from
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
to
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
, and similarly vice versa. We also write
S_{M}\coloneqq S
to give an accentuation on
M
.

This isomorphism is basically now Schwarz’s Theorem:

Remark A.2:
Revisit: Schwarz’s Theorem

By definition we have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\alpha

\displaystyle=S_{M}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha\right)

for all smooth
\alpha:I\times J\to M
, where
I
and
J
are open intervals containing 0. Similarly this extends to smooths maps
F:M\to N
, where
N
is another smooth manifold. So let
\xi=[\alpha]\in\mathrm{TT}M
, and then we calculate for
\mathrm{DD}F:\mathrm{TT}M\to\mathrm{TT}N
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{DD}F(\xi)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}(F\circ\alpha)

\displaystyle=S_{N}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}(F\circ\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{N}\left(\mathrm{DD}F\bigl(S_{M}(\xi)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(S_{N}\circ\mathrm{DD}F\circ S_{M}\right)(\xi).

Since the canonical involutions are clearly self-inverse, we can also write

\displaystyle S_{N}\circ\mathrm{DD}F

\displaystyle=\mathrm{DD}F\circ S_{M}.

For a notation with base points in
M
, consider a special case with
M=M_{1}\times M_{2}
, where
M_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) are smooth manifolds. Thus,
\mathrm{T}M\cong\pi^{*}_{1}\mathrm{T}M_{1}\oplus\pi^{*}_{2}\mathrm{T}M_{2}
, where
\pi_{i}:M_{1}\times M_{2}\to M_{i}
are the projections onto the
i
-th component. Then let
p_{i}\in M_{i}
,
Y_{i}\in\mathrm{T}_{p_{i}}M_{i}
and
\xi\coloneqq\left(Y_{1},Y_{2}\right)
. Denoting with
\gamma_{1}:I\to M_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}:J\to M_{2}
the curves with velocities
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
at 0, respectively, and so
\alpha(t,s)=\left(\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{2}(t)\right)
; then we define
\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}F(Y_{1})
as a map

\displaystyle M_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{T}N,

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left.\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}F(Y_{1})\right|_{p_{2}}\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{(p_{1},p_{2})}F\left(Y_{1},0_{p_{2}}\right),

where
0_{p_{2}}
is the zero tangent vector at
\mathrm{T}_{p_{2}}M_{2}
,
in a similar fashion for
\mathrm{D}_{p_{2}}F(Y_{2})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}F(Y_{1})\bigr)(Y_{2})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm{D}_{\left(p_{1},\gamma_{2}(t)\right)}F\left(Y_{1},0_{\gamma_{2}(t)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\bigl(F\circ\alpha(t,s)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{DD}F(\xi),

similarly

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p_{2}}F(Y_{2})\bigr)(Y_{1})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\mathrm{D}_{\left(\gamma_{1}(s),p_{2}\right)}F\left(0_{\gamma_{1}(s)},Y_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(F\circ\alpha(t,s)\bigr)

\displaystyle=S_{N}\bigl(\mathrm{DD}F(\xi)\bigr),

in total

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p_{2}}F(Y_{2})\bigr)(Y_{1})

\displaystyle=S_{N}\Bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p_{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{D}_{p_{1}}F(Y_{1})\bigr)(Y_{2})\Bigr).

In fact, using the canonical flip/involution, we can construct and state other properties which can be useful for calculations related to second derivatives.

Remark A.3:
Total derivatives of tangent bundle morphisms linear with respect to prolonged vertical structure

Consider a vector bundle morphism
L:\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}N
(over some map
M\to N
), where
N
is another smooth manifold. Then for
\xi,\eta\in\mathrm{TT}M
with their approximating curves
X
and
Y
, respectively, as previously,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}L\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(L(\lambda X+\kappa Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\lambda L(X)+\kappa L(Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}L(\xi)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}L(\eta)

for all
\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence we achieve linearity with respect to both vector bundle structures of
\mathrm{TT}M
. If we denote the base points as before, then this reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\lambda X_{0}+\kappa Y_{0}}L\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{X_{0}}L(\xi)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{Y_{0}}L(\eta).

Remark A.4:
Alignment of both vector bundle structures on
\mathrm{TT}M
on the restricted vertical bundle

By definition, the vertical bundle
\mathrm{VT}M
of
\mathrm{T}M
is a subbundle of
\mathrm{TT}M
. The zero section of
\mathrm{T}M
is a natural embedding of
M
into
\mathrm{T}M
; this embedded submanifold will be denoted by
\widetilde{M}
. Then Diagram (
A.1
) restricts onto
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{VT}M|_{\widetilde{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and
S=S_{M}
does not only restrict onto that,
S
is actually the identity on
\mathrm{VT}M|_{\widetilde{M}}
, as also stated in
[
10
, §9.6, Thm. 9.6.1, page 363; but without proof]
. This follows simply by the coordinate expressions Eq. (
A.3
) and (
A
) (recall the involved notation).
\xi\in\mathrm{VT}M|_{\widetilde{M}}
is in the kernel of both projections,
\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}
, thus
\omega=X_{0}=0\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
, therefore
S(\xi)=\xi
by Eq. (
A.3
) and (
A
). By Remark
A
the addition
\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}
and scalar multiplication
\boldsymbol{\cdot}
then also align with the typical addition
+
and scalar multiplication
\cdot
, respectively, of
\mathrm{TT}M
as tangent bundle.

Motivated by this, we can actually recover something similar for
\mathrm{VT}M
. In this case
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{VT}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}

and, as already mentioned earlier, the vertical bundle of vector bundles is trivially the pullback of the bundle along itself, here
\mathrm{VT}M\cong\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}\mathrm{T}M
. Thus, we have a canonical projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathrm{VT}M\to\mathrm{T}M
onto the second component, and we can write for
\xi=[\alpha]\in\mathrm{V}_{X_{0}}\mathrm{T}M

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{X_{0}}\cong\left(X_{0},\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\alpha^{i}\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\right|_{p}\right)

using the notation of Eq. (
A.3
) (especially
X_{0}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
,
p\in M
). Similarly for
\eta=[\beta]\in\mathrm{V}_{Y_{0}}\mathrm{T}M
(same notation as previously in this appendix,
i.e.

Y_{0}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
). Then by Eq. (
A.5
)

\displaystyle\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta

\displaystyle\cong\left(\lambda X_{0}+\kappa Y_{0},\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\right|_{s=0}\left(\lambda\alpha^{i}+\kappa\beta^{i}\right)\right)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\right|_{p}\right)

for all
\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\xi\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\eta\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}(\xi)+\kappa\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}(\eta).

For
\zeta\in\mathrm{V}_{X_{0}}\mathrm{T}M
we clearly get

\mathrm{pr}_{2}\left(\lambda\xi+\kappa\zeta\right)=\lambda\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}(\xi)+\kappa\penalty\ \mathrm{pr}_{2}(\zeta),

and so both linear structures on
\mathrm{VT}M
align under
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
.

Remark A.5:
Tangent lift,
[
23
, §2.2, last parapgraph in Subsection 2.2]

Let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, then its total derivative is a map
\mathrm{D}X:\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{TT}M
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ\mathrm{D}X

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}\underbrace{\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X\right)}_{=\mathds{1}_{M}}=\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{T}M},

so that
\mathrm{D}X
is a section of
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
. Due to the fact that
S=S_{M}
is a base-preserving vector bundle isomorphism from
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
to
\mathrm{TT}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{T}M
(and vice versa), we have a vector field
X_{T}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{T}M)
given by

\displaystyle X_{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq S\circ\mathrm{D}X,

called the
tangent lift of
X
. We then also have

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}\circ X_{T}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathrm{TT}M}\circ\mathrm{D}X=X,

thus the label as tangent lift. We also have linearity properties (sort of) and a Leibniz rule, that is, we clearly get

\displaystyle X_{T}\left(\lambda Y+\kappa Z\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}X_{T}\left(Y\right)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}X_{T}\left(Z\right).

for all
Y,Z\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
and
\kappa,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, and we also have something similar w.r.t.
X
: Recall Eq. (
A.3
) and (
A
), including their notation w.r.t. to local coordinates
\left(x^{i}\right)_{i}
. That is, by definition of
\mathrm{D}X
we can derive

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}X(Y)

\displaystyle=Y^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{X_{p}}+Y^{j}\left.\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\right|_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{X_{p}},

thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}(\kappa X+\lambda W)(Y)

\displaystyle=Y^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda X_{p}+\kappa W_{p}}+Y^{j}\left.\frac{\partial\left(\lambda X^{i}+\kappa W^{i}\right)}{\partial x^{j}}\right|_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{\lambda X_{p}+\kappa W_{p}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{p}X(Y)\mathbin{\rule[1.30005pt]{6.60004pt}{2.20001pt}\hskip-4.40002pt\rule[-0.80002pt]{2.20001pt}{6.60004pt}\,}\kappa\boldsymbol{\cdot}\mathrm{D}_{p}W(Y)

for all
X,W\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{p}(fX)(Y)

\displaystyle=Y^{i}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{f(p)X_{p}}+f(p)\penalty\ Y^{j}\left.\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\right|_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{f(p)X_{p}}+Y^{j}X^{i}(p)\left.\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}\right|_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{f(p)X_{p}}

(A.6)

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Therefore

\displaystyle(\lambda X+\kappa W)_{T}

\displaystyle=\lambda X_{T}+\kappa W_{T},

and by Eq. (
A
)

\displaystyle(fX)_{T}(Y)

\displaystyle=f(p)\penalty\ X^{i}_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{Y}+f(p)\penalty\ Y^{j}\left.\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\right|_{p}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{Y}+Y(f)\penalty\ X^{i}(p)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right)}\right|_{Y}

so

\displaystyle(fX)_{T}

\displaystyle=f\penalty\ X_{T}+\mathrm{d}f\otimes X^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial(\mathrm{d}x^{i})}.

Similar to Remark
A
, the vertical bundle of
\mathrm{T}M
is canonically isomorphic to
\pi_{\mathrm{T}M}^{*}\mathrm{T}M
, one can think of the second term as
\mathrm{d}f\otimes\pi^{*}_{\mathrm{T}M}X
.
